## New Test Cases Added in v1.225.0 (R342 — August 19 2026, 4h cycle 104)

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-457] **[P1] Chrome 159 Stable Web Bluetooth API Adds `Bluetooth.getDevices()` And `BluetoothDevice.forget()` So Pages Can Reuse Previously Granted Bluetooth Pairings Without Re-Prompting And Voluntarily Revoke Permissions** — Chrome 159 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=159`, chromestatus feature 4797798639730688, Chromium bug https://bugs.chromium.org/p/chromium/issues/detail?id=577953#c13, spec at https://webbluetoothcg.github.io/web-bluetooth/, sample at https://googlechrome.github.io/samples/web-bluetooth/get-devices.html) ships the Web Bluetooth `getDevices()` and `forget()` methods: (a) `navigator.bluetooth.getDevices()` returns a `Promise<sequence<BluetoothDevice>>` that resolves with the list of `BluetoothDevice` objects the current origin has been granted permission to use by the user (no permission prompt is shown); (b) `bluetoothDevice.forget()` returns a `Promise<undefined>` that revokes the user-granted permission for that device. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the page has previously granted permission for at least one Bluetooth device via `navigator.bluetooth.requestDevice()` and a user gesture, assert that `navigator.bluetooth.getDevices()` exists as a function on Chrome 159 stable (controlled fixture verifying `typeof navigator.bluetooth.getDevices === "function"`), `await navigator.bluetooth.getDevices()` returns a `Promise<Array<BluetoothDevice>>` that resolves to an `Array` (controlled fixture verifying the return-type contract — `Array.isArray(devices) === true`), `getDevices()` does NOT show a permission prompt (controlled fixture verifying the no-prompt contract — the call resolves without user interaction), the returned `BluetoothDevice` objects have the `name`, `id`, `gatt`, `watchingAdvertisements` IDL properties (controlled fixture verifying the IDL attribute set per the WebIDL contract), the returned array contains exactly the devices previously granted permission for (controlled fixture verifying the accuracy contract — the array length matches the number of granted devices), `getDevices()` works in a fresh origin with no prior pairings (controlled fixture verifying the empty-case contract — `await navigator.bluetooth.getDevices()` resolves to an empty `Array`, not `null` and not `undefined`), `BluetoothDevice.forget()` exists on each `BluetoothDevice` returned by `getDevices()` (controlled fixture verifying `typeof device.forget === "function"`), `await device.forget()` returns a `Promise<undefined>` (controlled fixture verifying the return-type contract), after `forget()`, the device is no longer returned by subsequent `getDevices()` calls (controlled fixture verifying the revocation semantic), after `forget()`, the device's `gatt` connection is disconnected (controlled fixture verifying the connection-cleanup semantic — `device.gatt.connected === false` post-forget), `forget()` is idempotent (controlled fixture verifying that calling `forget()` twice on the same device does NOT throw on the second call), `Chrome 158` controls (no `getDevices()` / `forget()` — these methods are Chrome 159+) report `typeof navigator.bluetooth.getDevices === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying the legacy behavior — neither method exists), `Safari 26.6` controls (no Web Bluetooth API per https://bugs.webkit.org/show_bug.cgi?id=101034) report `typeof navigator.bluetooth === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap), `Firefox 155` controls (Mozilla's official position is Negative per https://mozilla.github.io/standards-positions/#web-bluetooth) report `typeof navigator.bluetooth === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying Firefox does not implement the Web Bluetooth API), and a controlled fixture verifying that `getDevices()` does NOT return devices that were paired by a different origin (controlled fixture verifying the per-origin permission scope). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=159` mirror, Chromium bug https://bugs.chromium.org/p/chromium/issues/detail?id=577953#c13, Web Bluetooth Community Group spec at https://webbluetoothcg.github.io/web-bluetooth/, sample at https://googlechrome.github.io/samples/web-bluetooth/get-devices.html, Mozilla standards position at https://mozilla.github.io/standards-positions/#web-bluetooth.

- [W-458] **[P1] Chrome 160 Stable `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol()` Static Method Lets Pages Feature-Detect The Digital Credentials API Per-Protocol Before Issuing A Credential Request** — Chrome 160 stable (Aug 2026, via the W3C FedID Digital Credentials API spec at https://w3c-fedid.github.io/digital-credentials/#protocols, Chromium feature 6492906882990080, FedID WG resolution at https://github.com/w3c-fedid/digital-credentials/issues/396, Chromium bug https://crbug.com/465006289) ships the `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol()` static method that lets pages check whether a specific protocol identifier is allowed by the user agent for the Digital Credentials API issuance or presentation flow before calling `navigator.credentials.get({ digital: ... })`. The contract: `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol(protocolIdentifier)` returns a `boolean` — `true` if the protocol is supported by the user agent, `false` otherwise. The `protocolIdentifier` argument is a `DOMString` (e.g. `"org-iso-mdoc"`, `"openid4vp-v1-multisigned"`, etc.). Critically, the method returns `false` (NOT throws `ReferenceError`) for unknown protocol identifiers — the protocol identifier space is open and progressive per the spec. The method requires a `typeof DigitalCredential !== "undefined"` guard before invocation (calling it where `DigitalCredential` is undefined throws `ReferenceError`). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the page is on `https://game.example/`, assert that `typeof DigitalCredential === "function"` on Chrome 160 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `DigitalCredential` constructor is exposed — the Feature Detection gate per the spec), `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("org-iso-mdoc")` returns `true` on Chrome 160 stable (controlled fixture verifying the supported-protocol contract — the `org-iso-mdoc` mDL / mDoc protocol is the canonical supported protocol), `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("openid4vp-v1-multisigned")` returns `true` on Chrome 160 stable (controlled fixture verifying the OpenID4VP protocol support), `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("arbitrary-unsupported-protocol")` returns `false` on Chrome 160 stable (controlled fixture verifying the unknown-protocol fallback — the method does NOT throw `ReferenceError` for unknown protocols; it returns `false` per the spec's progressive-protocol-identifier contract), `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("")` returns `false` on Chrome 160 stable (controlled fixture verifying the empty-string fallback), `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol` is a static method (controlled fixture verifying the static-method contract — `typeof DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol === "function"` and it does not require `new`), the method composes with `Array.prototype.filter` for multi-protocol feature detection (controlled fixture verifying `const supported = ["org-iso-mdoc", "openid4vp-v1-multisigned", "unknown-protocol"].filter(DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol)` returns the subset of supported protocols), the method does NOT show a permission prompt (controlled fixture verifying the synchronous-evaluation contract), `Chrome 159` controls (no `userAgentAllowsProtocol` static method — the Digital Credentials API exists but the static feature-detection helper is Chrome 160+) report `typeof DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying the pre-Chrome-160 behavior), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has shipped a restricted Digital Credentials API supporting only the `org-iso-mdoc` protocol per https://webkit.org/blog/17431/online-identity-verification-with-the-digital-credentials-api) report `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("org-iso-mdoc") === true` and `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol("openid4vp-v1-multisigned") === false` (controlled fixture verifying Safari's narrower protocol support), `Firefox 155` controls (Mozilla's official position is Negative per https://mozilla.github.io/standards-positions/#digital-credentials) report `typeof DigitalCredential === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying Firefox does not implement the Digital Credentials API), and a controlled fixture verifying the progressive-protocol-identifier contract: as new protocols are added to the W3C FedID spec, the return value of `userAgentAllowsProtocol("new-protocol")` may transition from `false` to `true` across user-agent versions without requiring changes to the calling page. Source: W3C FedID Digital Credentials API spec at https://w3c-fedid.github.io/digital-credentials/#protocols, Chromium feature 6492906882990080 at https://chromestatuslite.com/feature/6492906882990080, FedID WG resolution at https://github.com/w3c-fedid/digital-credentials/issues/396, Chromium bug https://crbug.com/465006289, WebKit blog at https://webkit.org/blog/17431/online-identity-verification-with-the-digital-credentials-api, Mozilla standards position at https://mozilla.github.io/standards-positions/#digital-credentials.

- [W-459] **[P2] Chrome 159 Stable User Activation Persistence Across Same-Origin Navigation Lets A Page That Received User Activation Reuse It Within A 5-Second Window So Multi-Step Game Flows Don't Lose Activation Across Page Loads** — Chrome 159 stable (Aug 2026, per Chromium superchargebrowser release schedule, via the Web Platform contract tests at https://wpt.fyi/results/useractivation + the Chrome 159 release notes summary) ships the User Activation Persistence extension that lets a page that received a user activation (e.g. a button click or a keypress) reuse that activation within a 5-second window after same-origin navigation. The motivation is to fix the UX pain point where a user clicks "play" on a game (gets user activation), the game navigates to a settings page (the activation is lost), the settings page cannot call secure-payment-confirmation because it does not have user activation, the user has to click again on the settings page (extra friction). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the page is on `https://game.example/`, assert that `navigator.userActivation` exists on Chrome 159 stable (controlled fixture verifying the baseline contract — `typeof navigator.userActivation === "object"`), `navigator.userActivation.hasBeenActive` is a `boolean` (controlled fixture verifying the type contract), `navigator.userActivation.isActive` is a `boolean` (controlled fixture verifying the type contract), after a button click on `https://game.example/page-a` followed by a same-origin navigation to `https://game.example/page-b`, `navigator.userActivation.hasBeenActive === true` on `page-b` within 5 seconds of the navigation (controlled fixture verifying the same-origin persistence contract), after a button click on `https://game.example/page-a` followed by a cross-origin navigation to `https://other-origin.example/page-b`, `navigator.userActivation.hasBeenActive === false` on `page-b` (controlled fixture verifying the cross-origin-no-persistence contract), after a button click on `https://game.example/page-a` followed by a same-origin navigation that exceeds the 5-second window (e.g. setTimeout 6000ms), `navigator.userActivation.hasBeenActive === false` on `page-b` (controlled fixture verifying the 5-second-window contract), `navigator.userActivation.isActive` is `true` window (controlled fixture verifying the practical-effect contract — `navigator.clipboard.write([...])` succeeds on `page-b`), the activation does NOT persist across `window.close()` + `window.open()` ### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-479] **[P1] Chrome 160 Stable Removes Support For Unspecified Protocols In The Digital Credentials API So Only Normatively-Referenced Exchange Protocols (Per FedID WG Resolution) Can Be Used For Issuance / Presentation** — Chrome 160 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=160`, chromestatus feature 6492906882990080, Chromium bug https://crbug.com/465006289, FedID WG resolution at https://github.com/w3c-fedid/digital-credentials/issues/396, spec at https://w3c-fedid.github.io/digital-credentials/#protocols, deprecation started in Chrome 151 with final removal scheduled for Chrome 160) ships the final removal of unspecified protocol support from the Digital Credentials API. The Digital Credentials API was originally designed as an opaque pipeline for arbitrary exchange protocols; per the FedID WG resolution in November 2025, the spec changed to normatively reference only a specific set of exchange protocols. Chromium removes the "opaque pipeline" surface so that only verified protocols are used, enabling a more robust privacy and security threat model for identity verification. The supported protocols post-removal are: `org-iso-mdoc` (ISO mdoc / mDL mobile driving license), `org-iso-mdoc-multi` (multiple mdocs), `openid4vp-v1-multisigned`, `openid4vp-v1-unsigned`, and other protocols normatively referenced in the W3C FedID spec. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the page is on `https://game.example/` and the Digital Credentials API is available, assert that a `DigitalCredentialGetRequest` with `protocol: "org-iso-mdoc"` is accepted by `navigator.credentials.get({ digital: { requests: [{ protocol: "org-iso-mdoc", data: { ... } }] } })` and the request is fulfilled (controlled fixture verifying the supported-protocol contract), a `DigitalCredentialGetRequest` with `protocol: "openid4vp-v1-multisigned"` is accepted by `navigator.credentials.get({ digital: { requests: [{ protocol: "openid4vp-v1-multisigned", data: { ... } }] } })` (controlled fixture verifying the OpenID4VP protocol support), a `DigitalCredentialGetRequest` with `protocol: "arbitrary-unsupported-protocol"` is REJECTED with `NotSupportedError` (controlled fixture verifying the removal contract — unspecified protocols no longer reach the wallet dispatch layer), a `DigitalCredentialGetRequest` with `protocol: ""` (empty string) is REJECTED with `NotSupportedError` (controlled fixture verifying the empty-string rejection), the Digital Credentials API `navigator.credentials.get({ digital: ... })` call itself still works (controlled fixture verifying the API surface is preserved — only the protocol filtering is tightened), the `DigitalCredential.userAgentAllowsProtocol()` static method (W-458) returns `false` for all unspecified protocols (controlled fixture verifying the coordinated contract), a single-request `DigitalCredentialGetRequest` with multiple `protocol` values via `requests: [{ protocol: "org-iso-mdoc", ... }, { protocol: "arbitrary-unsupported", ... }]` is REJECTED with `NotSupportedError` (controlled fixture verifying the all-or-nothing contract — if any request specifies an unsupported protocol, the entire `credentials.get` call fails), the removal does NOT affect other Credential Management APIs (controlled fixture verifying `navigator.credentials.get({ password: true })` continues to function normally), the removal does NOT affect the `navigator.credentials.create({ digital: ... })` issuance path that uses `DigitalCredentialCreationOptions` with the same protocol-filtering contract (controlled fixture verifying the issuance path applies the same protocol-filtering as the presentation path), `Chrome 159` controls (Chrome 159 still supports arbitrary unspecified protocols — the deprecation was in console-warning state) report that the same unspecified-protocol request SUCCEEDS (controlled fixture verifying the pre-removal behavior), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has shipped a restricted Digital Credentials API supporting only the `org-iso-mdoc` protocol per https://webkit.org/blog/17431/online-identity-verification-with-the-digital-credentials-api) report the same protocol-filtering as Chrome 160 (controlled fixture verifying Safari's parallel-protocol-restriction contract), `Firefox 155` controls (Mozilla's official position is Negative per https://mozilla.github.io/standards-positions/#digital-credentials) report `typeof DigitalCredential === "undefined"` (controlled fixture verifying Firefox does not implement the Digital Credentials API), and a controlled fixture verifying that the removal does NOT require page-side code changes for pages that already used the supported protocols. Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=160` mirror, Chromium feature 6492906882990080, Chromium bug https://crbug.com/465006289, FedID WG resolution at https://github.com/w3c-fedid/digital-credentials/issues/396, W3C FedID Digital Credentials API spec at https://w3c-fedid.github.io/digital-credentials/#protocols.

- [S-480] **[P2] Edge 152+ Stable Unload-Event Default Behavior Deprecation Rolls Out In Phases Across Edge 152 / 153 (60%) → 154 (80%) → 155 (100%) So Enterprise Admins Can Plan The Migration To `pagehide` / `visibilitychange` Lifecycle Events** — Microsoft Edge 152 stable (Aug 6 2026 release, per https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/web-platform/release-notes/152 + Edge beta channel release notes 152.0.4191.10 Aug 6 2026) ships the staged rollout of the `unload` event deprecation timeline as part of the broader web platform cleanup. The rollout plan: (a) **Edge 152 and 153**: `unload` handlers stop firing by default for 60% of page loads unless the site explicitly enables them via Permissions Policy; (b) **Edge 154**: rollout increases to 80% of page loads; (c) **Edge 155**: rollout reaches 100% of page loads. Web developers should migrate to lifecycle events and APIs designed for these scenarios: `pagehide` (more reliable than `unload` for end-of-page signaling), `visibilitychange` (signals when the user switches away from the tab), and `navigator.sendBeacon()` (fire-and-forget async beacon for analytics). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the page is on `https://game.example/` and is registered with the Permissions Policy `unload` allowlist (or not), assert that on Edge 152 + 153 stable, the `unload` event fires for ~40% of page loads (controlled fixture verifying the 60% rollout contract — the controlled fixture navigates 100 times and counts `unload` firings; expected count is 30-50), on Edge 154 stable, the `unload` event fires for ~20% of page loads (controlled fixture verifying the 80% rollout contract — expected count is 10-30), on Edge 155 stable, the `unload` event NEVER fires by default (controlled fixture verifying the 100% rollout contract — expected count is 0-5 with the small variance from sites with Permissions Policy `unload` allowlist), a page with `<meta http-equiv="Permissions-Policy" content="unload=*">` (explicit allowlist) has the `unload` event fire reliably across all phases of the rollout (controlled fixture verifying the Permissions-Policy escape hatch), the `pagehide` event continues to fire reliably on all Edge versions (controlled fixture verifying the recommended-replacement contract), the `visibilitychange` event continues to fire reliably on all Edge versions (controlled fixture verifying the recommended-replacement contract), `navigator.sendBeacon()` continues to work reliably on all Edge versions (controlled fixture verifying the recommended-replacement contract), the rollout does NOT change the BFCache (back/forward cache) eligibility contract (controlled fixture verifying the BFCache contract), the rollout status is queryable via `edge://policy` (controlled fixture verifying the policy-debugging contract), the rollout does NOT affect `beforeunload` (controlled fixture verifying the `beforeunload`-independence contract), the rollout does NOT affect `DOMContentLoaded` / `load` / `readystatechange` (controlled fixture verifying the load-event-independence contract), `Chrome 157` controls (Chrome does not have a similar `unload` event deprecation per https://chromestatuslite.com/?version=157) report the `unload` event continues to fire 100% of page loads (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap — only Edge has the rollout), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox also does not have an `unload` event deprecation) report the same behavior as Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has not rolled one out per the WebKit blog) report the `unload` event continues to fire 100% of page loads (controlled fixture verifying Safari's status), and a controlled fixture verifying the migration path: replace `window.addEventListener('unload', () => { ... })` with `window.addEventListener('pagehide', () => { ... })` for end-of-page signaling, `document.addEventListener('visibilitychange', () => { ... })` for tab-switch detection, and `navigator.sendBeacon('/analytics-endpoint', JSON.stringify(data))` for analytics beacon delivery. Source: Edge 152 web platform release notes at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/web-platform/release-notes/152, Edge beta channel release notes at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-relnote-beta-channel, the Edge `unload` Permissions-Policy documentation at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-policies, the recommended-replacement lifecycle events at https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Window/pagehide_event and https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Document/visibilitychange_event.

## New Test Cases Added in v1.224.0 (R341 — August 18 2026, 4h cycle 103)

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-456] **[P1] Chrome 157 Stable Approximate Geolocation Adds `PositionOptions.accuracyMode` With Values `precise` (Default) And `approximate` So Sites Can Request Approximate-Only Location Data Without Triggering The High-Accuracy Permission Prompt** - Chrome 157 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=157`, feature 5143609755828224, Chromium bug https://issues.chromium.org/u/1/issues/495852854, spec at https://github.com/w3c/geolocation/pull/233) ships the Approximate Geolocation API: an extension to the existing `navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(success, error, options)` / `watchPosition(success, error, options)` methods that adds a new `accuracyMode` field on the `PositionOptions` dictionary. The `accuracyMode` accepts the string values `"precise"` (the default — preserves the existing high-accuracy contract) or `"approximate"` (the new value — requests approximate location only, e.g. city-level accuracy of several km rather than meter-level). The motivation is to let sites (news, weather, store-locator, privacy-conscious apps) request coarse location without triggering the browser's high-accuracy permission prompt that requires the user to consent to precise GPS / WiFi triangulation, reducing the user-friction on the first location request and supporting privacy-by-default. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS where the user agent is Chrome 157 stable and the page is on `https://game.example/`, assert that `navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(success, error, {accuracyMode: "approximate"})` accepts the option without throwing (controlled fixture verifying the option-bag accepts the new field — `TypeError` is NOT thrown on Chrome 157), the success callback receives a `GeolocationPosition` whose `coords.accuracy` is a `DOMHighResTimeStamp` representing meters and is at least 1000 m (1 km) and up to several km (controlled fixture verifying the coarse-accuracy contract — the accuracy value is large, in the range of city-level rather than GPS-level), the success callback receives a `GeolocationPosition` whose `coords.accuracy` is between 1000 m and 50000 m (50 km) on a typical desktop test environment (controlled fixture verifying the expected-accuracy range per the spec's "approximate" semantic), the `accuracyMode: "precise"` (the default) preserves the existing high-accuracy contract (controlled fixture verifying `navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(success, error, {accuracyMode: "precise"}).coords.accuracy` is below 100 m on a typical desktop test environment, matching the pre-existing Geolocation API contract), the `accuracyMode: "approximate"` works with `navigator.geolocation.watchPosition(success, error, {accuracyMode: "approximate"})` (controlled fixture verifying the watchPosition path also accepts the new field and the `GeolocationPosition` reports coarse accuracy on each call), omitting the `accuracyMode` field preserves the legacy behavior (controlled fixture verifying `navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(success, error)` without the field is equivalent to `accuracyMode: "precise"` — backward-compatibility), an invalid `accuracyMode` value (e.g. `{accuracyMode: "medium"}`) is ignored and the field falls back to the default `"precise"` (controlled fixture verifying the spec's unknown-value fallback — only `"precise"` and `"approximate"` are valid, any other value is treated as `"precise"`), `Chrome 156` controls (no `accuracyMode` field on the `PositionOptions` dictionary) report `accuracyMode` is silently dropped and the existing high-accuracy contract applies (controlled fixture verifying the legacy behavior — the new field has no effect because Chrome 156 does not know about it), `Safari 26.6` / `Safari TP 250` controls (Safari WebKit's Geolocation API does not ship the `accuracyMode` field per the W3C PR) report the same legacy behavior as Chrome 156 (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap — Safari does not implement this PR; only Chrome 157 implements it as of 2026-08-18), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Geolocation API does not ship the `accuracyMode` field per the W3C PR) report the same legacy behavior (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), the `accuracyMode` field is also accepted on `GeolocationPositionOptions` (the interface used by `watchPosition`; controlled fixture verifying the field is exposed on the same dictionary regardless of the call site), and a controlled fixture verifying that the Geolocation permission prompt is NOT triggered for `accuracyMode: "approximate"` calls in some User-Agent configurations (the spec leaves the prompt behavior to the UA; the typical implementation is to show a coarser prompt for `approximate` calls — controlled fixture documenting the expected permission-prompt UX). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=157` mirror (Chrome 157 enabled-by-default, "Approximate geolocation" entry), W3C Geolocation API spec PR at https://github.com/w3c/geolocation/pull/233, Chromium bug https://issues.chromium.org/u/1/issues/495852854.

### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-478] **[P1] Chrome 157 Stable Disallows Spaces In Non-file:// URL Hosts Closing The Interop Gap With Safari And Firefox And Aligning With The URL Standard Spec's "Forbidden Host Code Point" Rule** - Chrome 157 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=157`, feature 5083335148437504, spec at https://url.spec.whatwg.org/#forbidden-host-code-point, WPT Interop2024 at https://wpt.fyi/results/websockets?label=master&label=experimental&aligned&view=interop&q=label%3Ainterop-2024-websockets and https://wpt.fyi/results/url?label=master&label=experimental&aligned&view=interop&q=label%3Ainterop-2023-url, GitHub discussion at https://github.com/whatwg/url/issues/599) ships the URL Standard spec-compliance fix that disallows space characters in non-`file://` URL hosts. Per the URL Standard specification at https://url.spec.whatwg.org/#forbidden-host-code-point, the U+0020 SPACE character is a forbidden host code point that cannot appear in URL hosts; previously Chromium's URL parser was lenient and accepted spaces in non-`file://` URL hosts, causing Chromium to fail several tests in the Interop2024 HTTPS URLs for WebSocket and the URL focus areas. The fix preserves the existing `file://` URL behavior because spaces in `file://` hosts are used by Windows file URLs (e.g. `file:///C:/Program Files/MyApp/data.json`) and removing them would break Windows file-URL handling. For a controlled fixture where the page is served over HTTPS and a script constructs URLs via `new URL(input)` and `new URL(input, base)`, assert that `new URL("https://example.com/path")` parses successfully with `url.host === "example.com"` (controlled fixture verifying the baseline case — a normal host without spaces parses correctly on Chrome 157), `new URL("https://example .com/path")` (with a space) throws a `TypeError` on Chrome 157 because the URL is invalid (controlled fixture verifying the new spec-compliance behavior — Chrome 157 rejects the space; pre-Chrome-157 accepted the space and the host became `example .com` which is non-spec), `new URL("https://foo bar/path")` throws `TypeError` on Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying multiple spaces are also rejected), `new URL("https://example.com/path with space")` parses successfully on Chrome 157 because the space is in the path, not the host (controlled fixture verifying the fix is specifically about the HOST component — path spaces are unaffected per the URL Standard which only forbids space in host), `new URL("file:///C:/Program Files/data.json")` (a Windows file URL with a space in the path) parses successfully on Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying the Windows-file-URL compatibility — spaces in `file://` hosts are preserved, only non-file:// hosts are tightened), `new URL("ftp://example .com/path")` throws `TypeError` on Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying the fix applies to ALL non-file:// schemes, not just HTTPS — the spec is scheme-agnostic for forbidden host code points), `new URL("data:text/plain,hello world")` parses successfully (controlled fixture verifying `data:` URLs are not affected — they have no host component), the URL parser normalizes URL strings to remove forbidden host code points (controlled fixture verifying that `new URL("https://example\u0020.com/").href` reports the URL with the space escaped to `%20` or rejected, not the raw space character), the fetch path respects the new parser contract (controlled fixture verifying that `fetch("https://example .com/api")` throws `TypeError` before the request is sent — the URL is rejected at the API boundary per the URL parser contract), the `WebSocket` constructor path respects the new contract (controlled fixture verifying that `new WebSocket("wss://example .com/socket")` throws `SyntaxError` before the connection is attempted — this is the Interop2024 WebSocket URL test case), the `<a href="https://example .com/">` hyperlink in the DOM respects the new contract (controlled fixture verifying the link's `.href` is `https://example%20.com/` with the space percent-encoded, OR the link is invalid and reports an empty `.href`), `Chrome 156` controls (no spec-compliance fix) accept spaces in non-file:// URL hosts (controlled fixture verifying the legacy behavior — `new URL("https://example .com/path")` parses with `url.host === "example .com"` on Chrome 156 baseline; the new Chrome 157 behavior is a break in the spec-compliance direction), `Safari 26.6` / `Safari TP 250` controls (Safari WebKit's URL parser is per-spec) report the same behavior as Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying Safari is the spec-compliant baseline that Chrome 157 catches up to), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko's URL parser is per-spec) report the same behavior as Chrome 157 (controlled fixture verifying Firefox is also already spec-compliant), and a controlled fixture verifying that the URL parser's forbidden-host-code-point contract matches the URL Standard's authoritative list (the spec lists U+0000 NULL, U+0009 TAB, U+000A LF, U+000D CR, U+0020 SPACE, U+0023 #, U+002F /, U+003A :, U+003C <, U+003E >, U+003F ?, U+0040 @, U+005B [, U+005C \, U+005D ], U+005E ^, U+007C | as forbidden host code points — Chrome 157 enforces the entire list, not just U+0020). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=157` mirror (Chrome 157 enabled-by-default, "Disallow spaces in non-file:// URL hosts" entry), URL Standard spec at https://url.spec.whatwg.org/#forbidden-host-code-point, WPT Interop2024 at https://wpt.fyi/results/url, GitHub discussion at https://github.com/whatwg/url/issues/599.

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-278] **[P1] Safari Technology Preview 250 `ruby-overhang` Property Accepts `spaces` Value That Limits Annotation Overhang To Adjacent Spaces And Punctuation With `none` Now An Alias For `spaces`** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317569@main, WebKit bug 181808420, spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-ruby-1/#ruby-overhang) ships the `spaces` value of the CSS `ruby-overhang` property. The `ruby-overhang` property controls whether ruby annotations are allowed to overhang (extend beyond) the bounds of their base character. The property accepts the values `auto` (ruby may overhang adjacent characters, the browser default), `none` (ruby may NOT overhang — annotation is strictly contained within the base character's width), and now `spaces` (the new value, also the new alias for `none` per WebKit's commit 317569@main) that allows ruby to overhang only adjacent spaces and punctuation characters but not letters or digits. The semantic motivation is to allow CJK ruby annotation overhang into inter-character whitespace and adjacent punctuation (which is visually less disruptive) without overhanging into adjacent letters (which would be confusing). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS containing a CJK ruby structure like `<ruby>漢字<rt>かんじ</rt></ruby>` and `<ruby>word<rt>言葉</rt></ruby>` where the first has CJK base characters and the second has Latin base characters, assert that `ruby-overhang: auto` allows ruby to overhang adjacent characters in both cases (controlled fixture verifying the baseline behavior — auto permits overhang), `ruby-overhang: none` does NOT allow overhang in either case (controlled fixture verifying the strict-no-overhang baseline behavior), `ruby-overhang: spaces` (the new TP 250 value) allows overhang into adjacent spaces (controlled fixture verifying a `<ruby>X <rt>annotation</rt></ruby>` where X is followed by a space — the annotation is allowed to overhang into the space), `ruby-overhang: spaces` does NOT allow overhang into adjacent letters (controlled fixture verifying `<ruby>XY<rt>annotation</rt></ruby>` where the second character Y is a letter — the annotation is NOT allowed to overhang into Y), `ruby-overhang: spaces` DOES allow overhang into adjacent punctuation (controlled fixture verifying `<ruby>X,<rt>annotation</rt></ruby>` where , is a punctuation character — the annotation IS allowed to overhang into the comma), `ruby-overhang: none` is now an alias for `ruby-overhang: spaces` (controlled fixture verifying that on Safari TP 250, `getComputedStyle(rubyEl).rubyOverhang === "spaces"` after setting `ruby-overhang: none` — the value is normalized to `spaces` per the spec; the canonical value of the `none` keyword is `spaces`), the inheritance is from the parent (controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(rubyEl).rubyOverhang` reflects the inherited value from `<body style="ruby-overhang: spaces">`), the WPT fixtures at https://wpt.live/css/css-ruby/ruby-overhang-spaces-001.html and similar give controlled-fixture acceptance criteria (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine parity is being tracked in WPT), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome does not yet ship `ruby-overhang: spaces` per the spec; Chrome's `ruby-overhang` baseline is `auto` and `none`) report `getComputedStyle(rubyEl).rubyOverhang === "none"` for the `none` value (no normalization to `spaces` because Chrome does not know about `spaces` — controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko's `ruby-overhang` baseline is `auto` and `none`) report the same as Chrome 156 (controlled fixture verifying Firefox does not ship `spaces` either), the `none` and `spaces` values produce visually identical results even though `none` is now an alias for `spaces` (controlled fixture verifying the aliasing — the visual output is the same; only the computed-value string differs), and a controlled fixture verifying the spec's `auto` semantic (the auto semantic is unchanged: ruby may overhang any adjacent character per the browser-default rules, which on WebKit include CJK characters but not Latin characters). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317569@main, WebKit bug 181808420, W3C CSS Ruby Layout Module Level 1 spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-ruby-1/#ruby-overhang.

- [B-279] **[P1] Safari Technology Preview 250 Adds Support For The `content` Property On The `::marker` Pseudo-Element So Authors Can Customize List Item Bullets / Numbers With Strings, Images, Counters, Or Any Other CSS Content Value** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317570@main, WebKit bug 182750380, spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-pseudo-4/#marker-pseudo) ships support for the CSS `content` property on the `::marker` pseudo-element: the `::marker` pseudo-element represents the automatic marker (bullet or number) generated for `<li>` elements inside `<ul>` / `<ol>`. The `content` property accepts a normal `<content-list>` value (string, image, counter, attr() reference, etc.) and lets authors replace the browser-default marker with arbitrary content. The contract: setting `li::marker { content: "→ "; }` renders `→ ` as the marker instead of the default `•` for unordered lists, and `li::marker { content: counter(list-item) ". "; }` renders the ordinal number followed by `.` for ordered lists. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS with `<ol><li>First</li><li>Second</li><li>Third</li></ol>` where the `<ol>` has `list-style-type: decimal` by default, assert that the first `<li>` displays a `1.` marker (the default), `<style>li::marker { content: "→ "; }</style>` causes the first `<li>` to display `→ ` instead of `1.` (controlled fixture verifying the string-replacement contract — the marker text is replaced with the literal string), `<style>li::marker { content: counter(list-item) ") "; }</style>` causes the first `<li>` to display `1) ` and the second to display `2) ` (controlled fixture verifying the counter-function contract — the counter function produces a number for each `<li>`), `<style>li::marker { content: url("/icon.svg") " "; }</style>` causes the first `<li>` to display the icon as the marker (controlled fixture verifying the image-replacement contract — the URL is loaded and displayed as the marker), `<style>li::marker { content: attr(data-marker); }</style>` with `<li data-marker="★">First</li>` causes the first `<li>` to display `★` as the marker (controlled fixture verifying the attr() function contract — the data-marker attribute value is used as the marker text), the `::marker` pseudo-element does NOT affect the marker for `<li>` elements that have an explicit `display: list-item` (controlled fixture verifying the spec's `list-item` display requirement — only `list-item` display values get markers), the `::marker` pseudo-element is queryable via `querySelector("li::marker")` (controlled fixture verifying the CSSOM access contract — the marker is a real pseudo-element that can be queried), setting `content: none` on `::marker` suppresses the marker (controlled fixture verifying `content: none` — the marker is hidden), the `::marker` `content` works on `<summary>` elements inside `<details>` (controlled fixture verifying the spec's extension to `<summary>` markers), the `::marker` `content` works on `<legend>` elements inside `<fieldset>` (controlled fixture verifying the spec's extension to `<legend>` markers), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome supports `::marker` per spec but the `content` property was historically restricted; Chrome 156+ does support `::marker { content: "..."; }` after the chromestatuslite feature 5178263526834176) report the new `content` value (controlled fixture verifying Chrome also supports the contract as of recent versions — the cross-engine gap is closed), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko's `::marker` supports `content` per spec) report the new `content` value (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's existing support), and a controlled fixture verifying that the `content` property on `::marker` does NOT affect the layout of the `<li>` content (the marker is rendered in the marker box, not the principal box — controlled fixture verifying the layout isolation). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317570@main, WebKit bug 182750380, W3C CSS Pseudo-Elements Module Level 4 spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-pseudo-4/#marker-pseudo.

- [B-280] **[P1] Safari Technology Preview 250 Adds Support For The CSS `white-space-trim` Property That Controls Whitespace Trimming Behavior At Boundaries Of Preserved-Whitespace Contexts** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317573@main, WebKit bug 182751076, spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-text-4/#white-space-trim-property) ships the CSS `white-space-trim` property from CSS Text Level 4: the property controls how whitespace is trimmed at the start and end of preserved-whitespace contexts (i.e. contexts where `white-space: pre`, `pre-wrap`, `pre-line`, `break-spaces`, or any other preserved-whitespace value is in effect). The `white-space-trim` property accepts the values `none` (do not trim — the default, preserves all whitespace including leading/trailing newlines and spaces), `start` (trim leading whitespace at the start of each line — removes the leading whitespace of the first line if it consists entirely of spaces or tabs; does NOT affect the trailing whitespace), `end` (trim trailing whitespace at the end of each line — removes the trailing whitespace of the last line if it consists entirely of spaces or tabs; does NOT affect leading whitespace), and `both` (trim both leading and trailing whitespace — equivalent to `start end`). The motivation is to give authors fine-grained control over whitespace trimming in code blocks, terminal-style displays, and preformatted text where the default `white-space: pre` would preserve leading/trailing newlines that the author may not want. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS containing `<pre id="p" style="white-space: pre; white-space-trim: both;">   \n  Hello World\n   </pre>` where the `<pre>` content starts and ends with whitespace, assert that `getComputedStyle(p).whiteSpaceTrim === "both"` (controlled fixture verifying the computed-value contract — the property resolves to the specified value), the leading whitespace `   ` (3 spaces before the first \n) is trimmed (controlled fixture verifying the leading-trim semantic — the `start` portion of `both` removes the leading whitespace), the trailing whitespace `   ` (3 spaces after the last \n) is trimmed (controlled fixture verifying the trailing-trim semantic — the `end` portion of `both` removes the trailing whitespace), `white-space-trim: start` trims only the leading whitespace (controlled fixture verifying the partial-trim semantic — the leading whitespace is removed but the trailing whitespace is preserved), `white-space-trim: end` trims only the trailing whitespace (controlled fixture verifying the partial-trim semantic — the trailing whitespace is removed but the leading whitespace is preserved), `white-space-trim: none` preserves all leading and trailing whitespace (controlled fixture verifying the no-trim default — the existing `pre` behavior is preserved), the `white-space-trim` property has NO effect when `white-space: normal` (controlled fixture verifying the spec's scope — `white-space-trim` only applies in preserved-whitespace contexts, not in `normal`), the property inherits (controlled fixture setting `white-space-trim: both` on the `<body>` propagates to descendant `<pre>` elements), `@supports (white-space-trim: both)` returns `true` on Safari TP 250 (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` capability gate), the property applies to preserved whitespace within an inline element (controlled fixture verifying `white-space-trim: both` on a `<span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">` element trims the leading/trailing whitespace of the inline content), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome does not yet ship `white-space-trim` per the spec) report the property as the empty string in computed style (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap — Chrome does not implement the property), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko does not yet ship `white-space-trim` per the spec) report the same as Chrome 156 (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), and a controlled fixture verifying that `white-space-trim` does NOT affect whitespace in the middle of preserved-whitespace content (only the boundaries are trimmed — internal whitespace is preserved per the spec). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317573@main, WebKit bug 182751076, W3C CSS Text Module Level 4 spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-text-4/#white-space-trim-property.

- [B-281] **[P1] Safari Technology Preview 250 Adds Support For The CSS `wrap-inside` Property (Now Renamed `text-wrap` In The Current Spec Draft) That Controls Whether Text Wrapping Can Occur Inside A Box** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317574@main, WebKit bug 182751559, spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-text-4/#wrap-inside-property) ships the CSS `wrap-inside` property that controls whether text wrapping can occur inside a box. The property accepts the values `auto` (text may wrap inside the box, the browser default — the existing behavior), `avoid` (text should avoid wrapping inside the box — the browser should attempt to keep the box on a single line / single block, e.g. by increasing the line box size or moving the box to a new line), and `stable` (a new value proposed in the spec for the new `text-wrap` property where wrapping opportunities inside the box are preserved when the box is moved between lines, to prevent content from shifting when the layout changes). The `wrap-inside` property is a legacy name from an earlier spec draft — the current CSS Text Level 4 spec has renamed it to `text-wrap` with the values `wrap` (auto semantic), `nowrap` (no wrapping), and `balance` (balanced multi-line wrapping). Note: the Safari TP 250 implementation uses the legacy `wrap-inside` name from when the spec was named that, but the spec has since been renamed. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS containing `<p id="t" style="width: 100px; wrap-inside: avoid;">This is a long sentence that should not wrap inside this short-width box.</p>` over HTTPS, assert that `getComputedStyle(t).wrapInside === "avoid"` (controlled fixture verifying the computed-value contract — the property resolves to the specified value on Safari TP 250), the `<p>` element does not have its internal text broken across multiple lines (controlled fixture verifying the avoid semantic — the browser attempts to keep the text on a single line; the box may overflow horizontally but the internal text is preserved as a single line), `<p id="t" style="width: 100px; wrap-inside: auto;">` allows the text to wrap normally (controlled fixture verifying the auto semantic — the default `wrap-inside: auto` allows wrapping as needed), the `wrap-inside: avoid` value is a hint, not a strict requirement (controlled fixture verifying the spec's "hint" semantic — when keeping the box on a single line would cause the text to overflow the viewport, the browser MAY wrap anyway; the avoid value is a preference not a guarantee), the `wrap-inside: stable` value is the new spec proposal that prevents content from shifting when the box is moved between lines (controlled fixture verifying the new spec semantic — when the parent layout changes, the box's internal wrapping opportunities are preserved rather than re-wrapping), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome does not yet ship `wrap-inside` per the legacy spec) report the property as the empty string in computed style (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap — Chrome does not implement `wrap-inside`; Chrome does ship `text-wrap` with the new spec name), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko does not ship `wrap-inside` per the legacy spec) report the same as Chrome 156 (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), the WPT fixtures at https://wpt.live/css/css-text/wrap-inside-001.html and similar give controlled-fixture acceptance criteria (controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine parity is being tracked in WPT), and a controlled fixture verifying that the new spec's `text-wrap` property is the future name (the W3C CSS Text Level 4 spec has been updated to use `text-wrap` instead of `wrap-inside`; the legacy `wrap-inside` is the older name and may be deprecated in favor of `text-wrap` in a future spec revision). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317574@main, WebKit bug 182751559, W3C CSS Text Module Level 4 spec at https://drafts.csswg.org/css-text-4/#wrap-inside-property (the spec has since been renamed to `text-wrap` per https://drafts.csswg.org/css-text-4/#text-wrap-property).

### Cross-Engine (C)

- [C-239] **[P1] Safari Technology Preview 250 Adds Support For Copying And Pasting `image/svg+xml` Data Via The Clipboard API With Markup Sanitization On Both Read And Write Sides** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317528@main, WebKit bug 137553836, spec at https://www.w3.org/TR/clipboard-apis/) ships the Clipboard API extension for `image/svg+xml` MIME type: sites can now copy and paste SVG markup via the Async Clipboard API. The write path accepts a `ClipboardItem` with an `image/svg+xml` blob containing serialized SVG markup, and the read path returns SVG markup as a `Blob` of MIME type `image/svg+xml` when the user pastes from a system clipboard that contains SVG. The contract includes automatic markup sanitization on both read and write — WebKit sanitizes the SVG to remove dangerous content (e.g. `<script>` elements, `onload` attributes, external `<use>` references to non-allowed origins) before writing to or reading from the system clipboard, in compliance with the HTML sanitization spec (https://w3c.github.io/html-sanitization/). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS on `https://game.example/` that has user-gesture activation, assert that `navigator.clipboard.write([new ClipboardItem({"image/svg+xml": new Blob(["<svg xmlns=\"http://www.w3.org/2000/svg\"><circle cx=\"50\" cy=\"50\" r=\"40\" fill=\"red\"/></svg>"], {type: "image/svg+xml"})})])` resolves to a `Promise<void>` without throwing (controlled fixture verifying the write path accepts the SVG blob — the user-gesture activation is the only requirement), the write call sanitizes the SVG to remove `<script>` elements (controlled fixture verifying the write-side sanitization — if the input SVG contains `<script>alert(1)</script>`, the sanitized SVG in the system clipboard does NOT contain the script), the write call sanitizes the SVG to remove `onload` / `onclick` / `onerror` event handler attributes (controlled fixture verifying the write-side sanitization — handler attributes are removed per the HTML sanitization spec), the read path returns the sanitized SVG (controlled fixture verifying the read-side sanitization — if the system clipboard contains `<svg><script>alert(1)</script></svg>`, the `await navigator.clipboard.read()` blob contains the sanitized version without the script), the read SVG can be parsed and rendered via `new Blob([svg], {type: 'image/svg+xml'})` + `URL.createObjectURL(blob)` + `<img src="...">` (controlled fixture verifying the round-trip contract — the sanitized SVG is well-formed and renderable), the read SVG does NOT contain external `<use href="https://attacker.example/..."/>` references (controlled fixture verifying the cross-origin sanitization — external use references are removed because they could leak data to attacker.example), the Clipboard API still requires user-gesture activation (controlled fixture verifying the security gate — `navigator.clipboard.write` without a user gesture throws `NotAllowedError`), the SVG sanitization uses a `Document.parseHTMLUnsafe`-based parser per the HTML sanitization spec (controlled fixture verifying the spec-compliance — the parser is the standard HTML sanitization spec parser), the `image/svg+xml` MIME type is exposed in the ClipboardItem's `.types` array (controlled fixture verifying the MIME-type contract — `ClipboardItem.supports("image/svg+xml")` returns `true` on Safari TP 250), the read blob is `Promise<Blob[]>` (an array of blobs; the SVG is in one of the blobs with MIME type `image/svg+xml`; controlled fixture verifying the read API shape), the write blob is `Blob` (a single blob; the SVG is the blob body), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome supports the Clipboard API for text + PNG + JPEG but does NOT yet support `image/svg+xml` per the same spec; controlled fixture verifying the cross-engine gap — Chrome 156's `ClipboardItem.supports("image/svg+xml")` returns `false`), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox supports the Clipboard API for text but does NOT yet support `image/svg+xml` per the same spec; controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), and a controlled fixture verifying that the sanitized SVG retains the safe elements (`<circle>`, `<rect>`, `<path>`, etc. are preserved) — the sanitization is a whitelist of safe elements, not a blacklist of dangerous elements (controlled fixture verifying the spec's whitelist-based sanitization). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317528@main, WebKit bug 137553836, W3C Clipboard API spec at https://www.w3.org/TR/clipboard-apis/, W3C HTML Sanitization spec at https://w3c.github.io/html-sanitization/.

- [C-240] **[P2] Safari Technology Preview 250 Adds WebDriver `consume user activation` Extension Command That Consumes The Current Browsing Context's Transient User Activation And Reports Whether It Was Present** - Safari Technology Preview 250 (released 2026-08-13, via `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317715@main, WebKit bug 102634281, spec at https://w3c.github.io/webdriver-bidi/) ships the WebDriver `consume user activation` extension command: a WebDriver BiDi command that consumes the current browsing context's transient user activation and reports whether user activation was present at the time of the consume. The contract: when the command is invoked, the WebDriver implementation consumes the user activation (so any subsequent `navigator.userActivation` / `navigator.userActivation.isActive` returns `false`), and the response contains a boolean `present` field that was `true` if the user activation was present at the time of the consume and `false` otherwise. This is useful for test automation that needs to verify whether a UI action requires user-gesture activation (e.g. clipboard write, fullscreen request, payment request) and to programmatically simulate the user-activation consumption that would normally happen after a real user interaction. For a controlled fixture over HTTPS where the page is on `https://game.example/` and WebDriver is connected with a `browsingContext.consumeUserActivation` command, assert that the first invocation reports `present: true` after the page has been activated (controlled fixture verifying the user-activation-was-present contract — the response is `{present: true}` because user activation was just granted by the WebDriver session), the second invocation reports `present: false` (controlled fixture verifying the consumption semantic — the first call consumed the activation, so the second call finds no activation), the user activation is consumed for the page's `navigator.userActivation.isActive` property (controlled fixture verifying the script-visible effect — after `browsingContext.consumeUserActivation`, `navigator.userActivation.isActive === false` even though the WebDriver session may have intended to leave it active), the user activation is consumed for permission-gated APIs (controlled fixture verifying that after the consume, `navigator.clipboard.write([...])` throws `NotAllowedError` because user activation is no longer present), the `consume user activation` command is per-browsing-context (controlled fixture verifying that consuming activation in one tab does NOT consume activation in another tab — each browsing context has its own transient user activation), the `consume user activation` command is exposed via the WebDriver BiDi protocol over WebSocket (controlled fixture verifying the protocol contract — the command is sent as a BiDi message with `method: "browsingContext.consumeUserActivation"` and `params: {context: "<context-id>"}`, and the response has `result: {present: <boolean>}`), the command does NOT throw if user activation is not present (controlled fixture verifying the safe-default semantic — the command always succeeds; the `present` field is `false` if no activation is present, the command does NOT raise an error), the command is exposed only when WebDriver is connected (controlled fixture verifying the security gate — `browsingContext.consumeUserActivation` is not exposed in normal browser sessions; it is a WebDriver extension command that requires a WebDriver session), `Chrome 156` controls (Chrome WebDriver supports the same `consumeUserActivation` command per the W3C WebDriver BiDi spec) report the same behavior (controlled fixture verifying Chrome's existing support for the contract), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox WebDriver status on this command is implementation-specific) report `present: true` / `present: false` per the spec (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's status), and a controlled fixture verifying that the WebDriver `consume user activation` command is different from the legacy W3C WebDriver `userActivation` read-only property (the legacy property reads activation but does not consume it; the new BiDi command reads AND consumes in a single call — the spec's "consume" semantic is the new addition). Source: Safari TP 250 release notes at `https://webkit.org/blog/18191/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-250/`, WebKit commit 317715@main, WebKit bug 102634281, W3C WebDriver BiDi spec at https://w3c.github.io/webdriver-bidi/.

## New Test Cases Added in v1.223.0 (R340 — August 17 2026, 4h cycle 102)

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [B-276] **[P1] Chrome 155 Stable CSS `margin-trim` Property Omits Margins Before/After The First/Last Child Of A Block Or Multi-Column Container Giving Authors A More Powerful / Generic / Expressive Alternative To The Legacy Margin-Quirk That Applies To P/H1-H6 Inside BODY And Table-Cell Elements In Quirks Mode** - Chrome 155 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=155`, spec at `https://drafts.csswg.org/css-box-4/#margin-trim`, MDN at `https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/margin-trim`) ships the CSS `margin-trim` property from CSS Box Model Level 4: the property may be used to omit margins before or after the first or last child of a container. The property is supported on regular block containers and multicol containers (per the current spec text; a previous version of the spec also applied it to flex and grid containers, but that has been removed). The values are: `none` (no margin trimming - the default), `block` (trim the top margin of the first block-level child and the bottom margin of the last block-level child), `block-start` (trim only the first child's start-side margin), `block-end` (trim only the last child's end-side margin), and `inline` (trim inline-start and inline-end margins; this is for inline-axis containers but is currently a no-op per the spec note). The property is somewhat similar to the effect caused by the margin quirk that is applied to P/H1-H2 elements inside BODY and table-cell elements in quirks mode, but more powerful (the margin-trim is author-controlled rather than browser-default), generic (works on any block container, not just P/H1-H2), and expressive (the 5 values let the author opt into fine-grained control). For a controlled fixture serving `<div id="parent" style="margin-trim: block;"><p style="margin-top: 20px; margin-bottom: 30px;">First</p><p style="margin-top: 20px; margin-bottom: 30px;">Middle</p><p style="margin-top: 20px; margin-bottom: 30px;">Last</p></div>` over HTTPS where the parent's content is rendered, assert that the `margin-top: 20px` on the FIRST child (`<p>First</p>`) is TRIMMED (controlled fixture verifying the first-child top margin is omitted - the `First` paragraph has no top margin in the layout, the visual top of `First` aligns with the parent's content edge), the `margin-bottom: 30px` on the LAST child (`<p>Last</p>`) is TRIMMED (controlled fixture verifying the last-child bottom margin is omitted - the `Last` paragraph has no bottom margin in the layout, the visual bottom of `Last` aligns with the parent's content edge), the `margin-top: 20px` on the MIDDLE child (`<p>Middle</p>`) is NOT trimmed (controlled fixture verifying the middle-child margin is preserved - the `Middle` paragraph has its full top margin in the layout, the visual top of `Middle` is 20 px below the visual bottom of `First`), the `margin-bottom: 30px` on the FIRST child (`<p>First</p>`) is NOT trimmed (controlled fixture verifying the first-child's end margin is preserved when only the start margin is trimmed), `margin-trim: none` (the default) preserves all margins (controlled fixture verifying the all-margins-preserved contract - the controlled fixture with `margin-trim: none` reports the first-child top margin + last-child bottom margin + middle-child margins all preserved), `margin-trim: block-start` trims only the first-child top margin (controlled fixture verifying the one-side contract - the last-child bottom margin is preserved while the first-child top margin is trimmed), `margin-trim: block-end` trims only the last-child bottom margin (controlled fixture verifying the one-side contract - the first-child top margin is preserved while the last-child bottom margin is trimmed), `@supports (margin-trim: block)` returns `true` on Chrome 155 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate), `Chrome 154` controls (no `margin-trim` property; the legacy default is `none` per the original CSS Box Model Level 3 contract) report `getComputedStyle(parent).marginTrim === "none"` (the property is not present on the Chrome 154 baseline - the property is empty-string, the default value is implicit), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has not shipped `margin-trim` per WebKit implementation status) report the same `none` computed value (controlled fixture verifying the Safari baseline matches Chrome 154 - both engines lack `margin-trim`), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox has shipped `margin-trim` per Mozilla implementation status) report the resolved `block` value (controlled fixture verifying the Firefox parity assertion - this is the interop fix that aligns Blink with Gecko), the property works on multi-column containers (controlled fixture serving `<div id="multicol" style="column-count: 3; column-gap: 20px; margin-trim: block;"><p style="margin-top: 20px; margin-bottom: 30px;">Para 1</p>...<p>Para 7</p></div>` and verifying the first paragraph in each column has its top margin trimmed and the last paragraph in each column has its bottom margin trimmed - the property operates on a per-column basis, not just the overall first/last child), the property does NOT affect margin COLLAPSING (controlled fixture verifying that two adjacent block children still have their margins collapsed when both are middle children - the property only affects first/last child margins, not the general margin-collapse contract), the property applies to the FIRST CHILD only even if that child is a pseudo-element (controlled fixture serving `<div style="margin-trim: block;"><p>First</p></div>` where the `<p>` has a `::before` pseudo-element with `margin-top: 50px` and verifying the `::before` margin is treated as part of the first-child's top margin and is also trimmed - the property is per-element, not per-element-position), the property does NOT apply to flex containers (controlled fixture serving `<div style="display: flex; margin-trim: block;"><div style="margin: 20px;">A</div><div style="margin: 20px;">B</div></div>` and verifying the flex children's margins are NOT trimmed - per the spec note that the previous flex/grid application has been removed; this is the intentional cross-engine convergence), and the property does NOT apply to inline elements (controlled fixture serving `<div style="margin-trim: block;"><span>first</span> middle <span>last</span></div>` and verifying the inline-level margins are NOT trimmed - the property only applies to block-level first/last children per the spec). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=155` mirror (Chrome 155 enabled-by-default, "Margin-trim" entry), CSS Box Model Level 4 spec at `https://drafts.csswg.org/css-box-4/#margin-trim`, MDN at `https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/margin-trim`.

- [B-277] **[P1] Chrome 156 Stable SVG `<foreignObject>` Inside An `<img>` Sourced From A Blob URL Does Not Taint The Canvas So Games Can Use Rich SVG-Embedded-HTML Assets In `drawImage()` Without Losing The Canvas** - Chrome 156 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=156`, feature 5196074156032000, Chromium bug `https://issues.chromium.org/issues/41054640`) ships the SVG `foreignObject` canvas taint fix for blob URLs: the ability to use an `<img>` element with an SVG source in an HTML canvas `drawImage()` operation has long been supported by all browsers, but the canvas tainting behavior varies across platforms. All browsers taint the canvas when the SVG source includes a `<foreignObject>` tag and is referenced via an HTTP URI scheme. When the same SVG is referenced through a `data:` URI all browsers agree not to taint the canvas. However, when a `blob:` URI is used both Chromium (before this change) and WebKit taint the canvas, but Gecko does not. With this Chrome 156 fix Chromium's behavior matches that of Gecko, allowing a wider range of SVG content to be used in canvas `drawImage()` calls without tainting (a critical capability for game engines that compose rich SVG-with-foreignObject assets like embedded-HTML leaderboards, dynamic SVG-based UIs with HTML text labels, or SVG-rendered game tutorials that contain HTML formatting). For a controlled fixture serving `/game-tutorial.svg` over HTTPS with `Content-Type: image/svg+xml` and content `<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="200" height="200"><foreignObject x="0" y="0" width="200" height="200"><div xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml" style="color: red; font: bold 20px sans-serif;"><p>Game Tutorial HTML</p></div></foreignObject></svg>`, then the page converts the SVG to a `blob:` URL via `const blob = new Blob([svgText], {type: 'image/svg+xml'}); const blobUrl = URL.createObjectURL(blob);` and draws it to a canvas with `<canvas id="c" width="200" height="200"></canvas>` plus `const img = new Image(); img.onload = () => { const ctx = document.getElementById('c').getContext('2d'); ctx.drawImage(img, 0, 0); window.__svgDrawComplete = true; window.__svgCanvasTainted = false; try { ctx.getImageData(0, 0, 1, 1); } catch(e) { window.__svgCanvasTainted = true; } }; img.src = blobUrl;`, assert that `window.__svgDrawComplete === true` after the image loads (controlled fixture verifying the `drawImage` operation completes without throwing), the canvas is NOT tainted (controlled fixture verifying `window.__svgCanvasTainted === false` - the `getImageData()` call succeeds without throwing `SecurityError`; this is the GREEN path that the Chrome 156 fix enables for blob URLs), the same fixture using a `data:` URL source (`const dataUrl = 'data:image/svg+xml;base64,' + btoa(svgText);`) also produces a non-tainted canvas (controlled fixture verifying the `data:` URI baseline - both engines agreed on the no-taint behavior for `data:` URIs; the Chrome 156 fix is the parallel behavior for `blob:` URIs), the same fixture using an HTTP URL source (e.g. `<img src="/game-tutorial.svg">` directly) PRODUCES a tainted canvas (controlled fixture verifying the HTTP URI taint baseline - all browsers taint the canvas for HTTP-URI-sourced SVGs with `<foreignObject>`; this is the interop-consistent contract that does NOT change in Chrome 156), the same fixture without the `<foreignObject>` tag in the SVG (a plain `<svg><circle/></svg>`) does NOT taint the canvas for any URI scheme (controlled fixture verifying the no-foreignObject baseline - tainting only happens for SVGs WITH the foreignObject tag; a plain SVG with no foreignObject is always non-tainting), the `URL.revokeObjectURL(blobUrl)` correctly revokes the blob URL (controlled fixture verifying the blob URL lifecycle - after `revokeObjectURL`, attempting to draw the URL produces a `TypeError: Failed to execute 'drawImage'` because the URL is no longer valid; this confirms the test is using a real blob URL not a cached or proxied version), `Chrome 155` controls (pre-Chrome-156 fix; SVG with foreignObject sourced from a blob URL taints the canvas) report `window.__svgCanvasTainted === true` because the `getImageData()` call throws `SecurityError` (controlled fixture verifying the Chrome 155 baseline has the bug the Chrome 156 fix closes), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari WebKit has the same behavior as pre-Chrome-156 - SVG with foreignObject sourced from a blob URL taints the canvas) report `window.__svgCanvasTainted === true` (controlled fixture verifying the Safari baseline still has the bug; the fix is Chrome 156 only, Safari has not yet shipped the parallel change), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko has the no-taint behavior for blob URLs already - this is the cross-engine convergence target) report `window.__svgCanvasTainted === false` (controlled fixture verifying the Firefox parity assertion - the cross-engine convergence aligns Blink with Gecko, and Safari will likely follow in a future WebKit release), the canvas can be used as a `texImage2D()` source for a WebGL texture (controlled fixture verifying `gl.texImage2D(gl.TEXTURE_2D, 0, gl.RGBA, gl.RGBA, gl.UNSIGNED_BYTE, canvas)` does NOT throw `SecurityError` on Chrome 156 - the non-tainted canvas can be uploaded as a WebGL texture, which is the canonical game-engine use case for `drawImage` of an SVG asset), the fix does NOT affect the `crossOrigin` attribute on the `<img>` element (controlled fixture verifying that `<img crossorigin="anonymous" src="/game-tutorial.svg">` still produces a tainted canvas when the server does NOT return a `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header - the `crossOrigin` attribute is orthogonal to the foreignObject taint decision), and a controlled fixture verifying that drawing an SVG with `<foreignObject>` from a `file:` URL taints the canvas (controlled fixture verifying the file: URL taint baseline - file: URLs are not in the same security context as HTTPS, so they are always tainted regardless of foreignObject). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=156` mirror (Chrome 156 enabled-by-default, "SVG foreignObject does not taint the canvas for blob URLs" entry), feature page at `https://chromestatuslite.com/feature/5196074156032000`, Chromium bug `https://issues.chromium.org/issues/41054640`, WHATWG HTML spec at `https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/canvas.html#security-with-canvas-elements` (the canvas taint rules), W3C SVG 2 spec at `https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/embedded.html#ForeignObjectElement` (the `<foreignObject>` element contract).

- [S-477] **[P2] Chrome 156 Stable WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API Lets Applications Opt In To Diagnostic Logging Of WebRTC Activity For Local Debugging Or Out-Of-Band Upload To The Browser Vendor With An ID Similar To A Crash Report ID, Gated Behind The `WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins` Enterprise Policy** - Chrome 156 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=156`, enterprise policy at `https://chromeenterprise.google/policies/#WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins`, WebRTC extensions spec at `https://w3c.github.io/webrtc-extensions/`) ships the WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API that lets an application opt in to diagnostic logging. The logs contain information about the WebRTC activity by the application and are useful for local debugging or to file bug reports. Logs can be optionally uploaded out of band to the browser vendor and can be used for diagnosing bugs. The application gets an ID that can be attached to a bug report, similar to how crash reports get IDs. Diagnostic logs are enabled via the enterprise policy `WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins` per `https://chromeenterprise.google/policies/#WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins`. The API surface is `navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic` (a new sub-namespace under `navigator.mediaDevices`) which exposes `getDiagnostics({audio: true, video: true, datachannel: true})` returning a `Promise<DiagnosticLog>` and `getLocalDiagnostics(id)` returning a `Promise<DiagnosticLog>` for retrieving a previously-issued log by its ID. The `DiagnosticLog` interface exposes a `.id` (string, the bug-report ID), `.createdAt` (Date, when the log was created), `.events` (array of `DiagnosticLogEvent` with `.type`, `.timestamp`, `.details`), and `.upload()` method that returns a `Promise<string>` resolving to a URL where the log can be uploaded to the browser vendor's bug-report ingestion service. For a controlled fixture where a page on `https://game.example/` has been pre-granted diagnostic-logging permission via the `WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins` enterprise policy, assert that `typeof navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic === 'object'` (controlled fixture verifying the API surface is exposed under `navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic`), `navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic.getDiagnostics({audio: true, video: true, datachannel: true})` returns a `Promise<DiagnosticLog>` (controlled fixture verifying the `getDiagnostics` method exists and returns the right type), the returned `DiagnosticLog.id` is a non-empty string (controlled fixture verifying the ID contract - the ID is the bug-report identifier that the developer can attach to a Chromium bug report), the `DiagnosticLog.events` array contains WebRTC events (controlled fixture verifying the event collection - after the page establishes an `RTCPeerConnection` and exchanges a few SDP offers/answers + ICE candidates, the events array contains at least one `icecandidate` event and one `connectionstatechange` event per the WebRTC activity during the session), the `DiagnosticLog.upload()` method returns a `Promise<string>` resolving to a URL (controlled fixture verifying the upload contract - the URL is a `https://` URL pointing to the browser vendor's bug-report ingestion service, e.g. `https://bugs.chromium.org/p/chromium/issues/entry?diaglog=<id>`), the API is REJECTED with `NotAllowedError` on a page NOT in the `WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins` enterprise policy allowlist (controlled fixture verifying the enterprise-policy gate - the API throws `NotAllowedError` from `getDiagnostics()` when the page origin is not in the policy allowlist, per the design where only enterprise-IT-approved origins can collect diagnostic logs for privacy), the API does NOT log without explicit opt-in (controlled fixture verifying the opt-in contract - a page that does NOT call `getDiagnostics()` has NO events logged; the API is opt-in, not opt-out, so the privacy impact is bounded), `Chrome 155` controls (no WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API; the feature is Chrome 156 first) report `typeof navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic === 'undefined'` (the API surface is not exposed on the Chrome 155 baseline), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari WebKit has not shipped the WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API; Safari has WebRTC Stats API but not the diagnostic logging API) report `typeof navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic === 'undefined'` (controlled fixture verifying the Safari baseline lacks the API), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox Gecko has not shipped the WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API; Firefox has WebRTC Stats API but not the diagnostic logging API) report `typeof navigator.mediaDevices.diagnostic === 'undefined'` (controlled fixture verifying the Firefox baseline lacks the API), the `DiagnosticLog.upload()` method requires user consent (controlled fixture verifying the user-consent gate - the upload Promise rejects with `NotAllowedError` if the user has NOT granted permission to upload to the browser vendor; this is a per-operation consent gate, not a per-page consent), the API does NOT log the actual audio/video content (controlled fixture verifying the privacy contract - the events array contains METADATA about WebRTC activity like SDP sizes + ICE candidate counts + connection state transitions, but does NOT contain the actual audio/video frames, the SRTP keys, or the DTLS fingerprints; this is metadata-only logging for bug-report purposes), the API is available in regular browsing mode AND in incognito mode (controlled fixture verifying the incognito availability - the enterprise policy applies to the browser instance not to a specific browsing mode, so diagnostic logging is available in incognito too), and a controlled fixture verifying that the API integrates with the existing `RTCPeerConnection` event flow (a page that has an active `RTCPeerConnection` with a peer-connection state transition like `new -> connecting -> connected -> disconnected -> failed` produces events in the diagnostic log that correspond to these state transitions). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=156` mirror (Chrome 156 enabled-by-default, "WebRTC Diagnostic Logging API" entry), Chrome Enterprise policy list at `https://chromeenterprise.google/policies/#WebRtcDiagnosticLogCollectionAllowedForOrigins` (the enterprise policy gate), W3C WebRTC extensions spec at `https://w3c.github.io/webrtc-extensions/` (the API surface contract).
