(controlled fixture verifying the `.message` of the rejection `DOMException` contains the literal `user-cancelled`), the same is true for the readable stream path: `await response.body.getReader().read()` rejects with the abort reason (controlled fixture verifying the read promise rejects with the same `DOMException`), the same is true for `response.text()` and `response.json()` (controlled fixture verifying both body methods propagate the abort reason), the abort reason is preserved as the `name` field of the rejection when the reason is an `Error` instance (controlled fixture verifying `controller.abort(new Error("custom"))` results in a `DOMException` whose `.cause` is the custom `Error` instance — the Fetch spec aligns with the DOM `AbortController.abort(reason)` extension), `Chrome 152` controls (Fetch abort-reason-forwarding not yet shipped) reject with generic `AbortError` whose `.message` is `"signal is aborted without reason"` (controlled fixture verifying the message text differs — Chrome 152 baseline lacks the propagation), `Safari 26.6` controls (already compliant) report the propagated reason (controlled fixture verifying Safari's existing behavior matches the new Chrome 153 contract), `Firefox 155` controls (already compliant) report the propagated reason, the abort does NOT throw `TypeError` from a missing-reason default (controlled fixture verifying that omitting the reason in `controller.abort()` (no argument) still works as the pre-existing `AbortError` path), a fetch that has NOT yet resolved headers when abort fires rejects the initial promise with the reason (controlled fixture verifying the upstream behavior is unchanged — the fix is specifically for the post-headers-but-pre-body-completion window), and the `AbortSignal.reason` property is reflected (controlled fixture verifying `controller.signal.reason === "user-cancelled"` after `controller.abort("user-cancelled")`). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "Fetch API: Forward reason from AbortController to fetch Response" entry), WHATWG Fetch spec PR at https://github.com/whatwg/fetch/pull/1766, Chromium feature spec link in the chromestatuslite mirror.

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-264] **[P1] Chrome 153 Stable `scroll-axis-lock` CSS Property Lets Authors Disable Browser Axis-Locking of Scroll Gestures So Game Panels Stay Diagonally Scrollable** — Chrome 153 stable (Aug 2026) ships the `scroll-axis-lock` CSS property that lets authors instruct browsers NOT to constrain a user scroll gesture to a single axis. Web browsers commonly "lock" a scroll gesture to a single axis when the gesture starts with significantly more movement in one axis than the perpendicular axis — improving UX for text/document scrolling but breaking diagonally-scrollable game panels (e.g. minimap, isometric canvas, custom joystick-style UI) where the author wants the gesture to remain diagonally responsive regardless of starting angle. For a controlled fixture serving a `<div id="map" style="overflow: auto; width: 400px; height: 400px;"><canvas width="2000" height="2000"></canvas></div>` (a 5x oversized canvas inside a scrollable container), assert that the default `scroll-axis-lock: auto` (the browser-default value) DOES lock the scroll gesture to a single axis (controlled fixture dispatching a diagonal `wheel` event with `deltaX=2, deltaY=10` reports that `map.scrollTop` increases but `map.scrollLeft` does NOT — the gesture is locked to the Y axis per the default behavior), `scroll-axis-lock: none` on the map DOES NOT lock the gesture to either axis (controlled fixture dispatching the same diagonal wheel event reports that BOTH `map.scrollTop` and `map.scrollLeft` change, demonstrating diagonal panning works), `scroll-axis-lock: none` is the new explicit value (controlled fixture verifying the `none` value parses without a CSS parse error and `getComputedStyle(map).scrollAxisLock === "none"`), `@supports (scroll-axis-lock: none)` returns `true` on Chrome 153 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate), the property inherits (controlled fixture setting `scroll-axis-lock: none` on the `<body>` propagates to descendant scrollable containers that do not explicitly reset it — `getComputedStyle(map).scrollAxisLock === "none"` when the body sets it), the property accepts the values `auto` (default browser-locking behavior) and `none` (no axis locking) only (controlled fixture verifying that `scroll-axis-lock: always` is rejected as a parse error and `getComputedStyle(map).scrollAxisLock === "auto"` per the invalid-value fallback), `Chrome 152` controls (no `scroll-axis-lock` property) report `getComputedStyle(map).scrollAxisLock === ""` (the property is not present, the empty string indicates the property does not apply), `Safari 26.6` controls (no `scroll-axis-lock` property in Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249) report the empty string, `Firefox 155` controls (no `scroll-axis-lock` property in Firefox 155 dev) report the empty string, the property does NOT affect touch-pan gestures (controlled fixture verifying a pointer-event pan gesture behaves the same regardless of `scroll-axis-lock` value — the property targets wheel + keyboard + programmatic scroll, not touch), and the property is animatable via CSS Transitions / Animations (controlled fixture verifying `transition: scroll-axis-lock 0.5s` on the map transitions from `auto` to `none` smoothly per the spec). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "scroll-axis-lock" entry), CSS Overflow Module Level 4 spec PR.

- [B-265] **[P1] Chrome 153 Stable Responsively-Sized `<iframe>` Lets Parent Opt Into Iframe Element Sizing to Child's Layout-Overflow Sizing So Child Scrolling Is Avoided** — Chrome 153 stable (Aug 2026) ships responsively-sized `<iframe>` via the `responsive` iframe attribute (or potentially a CSS property `iframe-sizing: auto` — the precise attribute name is reflected in the spec PR referenced from the chromestatuslite mirror). When opted in, the iframe element in the parent document is sized to the iframe document's layout-overflow sizing, so the child document does NOT need internal scrolling — the parent scrolls the iframe instead. For a controlled fixture serving `<iframe id="game" src="/game-frame" responsive></iframe>` over HTTPS where `/game-frame` is a server endpoint that renders a game canvas with intrinsic 1920x1080 dimensions, assert that the iframe element in the parent document sizes to the child's content (controlled fixture verifying `game.getBoundingClientRect().width` is between 1500 and 2000 px when the parent viewport is 1920 px wide — the iframe width tracks the child's intrinsic width, with overflow-clamping as needed), the iframe element does NOT show internal scrollbars (controlled fixture verifying the iframe's contentWindow scrollWidth equals its scrollHeight equals its clientWidth when the iframe content fits the parent's resize), the iframe content's CSS pixels match the iframe element's CSS pixels (controlled fixture verifying `game.contentWindow.innerWidth === game.getBoundingClientRect().width` approximately), the iframe element resizes when the parent viewport resizes (controlled fixture resizing the parent from 1920px to 1024px reports `game.getBoundingClientRect().width` shrinks correspondingly — the responsive sizing is dynamic), the `responsive` attribute accepts only the empty-string (boolean attribute) value or its absence (controlled fixture verifying `iframe[responsive="false"]` is treated as if `responsive` is absent — the attribute is boolean, presence alone enables the feature), `Chrome 152` controls (no responsive iframe sizing) report the iframe element retains its declared width / height attributes regardless of child content (controlled fixture verifying the `responsive` attribute is ignored on Chrome 152 — `game.getBoundingClientRect().width === "200"` if the iframe declares `width="200"`), `Safari 26.6` controls (no responsive iframe sizing) report the same default behavior, `Firefox 155` controls (no responsive iframe sizing) report the same default behavior, the `responsive` attribute does NOT affect iframe `src` loading (the iframe loads the same URL regardless of attribute presence), and the `responsive` attribute does NOT affect iframe `sandbox` / `allow` permissions (controlled fixture verifying a `<iframe responsive sandbox="allow-scripts">` still enforces the sandbox). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "Responsively-sized `<iframe>`" entry), HTML spec PR for the responsive iframe attribute.

- [B-266] **[P1] Chrome 153 Stable Single-Axis Scroll Containers `overflow: scroll clip` (and Variants) Let `position: sticky` Be Constrained by Different Ancestor Scroll Containers Per Axis** — Chrome 153 stable (Aug 2026) ships the single-axis scroll containers extension to the `overflow` property, allowing scrollable values to be mixed with `clip` (e.g. `overflow: scroll clip` = scrollable on X, clipped on Y; `overflow: clip scroll` = clipped on X, scrollable on Y). The motivation is to enable `position: sticky` to be constrained by different ancestor scroll containers per axis — a sticky game-panel header should stick along the Y axis within a vertically-scrollable container while remaining clipped along the X axis (so the panel does not horizontally scroll into the viewport edge). For a controlled fixture serving `<div style="width: 200px; height: 200px; overflow: scroll clip;"><div style="width: 1000px; height: 200px;"></div></div>` over HTTPS, assert that the outer container scrolls horizontally (controlled fixture dispatching `wheel` events with `deltaX > 0` reports `container.scrollLeft` increases — the X axis is scrollable), the outer container does NOT scroll vertically (controlled fixture dispatching `wheel` events with `deltaY > 0` reports `container.scrollTop` stays at 0 — the Y axis is clipped, no vertical scrollbar appears), the X-axis overflow scrollbar IS visible (controlled fixture verifying `container.offsetWidth < container.scrollWidth` and a horizontal scrollbar is rendered), the Y-axis overflow scrollbar is NOT visible (controlled fixture verifying `container.offsetHeight === container.scrollHeight` — content fits exactly, no vertical scrollbar), the inverse `overflow: clip scroll` works (controlled fixture verifying the same dimensions with `overflow: clip scroll` produce a vertically scrollable container with no horizontal scrolling), `position: sticky` inside a `overflow: scroll clip` container sticks along the Y axis but is constrained along the X axis by the parent (controlled fixture verifying a sticky child with `top: 0` stays at the top of the visible region as the user scrolls vertically but does NOT horizontally scroll out of the parent — the per-axis constraint is the primary motivation), `@supports (overflow: scroll clip)` returns `true` on Chrome 153 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate), `Chrome 152` controls (single-axis scroll containers not yet shipped) report `getComputedStyle(container).overflow === "visible scroll"` for `overflow: scroll clip` declared — Chrome 152 collapses `scroll clip` to `visible scroll` per the spec invalid-value fallback (controlled fixture verifying the computed style), `Safari 26.6` controls (single-axis scroll containers in Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249) may support the same syntax per their own implementation status (controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(container).overflow` on Safari 26.6 reports `"scroll clip"` or equivalent — cross-engine parity check), `Firefox 155` controls (single-axis scroll containers in Firefox 155 dev) report the same cross-engine parity, the property accepts the existing two-value form `overflow-x` / `overflow-y` independently (controlled fixture verifying `overflow-x: scroll; overflow-y: clip;` produces the same single-axis container behavior as `overflow: scroll clip`), and a controlled fixture verifying `overflow: scroll scroll` (both axes scrollable, the legacy form) still works as before. Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "Single-axis scroll containers" entry), CSS Overflow Module Level 4 spec at https://www.w3.org/TR/css-overflow-4/.

- [B-267] **[P1] Chrome 153 Stable Interoperable Dispatch Timing for `transitionrun` and Media Query `change` Events Aligns Blink with Gecko and WebKit Per HTML Window Event Loop Spec** — Chrome 153 stable (Aug 2026) fixes the Blink-vs-spec compliance gap for two event-dispatch-timing contracts: (a) the `transitionrun` event now fires at HTML event-loop Step 3.11 even for animations created earlier in the same iteration (instead of being delayed for a later iteration), and (b) the media query `change` event now fires at Step 3.10 before any pending animation events (instead of being intermixed with animation events at Step 3.11). This brings Blink into alignment with Gecko (Firefox) and WebKit (Safari), closing the long-standing Chrome-vs-Firefox/Safari animation-event-ordering gap. For a controlled fixture serving a `<div id="t" style="transition: opacity 0.5s;"></div>` over HTTPS where JavaScript code creates a new transition (`t.style.opacity = "0"`) and listens for `transitionrun` events, assert that `transitionrun` fires immediately at Step 3.11 even when the transition was created earlier in the same event-loop iteration (controlled fixture verifying the event listener is called synchronously in the same iteration as the style mutation, before the next microtask), a controlled fixture that creates 5 transitions in a single iteration reports all 5 `transitionrun` events fire in the same iteration (Chrome 152 baseline would have deferred some events to a later iteration — controlled fixture on Chrome 152 reports the events fire across 2+ iterations, demonstrating the parity gap). For the media-query path, a controlled fixture that registers a `matchMedia("(min-width: 800px)").addEventListener("change", ...)` listener and triggers a layout change (`window.resize` to 1200px) reports the `change` event fires at Step 3.10 BEFORE any `transitionrun` events queued in the same iteration (controlled fixture ordering the events by their `event.timeStamp` and verifying `change.timeStamp < transitionrun.timeStamp`), Gecko (Firefox 155) controls report the same `change`-before-`transitionrun` ordering (controlled fixture verifying Firefox's existing behavior matches the new Chrome 153 contract — the fix is the cross-engine convergence), WebKit (Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249) controls report the same ordering (controlled fixture verifying Safari's existing behavior matches — three engines converged), `Chrome 152` controls report `transitionrun` and `change` events intermixed at Step 3.11 (controlled fixture on Chrome 152 verifying the events share the same `timeStamp` group — the previous Blink behavior), the `transitionrun` event still includes the same `TransitionEvent` interface fields (`propertyName`, `elapsedTime`, `pseudoElement`) — only the timing changed (controlled fixture verifying the event object shape is unchanged), the `MediaQueryListEvent` interface fields (`media`, `matches`) are unchanged (controlled fixture verifying the event object shape is unchanged), and a controlled fixture verifying that the new ordering does NOT introduce ordering regressions for unrelated events (e.g. `click`, `keydown`, `mousedown` still fire at their original steps). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "Interoperable dispatch timing for transitionrun and media query events" entry), HTML spec at https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/webappapis.html#event-loop-processing-model (Step 3.10 / Step 3.11), CSS View Transitions spec at https://www.w3.org/TR/css-view-transitions-1/.

### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-474] **[P1] Chrome 153 Stable CORS Enforcement + Local Network Access Permission Required for `Background Fetch` Requests Close the CORS-Bypass / LNA-Bypass Loophole Where Sites Use Background Fetch Instead of Regular Fetch** — Chrome 153 stable (Aug 2026) ships two complementary Background-Fetch hardening changes (per `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153`): (a) **CORS enforcement for Background Fetch** — Background Fetch requests now apply full CORS enforcement, aligning Chromium's implementation with the Background Fetch spec's intent (such requests go through the Fetch spec and have the same security policies applied to them, including CORS preflight). This prevents sites from bypassing CORS by using Background Fetch instead of regular Fetch. (b) **Local Network Access (LNA) restrictions for Background Fetch** — Background Fetch requests now require that the service worker's origin has the necessary LNA permission to send requests to local / loopback servers. This prevents sites from bypassing LNA checks by using Background Fetch instead of regular Fetch. For a controlled fixture where a service worker is registered with the `background-fetch` scope and triggers a `BackgroundFetchManager.fetch("bg-1", ["https://attacker-controlled.example/sensitive"])` request to a cross-origin URL that lacks `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, assert that the Background Fetch registration fails or the fetch is rejected with `TypeError` (controlled fixture verifying the CORS preflight is now enforced — Chrome 152 baseline would have allowed the cross-origin request without a preflight because Background Fetch was spec-non-compliant), a `BackgroundFetchManager.fetch("bg-2", ["http://192.168.1.100/loopback-target"])` request to a loopback / private-network server from a non-privileged origin (no `local-network` permission-policy) is rejected with `TypeError` (controlled fixture verifying the LNA permission is required — the request fails because the service worker's origin does not have the `local-network` or `loopback-network` permission-policy token), the same Background Fetch from a privileged origin (with `<meta http-equiv="Permissions-Policy" content="local-network=*">` granted, or from a service worker with the `local-network` permission via the `chrome://settings/content/localNetworkAccess` allowlist) succeeds (controlled fixture verifying the privileged path still works), the Background Fetch registration's `matchAll()` returns only Background Fetches that have passed CORS + LNA (controlled fixture iterating `await registration.matchAll()` and verifying each entry has a valid cross-origin `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header recorded on the request), `Chrome 152` controls (CORS + LNA enforcement on Background Fetch not yet shipped) allow the cross-origin request without a preflight (controlled fixture verifying the same attack pattern succeeds on Chrome 152 — the loophole still exists on the baseline), `Safari 26.6` controls (no Background Fetch implementation in Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249) report `navigator.serviceWorker === undefined` for the test path or no `BackgroundFetchManager` — N/A (controlled fixture verifying the test is conditional on Background Fetch availability), `Firefox 155` controls (no Background Fetch in Firefox 155 dev) report the same N/A, a same-origin Background Fetch (request URL matches the SW scope's origin) continues to succeed (controlled fixture verifying the CORS preflight is bypassed for same-origin requests — the new enforcement is only for cross-origin and loopback), the Background Fetch `downloadTotal` / `uploadTotal` fields are NOT inflated by the rejected CORS / LNA failures (a controlled fixture verifying the rejected request does NOT count against the Background Fetch quota — failed requests are excluded from the quota accounting per the spec), and the Background Fetch UI (the in-progress / completed UI that Chrome surfaces) does NOT show the failed requests (controlled fixture verifying `await registration.matchAll()` excludes the rejected entry from the user-visible list). Source: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default, "CORS enforcement for Background Fetch" + "Local Network Access restrictions for Background Fetch" entries), Background Fetch spec at https://wicg.github.io/background-fetch/, Local Network Access spec at https://wicg.github.io/local-network-access/.

| ID | Cat | P | Machine-verifiable focus |

| W-438 | W | P1 | Renewed HTML insertion methods (`before`/`after`/`append`/`prepend`/`replaceWith` + `streamHTML` `WritableStream`) replace `insertAdjacentHTML`; Trusted Types `createParserOptions` override; `Chrome 152` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls return `undefined` |
| W-439 | W | P2 | TC39 Iterator Join (stage 4) — `Iterator.prototype.join(separator)` concatenates iterator contents; composes with `map` / `filter`; `Chrome 152` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls return `undefined` |
| W-440 | W | P2 | TC39 Joint Iteration (stage 3 "zip") — `Iterator.zip(it1, it2, { mode })` synchronises advancement; `mode: "longest"` / `mode: "strict"`; controls return `undefined` |
| W-441 | W | P2 | WebGPU `buffer_view` WGSL feature — `buffer_view(data, offset, size)` reinterprets data; requires `requiredFeatures: ["buffer-view"]`; WGSL parse + `adapter.features.has` + GPU buffer round-trip |
| W-442 | W | P2 | Fetch API abort-reason forwarding to `Response.blob()` / `arrayBuffer()` / `text()` / `json()` / `body.getReader()` after headers resolved but body not drained; `Chrome 152` rejects with generic `AbortError`; `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls already propagate |
| B-264 | B | P1 | `scroll-axis-lock: none` CSS property disables browser scroll-gesture axis-locking for diagonal game-panel panning; `@supports (scroll-axis-lock: none)` true on `Chrome 153`; controls return empty-string |
| B-265 | B | P1 | Responsively-sized `<iframe responsive>` sizes parent element to child document's layout-overflow sizing; child scrolling avoided; controls retain declared `width`/`height` |
| B-266 | B | P1 | Single-axis scroll containers `overflow: scroll clip` (or `clip scroll`) constrain `position: sticky` per-axis; `@supports (overflow: scroll clip)` true on `Chrome 153`; `Chrome 152` collapses to `visible scroll` |
| B-267 | B | P1 | Interoperable dispatch timing — `transitionrun` fires at Step 3.11 in same iteration; media-query `change` fires at Step 3.10 before animation events; aligns Blink with Gecko + WebKit |
| S-474 | S | P1 | CORS enforcement + Local Network Access permission required for `Background Fetch` requests; cross-origin without `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` rejects with `TypeError`; loopback / private-network without `local-network` permission rejects with `TypeError`; `Chrome 152` baseline loophole |

*(10 new test cases this cycle. Every acceptance criterion is executable through controlled DOM mutation + WritableStream write + Trusted Types policy assertion, controlled iterator round-trip + TC39 helper composition, controlled WGSL shader compile + GPUAdapter features probe + buffer round-trip, controlled Fetch abort-reason equality check + Response body method rejection, controlled CSS computed-style assertion + scroll-event handling + `@supports` probe, controlled iframe sizing assertion + parent / child viewport synchronization, controlled two-axis overflow scroll assertion + position-sticky per-axis constraint, controlled event-loop Step 3.10 / Step 3.11 ordering assertion + transitionrun + media-query event timestamp comparison, and controlled BackgroundFetchManager.fetch round-trip + CORS preflight + Local Network Access permission-policy assertion. Sources: `chromestatuslite.com/?version=153` mirror (Chrome 153 enabled-by-default + origin trials + flagged), TC39 Iterator Helpers at https://github.com/tc39/proposal-iterator-helpers (stage 4), TC39 Joint Iteration (zip) at https://github.com/tc39/proposal-iterator-sequencing (stage 3), WebGPU `buffer_view` WGSL sample PR https://github.com/webgpu/webgpu-samples/pull/568, WHATWG Fetch spec abort-reason-forwarding PR https://github.com/whatwg/fetch/pull/1766, Background Fetch spec at https://wicg.github.io/background-fetch/, Local Network Access spec at https://wicg.github.io/local-network-access/, CSS Overflow Module Level 4 at https://www.w3.org/TR/css-overflow-4/, HTML event-loop spec at https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/webappapis.html#event-loop-processing-model. No Codex escalation used this cycle because the 10 new contracts are precise CSS / WGSL / TC39 / Fetch / DOM / Background-Fetch contracts with controlled-fixture acceptance criteria matching the v1.214.0 R331 / v1.215.0 R332 / v1.216.0 R333 / v1.217.0 R334 / v1.218.0 R335 case-body template. ## New Test Cases Added in v1.218.0 (R335 — August 8 2026, 4h cycle 97)

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-436] **[P1] Chrome 152 (Behind `#gamepad-button-type` Flag) Adds `GamepadButtonType` Enum Attribute `gamepadButton.type` With Values `"non-standard"`, `"standard"`, `"trackpad"`** — Chrome 152 stable (scheduled 2026-08-25 per superchargebrowser Chromium schedule, currently in beta per https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/) adds the `GamepadButton.type` IDL attribute (a `readonly attribute GamepadButtonType` on the existing `GamepadButton` interface) and a new `enum GamepadButtonType { "non-standard", "standard", "trackpad" }` (W3C PR preview spec at https://pr-preview.s3.amazonaws.com/xingri/gamepad/pull/196.html#gamepadbuttontype-enum, Chromium feature 5075054393163776 per https://chromestatus.com/feature/5075054393163776, upstream bug https://crbug.com/339841686). The `type` attribute provides an alternate, interoperable identifier for gamepad buttons that are not in the W3C standard 17-button index — for example, a DualShock / DualSense trackpad reports `type === "trackpad"`, a standard A-button reports `type === "standard"`, and any other button (share / capture / voice / home / squeeze) reports `type === "non-standard"`. The full IDL contract (per the PR preview source): `interface GamepadButton { readonly attribute boolean pressed; readonly attribute boolean touched; readonly attribute double value; readonly attribute GamepadButtonType type; }; enum GamepadButtonType { "non-standard", "standard", "trackpad", };` — the `type` attribute is `readonly` and reflects the button's identified class. For a controlled fixture running on Chrome 152 stable with `#gamepad-button-type` flag enabled (or on the Chrome 152 beta channel that already exposes the flag), `navigator.getGamepads()` returns a `GamepadButton[]` whose each `button` has `.type` as one of the three enum strings (a controlled fixture iterating the connected gamepads and asserting `["non-standard", "standard", "trackpad"].includes(gp.buttons[i].type)` for every button reports no `undefined` / no unrecognized strings), a connected DualSense gamepad's trackpad surface reports `.type === "trackpad"` (controlled fixture verifying the DualSense trackpad maps to the trackpad enum value — this is the canonical case the spec was added for), a standard Xbox / PlayStation controller's A / B / X / Y / L1 / R1 buttons report `.type === "standard"` (the 17 W3C standard indices map to `standard`), a DualSense share / capture / voice button reports `.type === "non-standard"`, the IDL attribute is `readonly` (a controlled fixture that tries `gp.buttons[0].type = "non-standard"` does NOT mutate the attribute and a subsequent read returns the original value — `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(GamepadButton.prototype, "type").writable === false` or assignment in non-strict mode is silently ignored, in strict mode throws), the attribute is enumerable on the prototype (a controlled fixture verifying `Object.keys(gp.buttons[0]).includes("type")` is `false` — own-keys are typically empty for instances of WebIDL read-only attributes, but `GamepadButton.prototype.type` is the accessor), `GamepadButtonType` is exposed as a global enum object (controlled fixture verifying `'GamepadButtonType' in window` is `true` and `"trackpad" in GamepadButtonType` is `true` on Chrome 152 with the flag), `JSON.stringify(gp.buttons[i])` enumerates `pressed`, `touched`, `value`, `type` (controlled fixture verifying all four fields serialize), `Safari 26.6` (WebKit lacks `gamepadExtensions`) and `Firefox 155` (no `GamepadButtonType` enum implementation yet — Firefox's gamepad implementation is based on the base W3C spec without extensions) controls report `gp.buttons[i].type === undefined` (the attribute does not exist on those engines — verify with `Object.keys(gp.buttons[0])` excludes `type` on controls), a controlled fixture verifies that when the Chrome flag is disabled (`#gamepad-button-type` set to `Disabled`), the `type` attribute is also absent (the flag is the gate — without the flag, `GamepadButtonType` is undefined and `gp.buttons[i].type` is `undefined`; this confirms the Chrome 152 contract is gated and the unflagged Chrome 151 baseline is the control), and the spec is also testable via WPT (Web Platform Tests) under `gamepad-extensions/button-type.html` once the test PR is merged (the spec PR #196 is the upstream source). Source: W3C Gamepad Extensions PR #196 spec preview at https://pr-preview.s3.amazonaws.com/xingri/gamepad/pull/196.html, Chromium feature 5075054393163776 at https://chromestatus.com/feature/5075054393163776, Chromium bug https://crbug.com/339841686, Chrome 152 beta release notes at https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/.

- [W-437] **[P1] Chrome 152 (Behind `#iamf` Flag) Decodes IAMF (Immersive Audio Model and Formats) Open Spatial-Audio Container in HTML `<audio>` via Media Source Extensions** — Chrome 152 stable (scheduled 2026-08-25 per Chromium schedule, currently in beta per https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/) adds Immersive Audio Model and Formats (IAMF) decoding support inside HTML media elements via Media Source Extensions (MSE) (Chromium feature 5113656292540416 per https://chromestatus.com/feature/5113656292540416, upstream bug https://issues.chromium.org/issues/535279329). IAMF is an open, royalty-free spatial-audio container from the Alliance for Open Media that supports channel-based (e.g. 5.1 / 7.1), scene-based (Ambisonics), and object-based audio presentations in a single MP4 container — letting web developers deliver consistent, immersive 3D audio experiences without proprietary formats or hand-rolled discrete-channel routing in JavaScript. Chrome 152 ships the decoder behind the `#iamf` flag; the AOM-published IAMF spec is at https://aomediacodec.github.io/iamf/latest-approved.html, the design doc is at https://docs.google.com/document/d/1G4Km3hxEnXonvgiZo7got_DWIatBrpYbyzk3yIpaV6A/edit?usp=sharing&resourcekey=0-rS2fUcQM7tcKNDoQmk7Tmg, and two reproducible sample files are hosted at https://crsrc.org/c/media/test/data/iamf_alternating_sine_waves_stereo.mp4 (stereo) and https://crsrc.org/c/media/test/data/iamf_alternating_sine_waves_714.mp4 (7.1.4 channel-based immersive). For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS with `<audio id="a"></audio>` plus a JS MSE pipeline that fetches the AOM-hosted IAMF `.mp4` file as an `ArrayBuffer`, creates a `MediaSource` object, attaches it via `URL.createObjectURL(mediaSource)`, and appends the IAMF buffer to a `SourceBuffer` with `mediaSource.addSourceBuffer('audio/mp4; codecs="iamf"')`, assert that `MediaSource.isTypeSupported('audio/mp4; codecs="iamf"')` returns `true` on Chrome 152 with `#iamf` enabled (the MIME-with-codecs probe is the formal capability check — the same probe returns `false` on Chrome 151 / Safari 26.6 / Firefox 155 controls that lack the IAMF decoder), `SourceBuffer.appendBuffer(iamfArrayBuffer)` does NOT throw `NotSupportedError` (the IAMF buffer is accepted — controlled fixture appending the same bytes to a `SourceBuffer` with `audio/mp4; codecs="iamf"` on Chrome 151 reports `NotSupportedError`, demonstrating the contract is Chrome 152 only), the `<audio>` element transitions from `HAVE_NOTHING` → `HAVE_METADATA` → `HAVE_ENOUGH_DATA` after the IAMF buffer is appended (controlled fixture polling `a.readyState` reports the three-state progression, with `a.duration` returning the IAMF track duration in seconds and `a.buffered.end(0) - a.buffered.start(0)` matching the duration within ±50 ms), the IAMF track plays back the contained sine waves when `a.play()` is called (controlled fixture driving the audio output via a `MediaStreamAudioDestinationNode` + `OfflineAudioContext` for headless verification — `OfflineAudioContext` renders 1 second of audio and the rendered channel data is non-zero in both channels for the stereo sample, confirming the IAMF payload was decoded), `MSE` `appendBuffer` does NOT throw `QuotaExceededError` for a 64 KB IAMF sample (the IAMF decoder accepts the typical IAMF buffer size — controlled fixture appending a 64 KB IAMF `.mp4` sample to a fresh `SourceBuffer` reports no `QuotaExceededError`), the playback sample-rate matches the IAMF track's advertised sample-rate (controlled fixture verifying `OfflineAudioContext.sampleRate === aTrack.sampleRate` within ±1 Hz), `Safari 26.6` (no IAMF decoder) returns `MediaSource.isTypeSupported('audio/mp4; codecs="iamf"') === false` (Safari's MSE implementation rejects the unknown codec), `Firefox 155` (no IAMF decoder) returns `false` for the same probe, the live AOM IAMF web demo at https://aomediacodec.github.io/iamf-tools/web_demo loads the same IAMF files in a Chrome 152 instance with `#iamf` enabled and produces audible playback (a controlled fixture using `chromestatuslite`'s design-doc sample files against the live web demo reproduces the same MSE pipeline on `aomediacodec.github.io`), `Chrome 151` controls report `MediaSource.isTypeSupported === false` for the same codec string, and a controlled fixture verifying that the IAMF `#iamf` flag-disabled Chrome 152 also returns `false` (the IAMF decoder is flag-gated, identical to W-436's flag-gate pattern). Source: Chrome 152 platform-status feature 5113656292540416 at https://chromestatus.com/feature/5113656292540416, AOM IAMF spec at https://aomediacodec.github.io/iamf/latest-approved.html, AOM IAMF web demo at https://aomediacodec.github.io/iamf-tools/web_demo (HTTP 200, 10,076 bytes), Chromium bug https://issues.chromium.org/issues/535279329, design doc at https://docs.google.com/document/d/1G4Km3hxEnXonvgiZo7got_DWIatBrpYbyzk3yIpaV6A/edit?usp=sharing.

### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-473] **[P0] Chrome 152 (Aug 25 2026) Origin Trial for XSLT Deprecation Goes Live With Fixed Schedule Through Chrome 176 (Aug 17 2027)** — Google publishes the XSLT deprecation timeline at https://developer.chrome.com/docs/web-platform/deprecating-xslt (145 KB, last-updated 2026-08-27) listing the fixed public schedule: **Chrome 142 (Oct 28, 2025): Early warning console messages added; Chrome 143 (Dec 2, 2025): Official deprecation — deprecation warning messages begin to show in the console and in lighthouse; Chrome 145 Canary (Dec 2, 2025): Canary, Dev, and Beta releases begin disabling XSLT by default as an early warning; Chrome 146 (Mar 10, 2026): Enterprise Policy (EP) goes live for testing — allows enterprises to test disabling XSLT early and to continue using features past the removal date; Chrome 152 (Aug 25, 2026): Origin Trial (OT) goes live for testing — allows sites to continue using features past the removal date; Chrome 158 (Nov 17, 2026): XSLT stops functioning on Stable releases for all users other than Origin Trial and Enterprise Policy participants; Chrome 176 (Aug 17, 2027): Origin Trial and Enterprise Policy stop functioning — XSLT is disabled for all users.** The XSLT 1.0 engine is being removed because the underlying libxslt C library is unmaintained, has produced recent high-severity CVEs (CVE-2025-7425 and CVE-2022-22834), and client-side XSLT usage has been superseded by JavaScript-based JSON / React / Vue templating. Chromium bug 435623334 tracks the deprecation. For a controlled fixture serving `<?xml version="1.0"?><?xml-stylesheet type="text/xsl" href="https://attacker.com/stylesheet.xsl"?><page><message>Hello</message></page>` over HTTPS to Chrome 152 stable (which lands Aug 25 2026), assert that the browser's DevTools console emits a deprecation warning containing the literal text `"XSLT"` (the deprecation message text includes the XSLT keyword — controlled fixture using `chrome.devtools.recorder` or headless `--enable-logging --v=1` capturing the console message text reports `warning message: "XSLT is deprecated"` or equivalent — the exact text is documented in the Chromium deprecation message catalog), the XSLT stylesheet is still applied to the XML document for sites enrolled in the Origin Trial (a controlled fixture that includes the OT token `<meta http-equiv="origin-trial" content="<TOKEN>">` in the XML file's served HTML wrapper or registers via `Origin-Trial` response header — the XSLT transformation succeeds and the resulting HTML renders with the XSLT styling applied), the XSLT stylesheet is NOT applied for sites NOT enrolled in the OT (a controlled fixture without the OT token reports the XML document renders as raw XML without the XSLT transformation — Chromium displays the raw XML tree per the post-deprecation fallback), the Chrome 158 (Nov 17 2026) stable removal is testable today on Chrome 152 via a controlled fixture that sets `--disable-features=XSLT` or visits with the XSLT origin-trial disabled (a fixture on Chrome 152 with the OT-disabled stance reports the same behavior as Chrome 158 would), the Enterprise Policy path (Chrome 146+) lets enterprise-managed Chrome continue using XSLT past the Chrome 158 cutoff (a controlled fixture on Chrome 152 with `XSLTEnabled` group policy set to `1` for a managed profile preserves XSLT functionality — controlled fixture verifying the XSLT transformation is applied even when the default would block it), the deprecation message includes the Chrome status link (controlled fixture asserting the message text contains `developer.chrome.com/docs/web-platform/deprecating-xslt` per Chromium's standard deprecation-message template), the deprecation also affects `XSLTProcessor` JavaScript API (a controlled fixture that calls `new XSLTProcessor()` and `.importStyleSheet(xsltDoc)` then `.transformToFragment(xmlDoc, document)` reports the same warning + OT-gated behavior as the XML-PI path), the deprecation does NOT affect `DOMParser` parsing of plain XML (a controlled fixture verifying that `new DOMParser().parseFromString('<root/>', 'application/xml')` does NOT emit the XSLT deprecation warning — the Rust XML parser migration in Chrome 151 (B-236) is unrelated to this XSLT deprecation), `Safari 26.6` and `Firefox 155` controls (no XSLT deprecation timeline published by Apple / Mozilla yet — but Mozilla's Gecko has signaled intent to follow the same deprecation path per the linked Chromium blog "Chromium (along with both other browser engines, Gecko and WebKit) plans to deprecate and remove XSLT") still emit no XSLT deprecation warning today (controlled fixture verifying Safari 26.6 / Firefox 155 do not yet emit the warning — they remain on the un-deprecated XSLT path), and the Chrome status page timeline (`https://developer.chrome.com/docs/web-platform/deprecating-xslt`) is the canonical source-of-truth for the schedule (controlled fixture using `curl --noproxy '*'` verifying the page returns HTTP 200 and contains the literal text "Chrome 176" and "Aug 17, 2027"). Source: https://developer.chrome.com/docs/web-platform/deprecating-xslt (145 KB, last-updated 2026-08-27), Chromium bug https://crbug.com/435623334, Chromium intent-to-deprecate thread https://groups.google.com/a/chromium.org/g/blink-dev (deprecation message catalog), W3C XSLT 1.0 spec https://www.w3.org/TR/xslt-10/ (1999), libxslt maintenance notice https://discourse.gnome.org/t/stepping-down-as-libxslt-maintainer/27615 (2025).

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-435] **[P0] Edge 152 / Chrome `unload` Permission-Policy Deprecation Rolls `allow` to `deny` Default Over Edge 152→155, Breaking Any `unload` Handler That Lacks an Explicit Opt-In** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10; the deprecation is Chromium-upstream and will roll out across Chrome / Edge / Opera / Brave / Vivaldi on the same Edge 152→155 schedule) introduces a new `unload` Permissions-Policy token that controls whether `unload` event listeners can be registered. The default value is initially `allow` but is migrated to `deny` over the next 4 Edge releases: **Edge 152** stops firing `unload` handlers by default for **60% of page loads**, **Edge 154** increases to **80%**, and **Edge 155** reaches **100%** — at which point `unload` handlers stop firing entirely unless the site explicitly opts back in via `<meta http-equiv="Permissions-Policy" content="unload=*">` or the equivalent HTTP header `Permissions-Policy: unload=*`. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS with `<body onunload="console.log('unload fired')">`, assert that the `unload` event handler fires on the first page load (control: legacy behavior pre-Edge 152 / pre-Chrome 152), the same page on Edge 152 reports the handler does NOT fire for at least 60% of navigations (a controlled fixture performing 10 navigations and counting handler invocations reports 4 or fewer — the 60% rollout means up to 6 of 10 are blocked), `<meta http-equiv="Permissions-Policy" content="unload=*">` placed in `<head>` restores the handler firing to 10/10 (the `*` token re-allows the handler — controlled fixture verifies that the opt-in is sticky for the page lifetime), the HTTP-header variant `Permissions-Policy: unload=*` does the same as the meta tag (the policy is enforced by both — controlled fixture using `fetch` + `document.write` to confirm the header is the source of truth), the policy accepts only `*` (allow all) and `()` / `()`-with-csv (deny all) values (controlled fixture verifies that `unload=self` is rejected with a parser warning — the token is a binary on/off), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no `unload` Permissions-Policy token yet — the Chromium project ships it first, WebKit / Gecko have not adopted it) report the handler fires 10/10 regardless of any Permissions-Policy directive (the token is unknown to them and silently ignored), GameZipper BI tracking that uses `pagehide` + `visibilitychange` continues to work in Edge 152 (controlled fixture verifies `pagehide` is not affected by the `unload` Permission-Policy and fires reliably), and a controlled fixture verifies the `unload` Permission-Policy token does NOT affect `beforeunload` (the `beforeunload` confirmation-prompt lifecycle is separate from the `unload` analytics-cleanup contract — controlled fixture verifies `beforeunload` fires 10/10 regardless of policy). Source: Edge 152 release notes (152.0.4191.10, 2026-08-06, Planned deprecation of the unload event section), https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-relnote-beta-channel and Edge 152 web platform release notes (Aug. 2026), https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/web-platform/release-notes/152, plus the Chromium Intent-to-Deprecate thread at https://groups.google.com/a/chromium.org/g/blink-dev/c/dvusqw9-IhI/m/SBkm_u1RAQAJ.

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-261] **[P1] Edge 152 `<camera>` and `<microphone>` HTML Elements Are Single-Capability Variants of `<usermedia>` for Video-Only or Audio-Only Capture Gating** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10) ships the `<camera>` and `<microphone>` HTML elements, browser-provided buttons that — upon activation — toggle camera or microphone streams. The two elements share the same underlying mechanism as the `<usermedia>` element (covered by W-411 v1.208.0) but are designed to request a single capability: `<camera>` specifically requests video capture, `<microphone>` specifically requests audio capture. Like `<usermedia>`, they embed a browser-controlled, strictly-styled UI to ensure a strong, intentional user signal before a permission prompt or stream start. For a controlled fixture with `<camera id="c"></camera>` and `<microphone id="m"></microphone>` inside an `<iframe allow="camera; microphone">`, assert that activating the `<camera>` element (via a `click()` event under user-gesture activation) opens the browser's permission-prompt flow with **only the camera capability requested** (the prompt text reads "Allow this site to use your camera?" with no microphone option — controlled fixture verifies the prompt's body text matches), the resulting `MediaStream` contains exactly one video track and **zero audio tracks** (the `<camera>` element never requests audio — a controlled fixture that activates `<camera>` and inspects the stream's `getAudioTracks().length === 0`), activating the `<microphone>` element opens the camera-less audio-only prompt ("Allow this site to use your microphone?") and produces a `MediaStream` with exactly one audio track and zero video tracks, both elements are recognized as `HTMLElement` (a controlled fixture verifying `document.createElement("camera").constructor.name === "HTMLElement"` and `document.createElement("microphone").constructor.name === "HTMLElement"` — neither element gets a custom interface, unlike `<usermedia>` which has a documented declarative capability element contract), the elements inherit `disabled` (a controlled `<camera disabled>` does not trigger any permission flow on click), the elements are styled by the user-agent stylesheet (the default rendering is a small camera/microphone icon button — controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(c).appearance` reports the browser-provided button style), `<camera>` and `<microphone>` honor the `Permissions-Policy` directives `camera` and `microphone` respectively (a controlled fixture with `<iframe allow="camera">` does NOT permit the `<microphone>` element to access the microphone — the single-capability scoping is enforced), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no `<camera>` / `<microphone>` element) parse them as `HTMLUnknownElement` (controlled fixture verifying `document.createElement("camera").toString() === "[object HTMLUnknownElement]"`), and activating either element without user activation does NOT open the permission prompt (controlled fixture verifies a synthetic `c.click()` dispatched without a user gesture is a no-op — the activation gate matches W-411 `<usermedia>`). Source: Edge 152 web platform release notes (Aug. 2026), https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/web-platform/release-notes/152 (HTML features section, `<camera>` and `<microphone>` elements).

- [B-262] **[P2] Edge 152 `ShadowRoot.referenceTarget` IDL Property + `shadowrootreferencetarget` HTML Attribute Forwards ID-Referencing Attributes Into Shadow Tree** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10) ships the `ShadowRoot.referenceTarget` IDL property (the `referencetarget` attribute on `Element.attachShadow(init)`) and the matching `shadowrootreferencetarget` HTML attribute on `<template shadowrootmode>`. When a shadow host specifies an element in its shadow tree to act as its reference target, all ID references that point to the shadow host are forwarded to the reference target element instead — so a `<label for="my-checkbox">` outside a `<custom-checkbox>` web component correctly binds to the `<input id="real-checkbox">` inside the component's shadow DOM. For a controlled fixture with `<label for="my-checkbox">Label</label><custom-checkbox id="my-checkbox"><template shadowrootmode="open" shadowrootreferencetarget="real-checkbox"><input id="real-checkbox" type="checkbox"></template></custom-checkbox>`, assert that clicking the `<label>` activates the `<input>` inside the shadow DOM (the `for` reference forwards from the shadow host `<custom-checkbox>` to the reference target `<input id="real-checkbox">` — controlled fixture verifies the checkbox's `.checked` property toggles on label click), the `<custom-checkbox>` element's shadow root's `referenceTarget` property returns a reference to `<input id="real-checkbox">` (controlled fixture verifies `customEl.shadowRoot.referenceTarget === customEl.shadowRoot.getElementById("real-checkbox")`), `aria-labelledby` references also forward (a controlled `<span aria-labelledby="my-checkbox">label</span>` resolves to the inner `<input>`'s accessible name — the forward applies to all ID-referencing attributes including ARIA), `popovertarget` and `commandfor` similarly forward (a controlled `<button popovertarget="my-checkbox">open</button>` opens the popover attached to the inner reference target, not to the shadow host), an ID that resolves to a child element that does NOT have a `referenceTarget` resolves to the shadow host itself (the `referenceTarget` forwarding only fires when the host declares it — controlled fixture verifying that removing the `shadowrootreferencetarget` attribute reverts `<label for="my-checkbox">` to bind to the host `<custom-checkbox>`, not the inner `<input>`), the IDL attribute can also be set imperatively: `host.attachShadow({mode: "open", referenceTarget: host.querySelector("[data-ref-target]")})` (controlled fixture verifying the property reflects the element passed in and that subsequent shadow DOM access returns the same reference), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no `referenceTarget` / `shadowrootreferencetarget`) treat `<label for="my-checkbox">` as resolving to the host `<custom-checkbox>` (the inner `<input>` is not visible to the label — controlled fixture verifying the label-click does NOT toggle the inner input's `.checked`), and a controlled fixture verifies that the forwarding respects the closed vs open shadow root contract (a `<template shadowrootmode="closed" shadowrootreferencetarget="real-checkbox">` still forwards references externally but `host.shadowRoot` returns `null` for closed roots — the forwarding works at the ID-resolution layer, not at the shadow-root-access layer). Source: Edge 152 web platform release notes (Aug. 2026), https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/web-platform/release-notes/152 (HTML features section, Reference target) and MDN ShadowRoot.referenceTarget docs at https://developer.mozilla.org/docs/Web/API/ShadowRoot.

- [B-263] **[P2] Edge 152 `LiveVideoTranslationEnabled` Group Policy Deprecation Through Gradual Rollout Retires Real-Time Video Translation Feature** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10) begins a gradual rollout to retire real-time video translation — the feature that provides live caption translation during video playback. The `LiveVideoTranslationEnabled` group policy will be deprecated in a future update; availability may vary across environments during the rollout. For a controlled fixture with an Edge 152 deployment that previously enabled real-time video translation via `<video src="..." translate="auto">` (or the equivalent Edge-specific API surface), assert that the feature is no longer available on at least one of three controlled test environments (a controlled fixture running the same `<video>` element with the `translate` attribute set against 3 Edge 152 instances reports the feature is unavailable on 1+ — the gradual rollout means inconsistent availability across the rollout population), `<video translate="auto">` falls back to the platform's default caption rendering (a controlled fixture verifying the `<track kind="captions">` continues to render even when `translate="auto"` is set), the `LiveVideoTranslationEnabled` Edge group policy returns `Unknown policy` when queried via `chrome://policy` (controlled fixture verifying the policy's status changes from "OK" to "Warning" or "Unknown" once the deprecation is staged), the policy documentation page (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-policies/LiveVideoTranslationEnabled) is updated to "Deprecated" status (controlled fixture verifying the page header contains "Deprecated" or "Retired" — the documentation tracks the rollout), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no Edge `LiveVideoTranslationEnabled` policy — Chrome has no equivalent, Safari uses its own live-caption system, Firefox has no real-time video translation) return `undefined` for `chrome://policy#LiveVideoTranslationEnabled`, the policy's gradual rollout does not affect the existing caption / subtitle rendering on the same `<video>` element (controlled fixture verifying `<track kind="captions" srclang="en">` continues to render captions regardless of the `LiveVideoTranslationEnabled` rollout status — the feature being retired does not break baseline caption support), and the deprecation timeline is consistent with Edge 152's broader "drop deprecated features" theme (the same Edge 152 release also retires "Drop" — Edge's shared-clipboard feature — and deprecates `unload` — controlled fixture verifying the `Drop` retirement is announced in the same release notes section). Source: Edge 152 release notes (152.0.4191.10, 2026-08-06, Feature updates section, Real-time video translation retirement), https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-relnote-beta-channel and the LiveVideoTranslationEnabled policy docs at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-policies/LiveVideoTranslationEnabled.

### Compatibility / Embedded Runtime (C)

- [C-233] **[P2] Edge 152 `BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed` Group Policy Allows Active WebSocket Connections to Be Disconnected and Restored in a Disconnected State When Entering Back/Forward Cache** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10) adds the `BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed` group policy that controls whether active WebSocket connections are disconnected when a page enters back/forward cache. Previously, an active WebSocket connection prevented the page from being stored in back/forward cache entirely; Edge 152 changes this behavior so WebSockets are disconnected (allowing the page to be cached) and restored in a disconnected state when the user navigates back. The policy is supported on Windows ≥152, macOS ≥152, and Android ≥152; iOS is not supported. For a controlled fixture with an Edge 152 deployment that has `BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed = 1` (allowed) and an open page at `https://example.com/game` that holds an active WebSocket connection to `wss://api.example.com/socket`, assert that navigating away to `https://example.com/page2` and then back via browser back button returns the page to its previous scroll position / DOM state (the page entered BF cache) and the WebSocket connection is now in a closed state (controlled fixture verifying `socket.readyState === WebSocket.CLOSED` after restoration), navigating to the same page WITHOUT the policy allowed (`BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed = 0`) results in a full page reload when navigating back (the WebSocket-prevents-BF-cache behavior is preserved — controlled fixture verifying the WebSocket `onclose` event fires on navigation away), the policy accepts values `1` (allow BF cache for WebSockets — disconnect and cache) and `0` (preserve existing behavior — page is not cached while WebSocket is active), the policy's effect is per-page (a controlled fixture verifying that pages with NO WebSocket connection are unaffected by the policy — they continue to enter BF cache regardless), iOS does not honor the policy (controlled fixture verifying the policy appears as `Not supported on this platform` in the Edge iOS admin UI — the BF-cache-for-WebSocket feature is desktop / Android only), `Chrome 150` controls (no `BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed` policy in Chrome — Edge ships its own policy surface) return `undefined` for the policy in `chrome://policy`, and the policy does not affect `EventSource` connections (controlled fixture verifying that an `EventSource` connection does NOT prevent BF cache entry regardless of the policy — the policy is WebSocket-specific, not all-stream). Source: Edge 152 release notes (152.0.4191.10, 2026-08-06, New policies section, BackForwardCacheForWebSocketsAllowed) and the policy documentation at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-policies/backforwardcacheforwebsocketsallowed.

- [C-234] **[P2] Edge 152 Stable 151.0.4129.72 (Aug 6 2026) Adopts 2-Week Major Release Cadence Distinct From Chrome 153+ 2-Week Cadence** — Edge 152 stable release 151.0.4129.72 (released 2026-08-06) announces that Microsoft Edge is moving to a 2-week major-version release cadence, mirroring (but separately scheduled from) Chrome's 153+ 2-week cadence (W-384 v1.200.0). The Edge cadence is documented at https://blogs.windows.com/msedgedev/2026/06/11/faster-updates-enterprise-friendly-schedule-the-new-microsoft-edge-release-cycle/ (Microsoft Edge Blog, 2026-06-11) and is distinct from Chrome's cadence in vendor (Microsoft vs Google), schedule (Edge 152 stable on 2026-08-06 maps to Edge 153 → ~2026-08-20; Chrome 153 → ~2026-09-02), and enterprise-pending posture (Edge's enterprise-stable channel lags the stable channel by ~3 weeks vs Chrome's ~1 week). For a controlled fixture with an Edge 152 deployment and a documented release-cadence schedule, assert that the gap between Edge 152 stable (151.0.4129.72, 2026-08-06) and the next stable major version is ≤ 16 days (the 2-week cadence contract — controlled fixture verifying the published Edge 153 stable date is between 2026-08-20 and 2026-08-22), Edge's release-cadence announcement page (https://blogs.windows.com/msedgedev/2026/06/11/faster-updates-enterprise-friendly-schedule-the-new-microsoft-edge-release-cycle/) is reachable and contains the 2-week cadence commitment (controlled fixture using `curl --noproxy '*'` verifying the page returns HTTP 200 and contains the literal text "2-week"), the Edge Enterprise update channel policy (`UpdateOnZeroWindowEnabled` — new in 151.0.4129.72 alongside the cadence change) reflects the new cadence (a controlled fixture verifying that background automatic relaunch on zero-window-close respects the new release schedule), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls have no Edge-cadence implications (the Edge cadence is Edge-specific — controlled fixture verifying that Chrome's cadence (per W-384) and Edge's cadence (per C-234) are independently scheduled and do not coincide), and a controlled fixture verifies that Edge's 2-week cadence doubles the per-7-day patch-lag pressure that W-384 estimated at ~$0.4/wk for Chrome — Edge has a similar but independent surface, with the patch-lag window halved from 4 weeks to 2 weeks. Source: Edge 152 release notes (152.0.4191.10, 2026-08-06, Announcements section, Microsoft Edge moving to a 2-week cadence) and the Microsoft Edge Blog announcement at https://blogs.windows.com/msedgedev/2026/06/11/faster-updates-enterprise-friendly-schedule-the-new-microsoft-edge-release-cycle/.

- [C-235] **[P2] Edge 152 `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled` Group Policy Controls Whether Copilot Cowork Browser Agent Can Take Actions on User's Behalf** — Edge 152 (released 2026-08-06 to the beta channel as 152.0.4191.10) ships the `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled` group policy that controls availability of the new Copilot Cowork browser agent feature. Copilot Cowork may perform tasks such as creating tabs, taking screenshots, and interacting with web pages on the user's behalf (the AI-agent-on-behalf-of-user surface). The policy corresponds to the **Allow Cowork to take actions on your behalf** setting in `edge://settings/ai`. The policy applies only when Cowork Browsing is enabled at the tenant level in the M365 admin center. For a controlled fixture with an Edge 152 deployment and Cowork Browsing enabled at the M365 tenant level, assert that `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled = 1` (allowed) enables the Copilot Cowork agent to perform tab-create / screenshot / interact actions on the user's behalf (controlled fixture verifying the agent's actions appear in the Edge activity log with `"actor": "copilot-cowork"` attribution), `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled = 0` (denied) blocks all Copilot Cowork actions — the agent can still read / observe the page (the read-only surface is unaffected) but cannot create tabs, take screenshots, or interact with web pages (controlled fixture verifying that an attempted screenshot action returns `PermissionDeniedError`), the policy requires Cowork Browsing to be enabled at the tenant level (controlled fixture verifying that `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled = 1` with Cowork Browsing disabled at the M365 tenant level still results in no Copilot Cowork actions — the policy is a no-op without the tenant-level opt-in), the policy's setting is reflected in `edge://settings/ai` (controlled fixture verifying that "Allow Cowork to take actions on your behalf" is checked when `CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled = 1` and unchecked when `= 0`), `Chrome 150` / `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no Copilot Cowork — the feature is Edge-specific) return `undefined` for `chrome://policy#CopilotCoworkToolActionsEnabled`, and a controlled fixture verifies that the policy does not affect the user's direct actions (the user can still create tabs, take screenshots, and interact with pages manually — the policy gates only the AI-agent surface, not the human-user surface). Source: Edge 152 release notes (152.0.4191.10, 2026-08-06, Feature updates section, New Copilot Cowork policy) and the policy documentation at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-policies/copilotcoworktoolactionsenabled.
