## New Test Cases Added in v1.215.0 (R332 — August 5 2026, 4h cycle 94)

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-255] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta `CSSPseudoElement` Web API Extends to `::backdrop`, `::scroll-marker`, and `::view-transition`** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) extends the `CSSPseudoElement` Web API to cover the `::backdrop`, `::scroll-marker`, and `::view-transition` pseudo-elements. Previously, `CSSPseudoElement` was defined only for `::after`, `::before`, and `::marker`; Chrome 152 adds support for all three new pseudo-elements via `Element.pseudo(type)`, where `type` is one of `"::backdrop"`, `"::scroll-marker"`, or `"::view-transition"`, returning a `CSSPseudoElement` instance with `.element`, `.type`, `.parent`, and matching `Element.getAnimations()` / `Animation` integration. For a controlled fixture with `<dialog id="d" open><p>x</p></dialog>`, `<div id="anim" style="view-transition-name: foo"></div>`, and `<ul id="list"><li>1</li><li>2</li></ul>` with `list-style-type: scroll-marker`, assert `d.pseudo("::backdrop") instanceof CSSPseudoElement` returns `true` and the returned object's `.type` is `"::backdrop"` (controlled fixture verifies the `.element === d` reference is preserved), `d.pseudo("::backdrop")?.parent === d` (the parent-element reference matches), `anim.pseudo("::view-transition") instanceof CSSPseudoElement` returns `true` (each `Element.pseudo()` call returns a fresh `CSSPseudoElement`, not a singleton — repeat the call twice and verify the second result is also a `CSSPseudoElement`), `CSS.supports("selector(::view-transition)")` returns `true` in Chrome 152 beta, `list.pseudo("::scroll-marker") instanceof CSSPseudoElement` returns `true` only when the `<ul>` has `list-style-type: scroll-marker` (the `::scroll-marker` pseudo only exists on elements that actually generate a scroll-marker), Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249 / Firefox 155 controls (no `CSSPseudoElement` for these pseudo types) return `null` for the same `Element.pseudo("::view-transition")` calls (or the typed `CSSPseudoElement` constructor does not exist), and a controlled fixture verifies that animating an element during a `document.startViewTransition(...)` updates the `.pseudo("::view-transition")` snapshot's effective position (the geometry-aware view transitions contract documented in the Chrome 152 beta release notes). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (CSS and UI section, `CSSPseudoElement` support for `::backdrop`, `::scroll-marker`, and `::view-transition`).
- [B-256] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta Exposes the HTML `autocorrect` Global Attribute to Web Developers** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) exposes the `autocorrect` global HTML attribute, letting web authors control whether autocorrection should be applied to user input in editable elements including `<input>`, `<textarea>`, and contenteditable hosts. The attribute accepts the keyword values `on` (browser-default autocorrection; the default when the attribute is omitted) and `off` (no autocorrection). For a controlled fixture with `<input id="i" type="text" autocorrect="off">` and `<textarea id="t" autocorrect="off"></textarea>`, assert `document.getElementById("i").autocorrect === "off"` (the IDL attribute reflects the content attribute), `document.getElementById("t").autocorrect === "off"` (controlled fixture for `<textarea>`), a controlled fixture with `<div contenteditable autocorrect="on"></div>` returns `.autocorrect === "on"`, a controlled fixture where the attribute is omitted returns `.autocorrect === ""` (the empty string signals "browser default"), programmatically setting `i.autocorrect = "off"` is reflected by `i.getAttribute("autocorrect") === "off"`, an invalid value such as `i.autocorrect = "invalid"` reads back as `"invalid"` but is treated as the browser default (per the HTML spec), the IDL attribute is enumerable on the prototype of every `HTMLInputElement` / `HTMLTextAreaElement` / `HTMLElement` (when contenteditable is set), `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls return `undefined` for `.autocorrect` (the attribute is unknown to those engines), and the formatter / autocorrection UI does not trigger when `autocorrect="off"` is set on a controlled `<input>` field (the on-screen keyboard's autocorrection popup is suppressed). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (CSS and UI section, expose the `autocorrect` global HTML attribute).
- [B-257] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta Media-Element Pseudo-Classes `:playing`, `:paused`, `:seeking`, `:buffering`, `:stalled`, `:muted`, and `:volume-locked` Match `<audio>` / `<video>` Current State** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships the `:playing`, `:paused`, `:seeking`, `:buffering`, `:stalled`, `:muted`, and `:volume-locked` CSS pseudo-classes (Interop 2026 focus area), which match `<audio>` and `<video>` elements based on their current state. For a controlled fixture with `<audio id="a" src="test.mp3"></audio>` and `<video id="v" src="test.mp4" muted></video>`, assert `.matches(":playing")` returns `true` on `#a` while the audio is actively playing (after `a.play()` resolves and the `playing` event fires), `.matches(":paused")` returns `true` on `#a` while the audio is paused (after `a.pause()`), `.matches(":seeking")` returns `true` during the in-flight seek (`a.currentTime = 100` returns within the seek window — use `await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 0))` or wait for the `seeking` event), `.matches(":buffering")` returns `true` while playback is suspended due to insufficient buffer, `.matches(":stalled")` returns `true` while the network is starved, `.matches(":muted")` returns `true` on `#v` (whose `muted` attribute is set on the markup), `.matches(":volume-locked")` returns `true` only when the user agent prevents volume adjustment (e.g. a controlled fixture with `a.volume = 1.0` after `.volume-locked` is forced via `Internals.SetAudioElementVolumeLocked(a, true)` is undefined in userland, so test this on a device where the volume lock UI fires — test plan uses `a.dispatchEvent(new MediaStreamTrackEvent(...))` simulation in a controlled fixture), the same `.matches()` returns `false` on a non-media element (e.g. `<div>` or `<img>`), `CSS.supports("selector(:playing)")` returns `true` in Chrome 152 beta and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Firefox 153 controls (no `:playing` pseudo-class), and the `@keyframes` selector can target `:playing` to apply different animations when the audio is playing vs paused. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (CSS and UI section, Media element pseudo-classes).
- [B-258] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta CSS `window-drag` Property Standardizes the Desktop-App Window-Drag Region Naming and Inheritance** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships the `window-drag` CSS property, which standardizes and renames the existing `app-region` property, changes its value names to `move` and `none`, and adds explicit inheritance behavior. The `window-drag` property lets web content designate regions of an installed desktop web app UI that should behave as draggable window titlebar areas. For a controlled fixture with an installed-ChromeOS / Edge / Chrome PWA that uses `<header style="window-drag: move">header</header>` content plus `<button>click</button>` sibling, assert `getComputedStyle(header).windowDrag === "move"` (the keyword is the computed value, not `drag` from the legacy `app-region`), `getComputedStyle(button).windowDrag === "move"` (the property inherits — `window-drag: inherit` is implicit on descendants, distinct from `app-region: no-drag` which did not inherit by default), `getComputedStyle(body).windowDrag === "none"` (the initial value is `none`, not `auto`), `CSS.supports("window-drag: move")` returns `true` in Chrome 152 beta and `false` in Chrome 151 / Safari 26.6 / Firefox 155 controls (the legacy `app-region` is the surface they support), `CSS.supports("window-drag: drag")` returns `false` in Chrome 152 beta (the legacy `drag` value is no longer accepted), `CSS.supports("window-drag: no-drag")` returns `false` in Chrome 152 beta (the legacy `no-drag` is replaced by `none`), and a controlled fixture verifying that `window-drag: none` on a parent overrides the inheritance on `<button>` (an explicit `none` cascades and does not inherit, so a `<button style="window-drag: none">click</button>` inside a `<header style="window-drag: move">` does NOT trigger window drag on click). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (CSS and UI section, `window-drag` CSS property).

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-430] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta CPU Performance API Graduates to Stable With `CpuPerformanceTierOverride` Enterprise Policy** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) graduates the **CPU Performance API** (previously experimental in Chrome 145 per P-164 / P-133 mentions in CHANGELOG) to a stable Web API. The API exposes `navigator.cpuPerformanceTier` (`"low"`, `"medium"`, `"high"`) and `navigator.cpuPerformance` (with details); Chrome 152 adds the **`CpuPerformanceTierOverride`** enterprise policy that lets administrators override the reported tier for managed devices. For a controlled fixture in Chrome 152 beta, assert `navigator.cpuPerformanceTier` returns one of `"low"`, `"medium"`, or `"high"` (and is **not** `undefined`, distinguishing stable from experimental), the same property in Chrome 151 / Safari 26.6 / Firefox 155 controls returns `undefined` (no CPU Performance API on those engines), a controlled fixture verifies that `navigator.cpuPerformanceTier` correlates with reported device class (a known-low-end device returns `"low"`, a known-high-end device returns `"high"`, both stable across reloads), and the value can be overridden via the `CpuPerformanceTierOverride` enterprise policy (controlled fixture on a managed-device profile with policy set to `"high"` returns `"high"` regardless of actual device class). Additionally, when combined with the Compute Pressure API (`navigator.computePressure`), the games should be able to lower effects when tier is `"low"` and pressure is `"critical"`. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, CPU Performance API) and the CPU Performance API Explainer at https://github.com/w3c/compute-pressure/blob/main/explainer.md.
- [W-431] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta Connection Allowlists Response Header Restricts Fetch and Other Web Platform API Connections to Authorized Endpoints** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships Connection Allowlists, a response-header-controlled origin allowlist for connections initiated through the Fetch API or other web-platform APIs (subresource fetches, `WebSocket`, `EventSource`, XHR, etc.). The server distributes an authorized endpoint list via an HTTP response header; before establishing any connection, Chrome evaluates the destination against this allowlist. Connections to verified endpoints are permitted; connections that fail to match are blocked at the network layer. For a controlled fixture served over HTTPS with `Connection-Allowlist: https://api.example.com https://cdn.example.com` set on `https://example.com/page.html`, assert `fetch("https://api.example.com/data")` from inside `page.html` succeeds (the destination is in the allowlist), `fetch("https://attacker.example.com/payload")` returns a controlled network error (the destination is NOT in the allowlist and the connection is refused), `fetch("/relative-path")` is allowed (same-origin requests remain permitted — controlled fixture verifies the relative URL resolves to `https://example.com/relative-path` which equals the document origin), `WebSocket("wss://api.example.com/socket")` succeeds (WebSocket connections honor the allowlist the same way as Fetch), `WebSocket("wss://attacker.example.com/socket")` fails with a controlled network error, the allowlist is enforced on the worker global scope (a controlled `new Worker("worker.js")` from `page.html` cannot `fetch("https://attacker.example.com/payload")` — the allowlist inherits into the worker), the allowlist header is per-page (a controlled fixture served WITHOUT the header allows all connections, demonstrating the opt-in nature), the header accepts a space-separated list of origins, `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no Connection Allowlists support) silently allow all connections regardless of the header (the header is unknown to them and ignored), and the allowlist does not affect the implicit same-origin document navigation (clicking `<a href="/internal">` continues to work; only cross-origin resource fetches are gated). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, Connection Allowlists) and the Connection Allowlists proposal explainer at https://github.com/WICG/connection-allowlists.

### Performance (P)

- [P-186] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta CSS Color 5 `alpha()` Function Takes an Origin Color and Modifies Only the Alpha Channel for Relative Transparency** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships the CSS Color 5 `alpha()` function, which takes an origin color and modifies only the alpha channel — letting web developers set transparency relative to an existing color value. The function accepts a color and an optional alpha percentage: `alpha(<color>, <alpha-percentage>)` returns the color with the alpha channel replaced by the new percentage. For a controlled fixture with `<style>.a { color: rgb(255, 0, 0); background: alpha(currentColor, 50%); }</style><div class="a" id="a"></div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(a).backgroundColor` returns `rgba(255, 0, 0, 0.5)` (the `red` color with alpha `0.5`), `alpha()` works with named colors (`alpha(rebeccapurple, 25%)` returns `rgba(102, 51, 153, 0.25)`), `alpha()` works with hex values (`alpha(#ff0000, 50%)` returns `rgba(255, 0, 0, 0.5)`), `alpha()` works with `color-mix()` (`background: alpha(color-mix(in oklab, red, blue), 80%)` returns the mixed color with alpha `0.8`), `alpha()` can be combined with the existing `color-mix(in oklab, red, alpha(currentColor, 50%))` and similar nested expressions, `CSS.supports("color: alpha(rgb(0, 0, 0), 50%)")` returns `true` in Chrome 152 beta and `false` in Chrome 151 / Safari 26.6 / Firefox 155 controls (the `alpha()` function is new to CSS Color 5 and not yet supported in stable Chrome), `getComputedStyle` reports the resolved color via `Color` IDL value (controlled fixture using `element.computedStyleMap().get("background-color")` returns a `CSSColorValue` with the alpha-modified color — distinguishable from the original color), the function accepts `from <color>` syntax variant: `alpha(from red alpha 0.5)` returns `rgba(255, 0, 0, 0.5)` (the `from` keyword lets the alpha be replaced without specifying the RGB channels), an `alpha()` whose origin color is invalid (e.g. `alpha(not-a-color, 50%)`) makes the whole property invalid (the rule is dropped, no fallback), and `alpha()` works with `currentColor` (controlled fixture where the page text color is `currentColor` and the background uses `alpha(currentColor, 25%)` returns the text color with 25% alpha). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (CSS and UI section, Relative alpha colors — CSS Color 5 alpha() function).
- [P-187] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta `OpaqueRange` Represents Live Text Spans Within Form-Control Values for Range-Like Operations** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships `OpaqueRange`, an object that represents a live span of text within a form control's value (a `<textarea>` or text-based `<input>`), so web developers can work with value text using range-like APIs. `OpaqueRange` supports operations such as `getBoundingClientRect()`, `getClientRects()`, integration with the CSS Custom Highlight API for inline suggestions / highlights / anchored popovers, and preserves encapsulation by exposing only value offsets (returning `null` for `startContainer` and `endContainer`). For a controlled fixture with `<textarea id="t">hello world</textarea>`, assert `const range = t.createRangeReference(0, 5)` returns an `OpaqueRange` (a controlled `console.log(range.constructor.name)` outputs `"OpaqueRange"` in Chrome 152 beta and `undefined` in controls), the range's `getBoundingClientRect()` returns a `DOMRect` whose width is approximately the pixel width of the `"hello"` substring at line 1 of the textarea (within a fixed pixel tolerance — the rect's top is the textarea's first line top), `range.getClientRects()` returns a list of `DOMRect` instances (one rect per line when the range spans multiple lines), `range.startOffset === 0` and `range.endOffset === 5` (the offsets reflect the value-text positions), `range.startContainer` and `range.endContainer` are `null` (the encapsulation contract — the range exposes only offsets, not DOM nodes), the range is live: a controlled fixture where the textarea value is mutated (`t.value = "hi world"; t.dispatchEvent(new Event("input"))`) updates the range's effective text without invalidating the range object (the range's `getBoundingClientRect()` returns the rect for `"hi wo"` after the mutation), `range.cloneRange()` returns another `OpaqueRange` with the same offsets (controlled fixture verifies the clone is independent of the original — modifying the original's offsets does not affect the clone), the range integrates with the CSS Custom Highlight API (`new Highlight(range); document.cssHighlights.add(highlight)` plus `::highlight(my-highlight) { background: yellow }` styles the range's text), `Safari 26.6` / `Firefox 155` controls (no `OpaqueRange`) return a different type for `t.createRangeReference(0, 5)` — either `undefined` (the method does not exist) or a plain object, and the size of the value-text that triggers an OOM is bounded (a controlled fixture with a 50 MB textarea value can create an `OpaqueRange` without crashing — heap delta ≤ 4x the parsed-string size, matching the v1.208.0 B-236 Rust XML parser heap bound). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, OpaqueRange) and the Custom Highlight API integration in the CSS Custom Highlight API spec.

Adjacent-but-distinct surfaces verified: B-255 `::view-transition` (CSS pseudo-element) is distinct from the existing `document.startViewTransition()` CSS API (a different surface — the existing API is the controller; this new surface is the `CSSPseudoElement` representation of an in-flight transition); B-256 `autocorrect` is distinct from the existing `autocapitalize` / `autocomplete` HTML attributes (different autocontrol dimension); B-257 `:playing` is distinct from `:hover` / `:focus` / `:active` interaction pseudo-classes; B-258 `window-drag` is the standardized replacement for `app-region` (same surface, renamed — controlled fixtures verify the legacy `app-region: drag` is rejected in Chrome 152 beta); W-430 is the stable contract of the P-164/P-133 experimental mentions (Chrome 152 first ships it stable — the v1.214.0 master never had a body because no stable version existed yet); W-431 Connection Allowlists is distinct from CSP `connect-src` (CSP is per-resource policy, Connection Allowlists is server-opted-in origin allowlist) and from Permissions Policy `direct-sockets-private` (v1.209.0 C-226); P-186 CSS `alpha()` is distinct from `color-mix()` (CSS Color 5) and from `oklch()` — `alpha()` is the alpha-only modifier; P-187 `OpaqueRange` is distinct from the existing `Range` API (existing `Range` operates on DOM nodes with `startContainer` / `endContainer`, `OpaqueRange` is a value-only encapsulation by design); neither appears in the existing Security / Performance / Compatibility sub-trees.

## New Test Cases Added in v1.216.0 (R333 — August 6 2026, 4h cycle 95)

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-259] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta Unframed Display Mode for Isolated Web Apps Removes Window Borders and Title Bars, Gated by `DefaultWindowManagementSetting` Policy** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) lets an installed IWA opt into the unframed display mode (CSS `display-mode: unframed`), which removes standard window borders and title bars and supports custom branding and menu hierarchies. The behavior is controlled by the `DefaultWindowManagementSetting`, `WindowManagementAllowedForUrls`, and `WindowManagementBlockedForUrls` enterprise policies. For a controlled fixture running inside an IWA shell that opts into `display-mode: unframed` via its manifest, assert that `matchMedia('(display-mode: unframed)').matches === true` when the IWA is in unframed mode (the media query is the CSS-side contract — the same media query that the W3C Display Modes spec defines for PWA display-mode), the OS chrome (window borders, title bars) is absent in unframed mode (controlled fixture verifies the window's outer-bounds rectangle equals the inner-bounds rectangle — no OS-chrome height delta), the IWA can render a custom menu hierarchy via a `<button>` element positioned at `top: 0, left: 0` (the absence of OS chrome means the IWA's UI starts at `y: 0` of the window, not `y: OS-chrome-height`), `WindowManagementAllowedForUrls` overrides the default to allow the URL to enter unframed mode without user prompt (the policy is per-origin — when set, the user gesture for window management is bypassed), `WindowManagementBlockedForUrls` blocks the URL from entering unframed mode regardless of any other permission state (the policy is a hard override — even an IWA installed with the proper manifest is denied unframed mode), `DefaultWindowManagementSetting` controls the baseline behavior (prompt / allow / block) for URLs not covered by the per-origin allow/block lists (the same three-tier policy composition as C-232 sub apps), Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no unframed display mode for IWA) return `matchMedia('(display-mode: unframed)').matches === false` for any URL (the display-mode is unframed-specific to Chrome's IWA surface), and a controlled fixture verifies that toggling between `display-mode: standalone` and `display-mode: unframed` in the manifest updates the `matchMedia` result on next launch (the media query is bound to the install-time manifest — runtime switching requires re-install), demonstrating the manifest-bound contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, Unframed display mode for Isolated Web Apps).
- [B-260] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta Notification Attribution for PWAs on macOS Routes PWA Notifications to the PWA's Own Name and Icon in Notification Center** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) attributes notifications from installed Progressive Web Apps (PWAs) on macOS to the PWA itself — using its own name and icon in Notification Center — rather than to Google Chrome. As a side effect, the `requireInteraction` field is no longer supported for PWA notifications on macOS because notification persistence is now controlled per-app by macOS settings, and the Badging API requires notification permissions for the app badge to display. For a controlled fixture with a PWA installed on macOS that calls `serviceWorker.showNotification('Hello', {body: 'World'})` (or `registration.showNotification` from the service worker), assert that the resulting notification in macOS Notification Center is attributed to the PWA's display name (not "Google Chrome") and shows the PWA's manifest icon (not the Chrome browser icon), `registration.showNotification('Hello', {requireInteraction: true, ...})` ignores the `requireInteraction: true` flag (the field is silently dropped — macOS controls persistence via its own per-app settings), `navigator.setAppBadge(1)` requires that the PWA has been granted notification permission (the Badging API is now permission-gated; without `Notification.permission === 'granted'`, `setAppBadge` rejects with `NotAllowedError`), an MDM configuration profile is needed to pre-grant notification permission for the PWA's bundle ID (the `NotificationsAllowedForUrls` Chrome policy pre-grants for Chrome but a separate macOS MDM profile is needed for the PWA's specific bundle ID — without both, the PWA's notifications fall back to the Chrome attribution), the PWA's notifications still respect `Notification.permission` requests via `Notification.requestPermission()` (the permission UI is the system permission dialog, not Chrome's), Safari 26.6 (no Chrome PWA installation attribution on macOS — Safari uses its own add-to-dock flow) and Firefox 154 controls (no Chrome PWA installation) report `Notification.permission` per their own implementations without the Chrome PWA attribution path, and a controlled fixture verifies that uninstalling the PWA removes its notifications from Notification Center (notification lifetime is tied to the PWA install, not Chrome), demonstrating the per-app identity contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, Notification attribution for PWAs on macOS).

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-432] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta `getDisplayMedia` Adds `audioPreference` Attribute to `DisplayMediaStreamOptions` for Audio-Capture Hint** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) adds an `audioPreference` attribute to the `DisplayMediaStreamOptions` dictionary used in `navigator.mediaDevices.getDisplayMedia()`, which lets web applications signal to the browser that audio sharing is preferred along with video capture. For a controlled fixture with `navigator.mediaDevices.getDisplayMedia({video: true, audioPreference: "prefer"})` invoked under a user-gesture activation and a mocked `MediaDevices` implementation, assert that the resulting `MediaStream` either includes an audio track (the user selected a tab/screen with audio and the preference was honored) or has no audio track but the call succeeds with `video: true, audio: false` effective capture (when the chosen surface has no audio, the preference is a hint not a hard requirement — the browser may elect to capture video-only rather than reject), `audioPreference: "deny"` produces a `MediaStream` with no audio track regardless of the chosen surface's audio capability (the user-gesture capture flow respects the preference; a tab/screen with audio is still captured without audio when the preference is `deny`), the default value of `audioPreference` (omitted) preserves the legacy behavior (no preference change — the capture flow follows the `audio: boolean` flag), `DisplayMediaStreamOptions.audioPreference` is enumerable in Object.keys and reflects the string value passed in, `audioPreference` accepts only `"prefer"` / `"deny"` (unknown strings either fall back to the default or throw a `TypeError` per the dictionary's string-enum contract), Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no `audioPreference` attribute on `DisplayMediaStreamOptions`) treat the attribute as an ignored extra property (the call succeeds and the option is silently dropped, demonstrating no breakage on cross-browser implementations), and a controlled fixture verifies that `audioPreference: "prefer"` is honored on user-facing surfaces that have audio (e.g. a tab with playing media) versus ignored on surfaces without audio (e.g. a window with no audio track), demonstrating the hint-not-mandate contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Media section, `audioPreferred` capture in getDisplayMedia API).
- [W-433] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta WebGPU Adds Optional `subgroup-size-control` Feature for Explicit Subgroup Size Selection in Compute Shaders** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) adds an optional GPU feature `subgroup-size-control` to `GPUAdapter.features` and `GPUDevice.features` that lets WGSL compute shaders explicitly set the subgroup size via `enable subgroups; enable subgroupsizecontrol; override-subgroup-size: 4;` (or 8 / 16 / 32 / 64). For a controlled fixture with a WebGPU-capable adapter that supports `subgroup-size-control`, assert that `adapter.features.has("subgroup-size-control") === true` when the underlying hardware exposes the capability (the feature is opt-in via `requiredFeatures: ["subgroup-size-control"]` on `requestDevice()`), a WGSL shader with `enable subgroups; enable subgroupsizecontrol; override-subgroup-size: 32;` compiles and a `workgroupSize(32)` `subgroupAdd` returns the expected per-subgroup reduction result (the `subgroupAdd` reduction honors the explicitly requested size of 32 even if the underlying hardware's native subgroup size is 64 or 128), `adapter.limits.subgroupMinSize` and `adapter.limits.subgroupMaxSize` reflect the supported bounds (e.g. `subgroupMinSize: 4, subgroupMaxSize: 64` on Vulkan-class hardware; the chosen `override-subgroup-size` must be within this range), a WGSL shader that uses `override-subgroup-size` without `enable subgroupsizecontrol` fails WGSL validation (the `override-subgroup-size` attribute is gated by the `subgroupsizecontrol` enable-extension), a `requestDevice({requiredFeatures: ["subgroup-size-control"]})` call on an adapter without that feature rejects with `GPUDevice` creation failure (the feature is a true capability, not silently granted), Safari 26.6 / Safari TP 249 controls (subgroups exist per W-428 but `subgroup-size-control` is Chrome 152 beta only) reject the `subgroupsizecontrol` enable-extension as unknown, and a controlled fixture verifies that the runtime-selected subgroup size affects `subgroupBallot` / `subgroupBroadcast` semantics (a 32-subgroup broadcast returns a 32-lane ballot; a 64-subgroup broadcast returns a 64-lane ballot — the size is observable in `subgroupSize`). Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, WebGPU Subgroup Size Control).
- [W-434] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta Window Shape API Lets ChromeOS Isolated Web Apps Set Non-Rectangular Window Geometry via `window.chromeos.isolatedWebApp.setShape`** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) ships the Window Shape API on ChromeOS, accessed via `window.chromeos.isolatedWebApp.setShape(rects)`, which lets an installed Isolated Web App (IWA) customize the non-rectangular shape of the window by passing an array of `DOMRect` (or `{x, y, width, height}`) rectangles describing the visible regions of the window. For a controlled fixture running inside an IWA shell on ChromeOS, assert that `window.chromeos.isolatedWebApp?.setShape([{x: 0, y: 0, width: 200, height: 100}, {x: 250, y: 0, width: 100, height: 50}])` succeeds when the window is in the unframed display mode (the API is gated by the unframed mode and by the `window-management` permission; the call returns a resolved `Promise<void>`), `setShape([])` resets the window to a single rectangle matching the window's current bounding box (the empty array is the explicit reset — useful for toggling custom-shape off), `setShape` rejects with `NotAllowedError` when the window is in framed display mode (the API requires unframed display mode — framed windows cannot have a non-rectangular shape because they have OS chrome), `setShape` rejects with `NotAllowedError` when the IWA has not been granted the `window-management` permission (the API is permission-gated per the Permissions Policy `window-management` directive), a `setShape` call with rectangles outside the window's bounding box clamps to the bounding box (a `{x: -100, y: 0, width: 500, height: 200}` call with a 200×200 window is clamped to `{x: 0, y: 0, width: 200, height: 200}` — no overflow), `setShape` reflects in the window's `Window.boundingBox` / `matchMedia('(display-mode: unframed)')` queries (the window remains unframed and the bounding box tracks the union of the rectangles), Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no `window.chromeos.isolatedWebApp` namespace, no Window Shape API) return `undefined` for `window.chromeos` (the API is ChromeOS-only and IWA-only), and a controlled fixture verifies that `setShape` is observable in the resulting window's screen-capture shape (a window with two rectangles is captured as two regions — the area between them is transparent / not captured), demonstrating the visual-geometry contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, Window Shape API).

### Performance (P)

- [P-188] **[P1] Chrome 152 Beta `MediaCapabilities.decodingInfo` Adds `encryptionScheme` Attribute to `KeySystemTrackConfiguration` for EME Encryption Scheme Capability Query** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) extends the `KeySystemTrackConfiguration` dictionary used in `navigator.mediaCapabilities.decodingInfo()` with an `encryptionScheme` attribute that lets web applications query whether a specific encryption scheme such as `'cenc'` (AES-CTR) or `'cbcs'` (AES-CBCS with subsample encryption) is supported by the underlying Encrypted Media Extensions (EME) implementation. For a controlled fixture with a synthetic `MediaCapabilities` instance, assert that `navigator.mediaCapabilities.decodingInfo({type: "media", video: {contentType: "video/mp4; codecs="avc1.42E01E"", encryptionScheme: "cenc", robustness: ""}, keySystemConfiguration: {keySystem: "com.widevine.alpha", videoCapabilities: [{contentType: "video/mp4; codecs="avc1.42E01E"", encryptionScheme: "cenc"}]}})` returns a `MediaCapabilitiesInfo` whose `.supported` reflects the EME implementation's actual `cenc` capability (the fixture asserts the returned value's `.supported` boolean matches what Chrome's EME Widevine implementation actually reports — true for Widevine-cleared fixture, false for unsupported configurations), `navigator.mediaCapabilities.decodingInfo({... encryptionScheme: "cbcs" ...})` returns the corresponding `cbcs` capability and a separate `.supported` boolean (the `cenc` vs `cbcs` distinction is preserved — fixtures that ask only for `cenc` must not silently return the `cbcs` capability and vice versa), the dictionary reports `KeySystemTrackConfiguration.encryptionScheme` as part of its serialization (the attribute is enumerable and reflects the value passed in), `navigator.mediaCapabilities.decodingInfo({... encryptionScheme: "foo" ...})` for an unknown encryption scheme string returns a `MediaCapabilitiesInfo` with `.supported === false` and a `MediaCapabilitiesException` is thrown when the dictionary fails validation (unknown values fall back to not-supported), Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no `encryptionScheme` attribute on `KeySystemTrackConfiguration`) treat the same dictionary as if `encryptionScheme` were absent (the call succeeds and returns the same value as without the attribute, demonstrating graceful degradation), and a controlled fixture verifies that omitting `encryptionScheme` (backward-compatible default) yields the same `.supported` boolean as omitting `robustness` (the previous attribute for capability scoping), demonstrating that `encryptionScheme` is an additional dimension rather than a replacement. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Media section, `MediaCapabilities encryptionScheme`).

### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-472] **[P0] Chrome 152 Beta Removes the Private Aggregation API Following the Third-Party Cookies Maintained Announcement, Affecting Shared Storage and Protected Audience Call Sites** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) removes the Private Aggregation API following Chrome's announcement that the current approach to third-party cookies will be maintained. The Private Aggregation API was only exposed through the Shared Storage and Protected Audience APIs, which are also planned to be deprecation-removed. For a controlled fixture with a Shared Storage or Protected Audience worklet, assert that `privateAggregation.contributeToHistogram(...)` (the previously-available worklet-scoped function) is now `undefined` (the API is removed from the global scope — worklet scripts that previously called it now throw `ReferenceError`), Shared Storage `Worklet` scripts that call `privateAggregation.contributeToHistogram({bucket, value})` fail at parse / script-evaluation time with `ReferenceError: privateAggregation is not defined` (the removal is at the script-eval level — the worklet does not load), Protected Audience `AuctionWorklet` scripts that previously called `privateAggregation.contributeToHistogram` similarly fail at evaluation (the API is not polyfilled back), `navigator.sharedStorage?.worklet?.addModule('...')` still resolves for backward compatibility but the loaded worklet no longer has access to `privateAggregation` (the Shared Storage surface remains, but the aggregation primitive is gone — call sites that depended on aggregation must migrate to a different telemetry channel), `navigator.runAdAuction({...})` Protected Audience calls that previously expected aggregation results from the auction's `reportResult` no longer receive aggregation results (the `reportResult` callback can still run, but `forDebuggingOnly.privateAggregation.contributeToHistogram` inside it is `undefined`), Chrome 151 / Chrome 150 controls (Private Aggregation still available before removal) expose `privateAggregation` in Shared Storage and Protected Audience worklets — running the same fixture against Chrome 151 returns the aggregation result, demonstrating the removal contract, Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no Shared Storage / Protected Audience — those APIs are Chromium-only Privacy Sandbox APIs) return `undefined` for `navigator.sharedStorage` and `navigator.runAdAuction` (the APIs do not exist outside Chrome), and a controlled fixture verifies that the Shared Storage `selectURL` and Protected Audience auction flows still run and return their non-aggregation outputs (the auction logic is intact — only the aggregation primitive is removed), demonstrating the targeted removal contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Deprecations and removals section, Remove Private Aggregation API).

### Compatibility / Embedded Runtime (C)

- [C-232] **[P2] Chrome 152 Beta Isolated Web Apps (IWA) Sub Apps Let Multiple Apps Share a Single IWA Installation With Distinct Identities and Enterprise Policies** — Chrome 152 beta (released 2026-07-30 to the beta channel) lets developers create multiple sub apps under a single Isolated Web App (IWA) installation via the `DefaultSubAppsWithoutPromptsSetting`, `SubAppsWithoutPromptsAllowedForOrigins`, and `SubAppsWithoutPromptsBlockedForOrigins` enterprise policies. For a controlled fixture with an installed IWA bundle that declares two sub apps in its manifest, assert that each sub app appears in the OS desktop shelf with its own distinct name and icon (the parent IWA's identity is shared, but each sub app has its own OS integration — the icon is sourced from the sub app's manifest, not the parent), a sub app can declare its own file-type association (e.g. `.foo`) and the OS routes that file type to the sub app rather than the parent IWA (the file handler is per-sub-app, not global to the IWA), `SubAppsWithoutPromptsAllowedForOrigins` permits an origin to install sub apps without a per-app user prompt (the policy is per-origin — when set, the prompt for the listed origin is suppressed), `SubAppsWithoutPromptsBlockedForOrigins` denies sub app installation from a listed origin (the policy blocks installation regardless of any other permission state), `DefaultSubAppsWithoutPromptsSetting` controls the default behavior (prompt / allow / block) for sub apps not covered by the per-origin allow/block lists (the three-tier policy composition: per-origin overrides default which overrides the user-prompt baseline), sub app installation calls `navigator.subApps?.add({name, icon, ...})` (the proposed Web API) and resolves with a `SubApp` instance when the parent IWA has been granted the permission (the `subApps` API is permission-gated), Safari 26.6 / Firefox 154 controls (no IWA / no sub apps concept) return `undefined` for `navigator.subApps` (the API does not exist outside Chrome's IWA surface), and a controlled fixture verifies that uninstalling the parent IWA removes all sub apps (sub app lifecycle is tied to the parent — no orphan sub apps are left in the OS shelf), demonstrating the install-tree contract. Source: Chrome 152 beta release notes, https://developer.chrome.com/blog/chrome-152-beta/ (Web APIs section, Isolated Web Apps (IWA) Sub apps).

## New Test Cases Added in v1.214.0 (R331 — August 4 2026, 4h cycle 93)

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-249] **[P1] Safari TP 249 CSS `attr()` Function Resolves Live CSS Custom Properties and Type-Modifier Conversions** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds CSS `attr()` function support (CSS Values Level 5, commit 316672@main, bug 173992627). `attr()` lets any property accept the value of an HTML attribute, with optional type-modifier conversions (`attr(data-x type(<color>))`, `attr(data-num type(<number>))`, `attr(data-len type(<length>))`). For a controlled fixture with `<style>.x::before { content: attr(data-label); } .y { background-color: attr(data-bg type(<color>), red); width: attr(data-w type(<length>), 100px); }</style><div class="x" data-label="hello"></div><div class="y" data-bg="#ff0000" data-w="250px"></div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(divX, '::before').content === '"hello"'` (the literal text from `data-label`), `getComputedStyle(divY).backgroundColor === "rgb(255, 0, 0)"` (the color parsed from `data-bg="#ff0000"`), `getComputedStyle(divY).width === "250px"` (the length parsed from `data-w="250px"`), the fallback value (e.g. `, red`) is used when the attribute is missing (controlled fixture with `<div class="y" style="width: attr(data-w, 200px)"></div>` and no `data-w` attribute returns width `200px`), `CSS.supports("background-color: attr(data-bg type(<color>))")` returns `true` in Safari TP 249 and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (no CSS `attr()` function support on properties other than `content`), and the type-modifier `<color>` rejects malformed values (controlled fixture with `data-bg="not-a-color"` falls back to the fallback value, not throwing). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `attr()` function, commit 316672@main).
- [B-250] **[P2] Safari TP 249 `outline-offset: inset` Pulls the Outline Inside the Border Box, Reversing the Default Outward Offset** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds `outline-offset: inset` (commit 316677@main, bug 180547604). By default, `outline-offset` accepts only positive lengths that push the outline **outward** from the border edge; `inset` is a keyword that pulls the outline **inward** so it renders inside the element's border box, useful for focus rings that should not enlarge the visual bounding box. For a controlled fixture with `<style>.x { outline: 2px solid blue; outline-offset: inset; padding: 8px; border: 4px solid black; }</style><div class="x" id="t">test</div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(t).outlineOffset === "inset"` (the keyword is the computed value, not a px), the rendered outline appears **inside** the border box (the visible outline pixels are between the inner border edge and the padding edge, not outside the border), the element's outer bounding box (used for hit-testing and overflow calculations) does not grow by 2x the outline width (a sibling element with `margin-left: 0` is not pushed further away), the legacy positive-length `outline-offset: 2px` still pushes outward as before (regression check), `CSS.supports("outline-offset: inset")` returns `true` in Safari TP 249 and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls, and an animation between `outline-offset: inset` and `outline-offset: 2px` interpolates correctly (the outline visually migrates from inside to outside the border). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `outline-offset: inset`, commit 316677@main).
- [B-251] **[P1] Safari TP 249 CSS `@function` Custom Functions Rule Lets a Stylesheet Define Reusable Typed CSS Functions** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds support for the `@function` custom functions rule (CSS @function, commit 316671@main, bug 181625123). `@function` lets a stylesheet author declare a reusable typed CSS function (with parameter types and a result type) that can be called from any property value. For a controlled fixture with `<style>@function --shade(--c <color>, --p <number>) returns <color> { result: color-mix(in oklab, var(--c), black calc(100% - var(--p))); } .x { background: --shade(red, 30%); }</style><div class="x" id="t"></div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(t).backgroundColor` returns the computed mixed color of `red` and `black` at the 30% ratio (a controlled pixel sample from the rendered element matches the expected `color-mix(in oklab, red 30%, black 70%)` value), `@function` declarations appear in the stylesheet's parsed rules (the `@function` at-rule itself is parsed and not silently dropped), calling an `@function` with wrong argument types (e.g. `--shade(red, blue)` where the second arg should be `<number>`) is rejected by the parser (the `.x { background: ... }` rule with wrong types is dropped, and a controlled fixture where the type matches uses the fallback), `CSSFunctionRule` is exposed in the CSSOM (controlled fixture with `document.styleSheets[0].cssRules` contains a `CSSFunctionRule` for the `@function`), and `@function` rules do not interfere with the cascade (they don't accidentally apply to selectors, only enable a callable identifier). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `@function` custom functions rule, commit 316671@main).
- [B-252] **[P1] Safari TP 249 CSS `if()` Function Returns a Value Based on a Style Query Condition Without Custom Selectors** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds support for the CSS `if()` function (CSS Conditional Values, commit 316813@main, bug 181806268). `if()` lets a property value conditionally resolve based on a style query — for example, `width: if(style(--w: 200px): var(--w); else: 100px)` returns `var(--w)` if the inline custom property `--w` is declared as `200px`, otherwise the fallback `100px`. For a controlled fixture with `<style>.x { --w: 250px; width: if(style(--w: 200px): var(--w); else: 100px); } .y { width: if(style(--w: 200px): var(--w); else: 100px); }</style><div class="x" id="x"></div><div class="y" id="y"></div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(x).width === "250px"` (the `--w` value is used even though it does not equal `200px`, because the `if()` condition passes when `--w` is **any** value with the matching custom-property name — verify the actual semantic in the controlled fixture by adjusting the registered property definition), `getComputedStyle(y).width === "100px"` (the fallback when `--w` is not declared on `.y`), `CSS.supports("width: if(style(--w: 200px): 100px; else: 200px)")` returns `true` in Safari TP 249 and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls, the `if()` function can be nested (controlled fixture with `width: if(style(--a: 1): if(style(--b: 2): 300px; else: 200px); else: 100px)` resolves the nested condition correctly), and an `if()` whose `else:` branch is omitted returns the property's initial value when the condition is false. Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `if()` function, commit 316813@main).
- [B-253] **[P2] Safari TP 249 CSS `calc-mix()` Mixes Two Length Values at a Ratio Without a Color Space** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds support for the CSS `calc-mix()` function (CSS Mix function, commit 316847@main, bug 181839626). `calc-mix()` linearly interpolates two values at a ratio — `calc-mix(20%, 10px, 30px)` returns `14px` (20% of the way from 10px to 30px). It works on length, percentage, number, angle, time, and frequency types, distinct from `color-mix()` (which is color-space-aware) and `calc()` (which performs arithmetic). For a controlled fixture with `<style>.a { width: calc-mix(50%, 100px, 300px); } .b { width: calc-mix(0%, 100px, 300px); } .c { width: calc-mix(100%, 100px, 300px); }</style><div class="a" id="a"></div><div class="b" id="b"></div><div class="c" id="c"></div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(a).width === "200px"` (midpoint between 100 and 300 at 50%), `getComputedStyle(b).width === "100px"` (the start value at 0%), `getComputedStyle(c).width === "300px"` (the end value at 100%), `calc-mix(20%, 10px, 30px)` returns `14px` (linear interpolation), `CSS.supports("width: calc-mix(50%, 100px, 300px)")` returns `true` in Safari TP 249 and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls, and `calc-mix()` works with mixed types where one operand is a percentage and the other is a length (e.g. `calc-mix(50%, 50%, 200px)` resolves to a context-dependent length). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `calc-mix()`, commit 316847@main).
- [B-254] **[P2] Safari TP 249 `justify-self` Now Applies to Block-Level Boxes, Not Just Grid / Flex Items** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) extends `justify-self` to apply to block-level boxes (commit 317212@main, bug 181926245). Previously `justify-self` only applied to Grid items and Flex items; it now applies to any block-level box whose containing block is a block container with a writing mode that supports inline-axis self-alignment. This makes `justify-self: center` (and `start`, `end`, `self-start`, `self-end`, `left`, `right`, `stretch`, etc.) work on plain block elements to align themselves within their containing block. For a controlled fixture with `<style>body { width: 600px; } .x { width: 200px; justify-self: center; background: blue; }</style><div class="x" id="x">test</div>`, assert `getComputedStyle(x).justifySelf === "center"` (the property is computed on a block-level box, not ignored), the rendered element's left edge is at approximately `200px` from the body's left edge (centered: `(600 - 200) / 2 = 200`), a controlled fixture with `justify-self: end` aligns the block to the right edge of its containing block (left edge at `400px`), `justify-self: start` and `justify-self: stretch` (the default) behave as expected, `CSS.supports("justify-self: center")` is `true` in all browsers, but the controlled fixture `<div style="width: 200px; justify-self: center"></div>` only **visually centers** in Safari TP 249 / Chrome 150 (where block-level box support exists) and not in Safari 26.6 (where block-level box support is missing for `justify-self`), and `align-self` (the cross-axis variant) gets the same block-level box support. Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (CSS New Features section, `justify-self` on block-level boxes, commit 317212@main).

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-429] **[P2] Safari TP 249 `DOMMatrix` and `IntersectionObserver` Reject Relative-Length Values Like `1em` and `100%`** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) fixes an issue where `DOMMatrix` and `IntersectionObserver` accepted relative-length units like `em`, `rem`, `ex`, `ch`, and `%` in property values where only absolute-length units are valid (commit 316532@main, bug 181453666). The fix enforces absolute-length-only parsing: `DOMMatrix.fromMatrix({...})` and `IntersectionObserver` root-margin / `IntersectionObserver` thresholds now reject `em`, `rem`, `ex`, `ch`, `%`, `vw`, `vh`, `vmin`, `vmax`, and similar relative units (these units were silently accepted before but produced undefined rendering behavior). For a controlled fixture using `new IntersectionObserver(callback, { root: null, rootMargin: "10px 1em 10px 1em" })`, assert the constructor throws a `SyntaxError` (or returns an observer whose `rootMargin` is `0px` — controlled check against the spec-compliant behavior), `new DOMMatrix("1em")` throws `SyntaxError` (a controlled fixture with `new DOMMatrix("10px")` succeeds), a controlled fixture with `IntersectionObserver({rootMargin: "10%"})` throws, `IntersectionObserver({rootMargin: "10px 20px 30px 40px"})` succeeds and the observer's `rootMargin === "10px 20px 30px 40px"`, and the previous silent-acceptance behavior (which would have crashed at runtime when an em value resolved against an undefined context) is gone. Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (Web API Resolved Issues section, `DOMMatrix` / `IntersectionObserver` absolute-length enforcement, commit 316532@main, bug 181453666).

### Compatibility / Embedded Runtime (C)

- [C-231] **[P2] Safari TP 249 Web Inspector Adopts the Range Mappings Source Maps Proposal for Cross-Range Generated-To-Source Mapping** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29) adds support for the range mappings proposal in source maps (Web Inspector, commit 316748@main, bug 178564308). The range mappings proposal extends the source map v3 format with cross-range generated-to-source mappings — useful when a generated code range maps to multiple disjoint source ranges (e.g. a transpiled function whose body comes from multiple source lines, or a CSS preprocessor `@import` chain). For a controlled fixture with a JavaScript bundle `bundle.js` and an accompanying source map that uses the range mappings extension (e.g. `"mappings": "AAAA,...;ABCD,..."` with a `x_google_rangeMappings` field), assert the Web Inspector's source-map panel correctly resolves breakpoints inside the bundle to the appropriate source file and range (a breakpoint set on `bundle.js:42` highlights the correct line in the original source), the Web Inspector's `Sources` panel shows the original file content when navigating via `Cmd+Click` (Go to Source) on the mapped range, the range mapping extension is parsed without throwing (no `SyntaxError` in the Web Inspector console), a fixture with a malformed `x_google_rangeMappings` extension falls back to the legacy line-column mapping gracefully, and the Web Inspector `debugger` integration correctly steps through range-mapped code (the variable scope shown in the inspector matches the source position, not the generated position). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (Web Inspector New Features section, range mappings proposal in source maps, commit 316748@main, bug 178564308).

## New Test Cases Added in v1.213.0 (R330 — August 3 2026, 4h cycle 92)

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-244] **[P1] Safari 27 Beta `revert-rule` CSS Cascade Keyword Rolls Back to "As If the Current Rule Had Not Been Present", Distinct From `revert` and `revert-layer`** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) adds the `revert-rule` keyword (CSS Cascade Level 5) that rolls back the cascade to the state as if the current style rule had not been present. It is the third member of the `revert` / `revert-layer` / `revert-rule` cascade-keyword family: `revert` rolls back to the user-agent / user style level, `revert-layer` rolls back to the previous cascade layer, and `revert-rule` rolls back to "as if the current rule had not been present" (the cascade as it would have computed without the rule currently being declared). For a controlled fixture with `<style>:root { --accent: red } body { color: var(--accent) } .x { color: blue } .x { color: revert-rule; }</style><p class="x">test</p>`, assert `getComputedStyle(p).color === "rgb(255, 0, 0)"` (the `red` value from `:root` since the `color: blue` rule is rolled back as if not present, not the original cascade), a controlled fixture where `revert-rule` is replaced with `revert` returns `color: "rgb(0, 0, 0)"` (the user-agent default `black` for `color`), a controlled fixture where `revert-rule` is replaced with `revert-layer` returns the same as `revert-rule` (no explicit layer is declared), `CSS.supports("color", "revert-rule")` returns `true` in Safari 27 beta and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls, the keyword is rejected on properties where it does not apply (e.g. `display: revert-rule` is invalid per CSS Cascade Level 5), and a controlled fixture with a `@layer foo { .x { color: blue } }` plus `.x { color: revert-rule; }` rolls back the `.x` rule but keeps the layer-wrapped declaration's effect. Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (revert-rule keyword section).

- [B-245] **[P1] Safari 27 Beta Transform-Aware Anchor Positioning Tracks a Transformed Anchor's Post-Transform Position, Not Its Pre-Transform Layout Box** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) adds transform-aware anchor positioning. When an anchor element has a CSS transform applied (via the `transform` property or the individual `translate` / `rotate` / `scale` properties), elements positioned relative to that anchor follow its **transformed** position rather than its pre-transform layout position. For a controlled fixture with `<style>.a { anchor-name: --gh; transform: scale(2) translate(40px, 30px); } .pop { position: fixed; position-anchor: --gh; position-area: top; }</style><div class="a" id="a"></div><div class="pop" id="pop">…</div>`, assert the popover's `getBoundingClientRect()` reflects the anchor's post-transform position (the anchor's geometric position after `scale(2) translate(40px, 30px)` is applied, not its layout-box position), the popover's `position-anchor` reference resolves through the transformed anchor box (the `position-area: top` value is computed relative to the transformed box), the same fixture with `transform: none` on the anchor keeps the legacy pre-transform positioning behavior (the popover tracks the layout box), animated transforms on the anchor (e.g. `transform: scale(...)` driven by a `@keyframes` animation) cause the popover to track the animating anchor in real time, and Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (no transform-aware anchor positioning) keep the popover anchored to the pre-transform layout box even when the anchor has a `transform` declared, causing the popover to lag behind the visible anchor. Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (transform-aware anchor positioning section).

- [B-246] **[P2] Safari 27 Beta Singular `anchor-valid` / `anchor-visible` (Renamed From Deprecated `anchors-valid` / `anchors-visible`) Hide a Positioned Element Only When Its Default Anchor Box Cannot Be Resolved** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) renames the `position-visibility: anchors-valid` and `position-visibility: anchors-visible` keywords to singular `anchor-valid` and `anchor-visible` respectively, to match the updated CSS Anchor Positioning specification that changed the behavior to look only at the **default anchor box** rather than all required anchor references. For a controlled fixture with `<style>.a { anchor-name: --gh; } .pop { position: fixed; position-anchor: --gh; position-visibility: anchor-valid; }</style>`, assert `getComputedStyle(pop).positionVisibility === "anchor-valid"` in Safari 27 beta, the same fixture with `position-visibility: anchors-valid` is parsed but produces a non-`anchor-valid` value (the deprecated plural is not migrated automatically), the positioned element is hidden (`getComputedStyle(pop).display === "none"` or equivalent) when the default anchor box cannot be resolved, a controlled fixture where the anchor IS resolved keeps the element visible, a controlled fixture using `position-visibility: anchor-visible` hides the element only when the default anchor is **not visible** (i.e. hidden by another element or scrolled out of view), `CSS.supports("position-visibility: anchor-valid")` returns `true` in Safari 27 beta and `false` in Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 (still plural `anchors-valid`) / Firefox 153 (still plural) controls, and a controlled fixture where multiple `anchor-name`s are declared on the anchor keeps `anchor-valid` looking only at the default anchor (matching the new spec). Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (anchor-valid / anchor-visible section).

- [B-247] **[P2] Safari 27 Beta Browser-Default `overflow-anchor: auto` Scroll Anchoring Prevents Content-Jumps When Content Is Inserted Above the Viewport** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) adds scroll anchoring, controlled by the `overflow-anchor` CSS property that defaults to `auto`. When content is inserted or removed above the current viewport position (an image loads, an ad injects, a comment appears), the browser automatically adjusts the scroll position so the content the user is reading stays put instead of jumping. For a controlled fixture with `<div style="height: 200vh"><div id="top" style="height: 200px">top</div><div id="mid" style="height: 200px">mid</div><div id="bot" style="height: 200px">bot</div></div>`, scroll to `scrollTo(0, 100)`, then dynamically insert a 1000-pixel-tall `<div>` before `#top` (e.g. `document.body.insertBefore(bigEl, topEl)`), assert the post-insertion `window.scrollY` is `1100` (1000 px of new content above the original 100 px scroll position, no jump), the visible viewport keeps the same `#mid` / `#bot` content scrolled to the same relative position, a controlled fixture where `#top` has `overflow-anchor: none` causes a visible jump (the original 100 px scroll position is preserved, not compensated for the inserted content), `getComputedStyle(document.scrollingElement).overflowAnchor === "auto"` is the default, and Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (which already ship scroll anchoring) keep the same behavior — the Safari 27 beta case validates the **Safari implementation** (since the WWDC26 announcement explicitly mentions fixing scroll anchoring regressions like 170889205, 171221075, 173885027, 175195943 in the same release). Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (scroll anchoring section).

- [B-248] **[P2] Safari 27 Beta Predefined Linear-Light Color Spaces `srgb-linear` and `display-p3-linear` Are Available in Canvas, WebGL, and Color APIs** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) adds the predefined linear-light color spaces `srgb-linear` and `display-p3-linear` (CSS Color Level 4). These linear-light color spaces are the linearized form of the gamma-encoded sRGB and Display P3 color spaces and are essential for color-correct image compositing, HDR rendering, and 3D rendering pipelines. For a controlled fixture using `<canvas id="cv"></canvas>` and `const ctx = cv.getContext("2d"); const imgData = ctx.createImageData(1, 1); imgData.data.set([255, 0, 0, 255]); ctx.putImageData(imgData, 0, 0);`, assert `ctx.getContext("2d").getImageData(0, 0, 1, 1).data` returns the byte-equal `[255, 0, 0, 255]` payload (the `srgb-linear` color space is a **predefined color space identifier** that does not change the default canvas color space, which remains `srgb` unless explicitly converted), a controlled fixture that explicitly converts via `new Color("color(srgb-linear 1 0 0)")` returns a `Color` object whose serialized form is the linear-light encoding, the same fixture using `new Color("color(display-p3-linear 1 0 0)")` returns a `Color` object whose serialized form is the linear Display P3 encoding, `CSS.supports("color: color(srgb-linear 1 0 0)")` returns `true` in Safari 27 beta, and the same `CSS.supports` check returns `false` in Safari 26.6 (no `srgb-linear` predefined color space) and `true` in Chrome 150 (already ships `srgb-linear`). Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (srgb-linear and display-p3-linear section).

### Web Platform / Browser (W)

- [W-426] **[P1] Safari 27 Beta `<model>` HTML Element Lands on iOS / iPadOS / macOS, Joining Its Prior visionOS Availability, to Embed Interactive 3D USDZ Content** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) extends the `<model>` HTML element from its prior visionOS-only availability to iOS, iPadOS, and macOS. Web developers can now embed interactive 3D content using standard HTML across Apple platforms. For a controlled fixture with `<model id="m" src="teapot.usdz"></model>` served on a controlled HTTPS origin with a valid `.usdz` payload, assert `document.querySelector("model").constructor.name === "HTMLModelElement"` (the element is recognized as a first-class HTML element on iOS/iPadOS/macOS, not `HTMLUnknownElement`), the element's `src` attribute getter returns the original `teapot.usdz` URL, dispatching a controlled `click` event on the element enters its stage mode (assert the element's `stageMode` property or equivalent observable changes), a controlled fixture where the `.usdz` payload is a 50 MB asset loads without OOM (heap delta ≤ 8x the parsed-string size, the same memory bound as v1.208.0 B-236 for standalone SVG), a controlled fixture where the `src` URL is a 404 returns a controlled `error` event, Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (no `<model>` support) report `document.querySelector("model").constructor.name === "HTMLUnknownElement"`, and the Safari 27 beta case explicitly tests **iOS / iPadOS / macOS** platform parity — visionOS already had the element and is the regression baseline. Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (`<model>` on iOS / iPadOS / macOS section).

- [W-427] **[P2] Safari 27 Beta `dynamic-range-limit` Content-Attribute on `<model>` Controls the HDR Rendering Range for 3D Content on iOS and macOS** — Safari 27 beta (WWDC26, released 2026-06-08) adds support for the `dynamic-range-limit` content-attribute on the `<model>` HTML element, giving Web developers control over the HDR rendering range for 3D content on iOS and macOS. The attribute accepts the values `standard` (the default, treats the content as standard dynamic range) and `high` (renders the content as high dynamic range, exploiting the device's HDR capabilities where available). For a controlled fixture with `<model id="m" src="hdr-content.usdz" dynamic-range-limit="high"></model>` and a parallel `<model id="s" src="hdr-content.usdz" dynamic-range-limit="standard"></model>`, assert `document.querySelector("#m").getAttribute("dynamic-range-limit") === "high"`, the same attribute read on `#s` returns `"standard"`, the element reflects the `dynamic-range-limit` IDL attribute (`document.querySelector("#m").dynamicRangeLimit === "high"`), setting `dynamicRangeLimit = "high"` programmatically on a model that loaded with `dynamic-range-limit="standard"` updates the rendering range without re-fetching the asset, a controlled fixture where the attribute is omitted falls back to `standard` (the default), a controlled fixture with `dynamic-range-limit="invalid"` falls back to `standard` (unknown values are ignored, no console error), and Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (no `dynamic-range-limit` attribute) read `document.querySelector("model").getAttribute("dynamic-range-limit")` as `null` (the attribute is unknown). Source: WebKit News from WWDC26 — WebKit in Safari 27 beta, https://webkit.org/blog/17967/news-from-wwdc26-webkit-in-safari-27-beta/ (`dynamic-range-limit` on `<model>` section).

- [W-428] **[P2] Safari Technology Preview 249 Adds WebGPU Subgroups Support for Thread-Group Partitioning in Compute and Game Rendering** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29, commit 317145@main, bug 154874391) adds WebGPU subgroups support. Subgroups enable a compute or render shader to coordinate and partition work within a thread-group (a "subgroup" of the threads), enabling efficient parallel reductions, vote operations, and synchronized data exchange that is essential for modern game-rendering pipelines (e.g. tile-based deferred rendering, GPU-driven particle systems, hierarchical-Z occlusion culling). For a controlled WebGPU pipeline fixture that creates a compute shader with `@compute @workgroup_size(64) fn main(@builtin(subgroup_size) size: u32, @builtin(subgroup_invocation_id) id: u32) { /* uses subgroup_size + subgroup_invocation_id */ }`, assert the WGSL parser accepts `subgroup_size` and `subgroup_invocation_id` as built-in attributes without a parser error, a parallel `subgroupAdd(...)` / `subgroupMin(...)` / `subgroupMax(...)` built-in call returns the expected reduction result on a fixture workgroup (e.g. `subgroupAdd(1u)` for a workgroup of 8 threads returns `8u`), the shader executes to completion and the GPUDevice's `adapter.limits` includes the relevant `subgroupMinSize` and `subgroupMaxSize` constants, Safari TP 248 control (no subgroups) returns a WGSL parser error on the `subgroup_size` built-in, and a non-WebGPU control (Chrome 150 / Firefox 153) returns a WGSL parser error as well — the case verifies the **Safari TP 249** contract, distinct from the v1.202.0 W-385 WebGPU 2.0 long-session GC case (a different WebGPU surface). Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (Web API section, WebGPU subgroup support commit 317145@main, bug 154874391).

### Compatibility / Embedded Runtime (C)

- [C-230] **[P1] Safari 27 Beta WebDriver Support for the Digital Credentials API Adds Commands to Simulate Wallet Payloads, Indefinite Waits, and User Rejection** — Safari Technology Preview 249 (released 2026-07-29, commit 316435@main, bug 168941907) and Safari 27 beta add WebDriver support for the Digital Credentials API, including commands to simulate wallet payloads, indefinite waits, and user rejection. This is the WebDriver-side testing surface that complements the request-side Digital Credentials API (v1.205.0 S-467 W3C Digital Credentials). For a controlled fixture using Safari's WebDriver, the page calls `navigator.credentials.get({identity: {providers: [{protocol: "openid4vp", request: "..."}]}})` to request a Digital Credential, assert a WebDriver `setDigitalCredentialWallet` command with a controlled wallet payload resolves the credential request with a byte-equal verifiable credential, a parallel `setDigitalCredentialRejection` command rejects the credential request with a controlled error reason, the WebDriver command `waitForDigitalCredentialIndefinite` blocks until a credential request is initiated by the page (used for testing the "wallet does not respond" case), a parallel WebDriver `dispatchDigitalCredentialUserRejection` command simulates a user-cancel event on the credential request, and Safari 26.6 / Chrome 150 / Firefox 153 controls (where WebDriver does not support the Digital Credentials API commands) return an unknown-command error rather than silently dispatching the wallet payload. Source: Safari Technology Preview 249 release notes, https://webkit.org/blog/18182/release-notes-for-safari-technology-preview-249/ (WebDriver section, Digital Credentials API support commit 316435@main, bug 168941907), and the user-gesture authorization token fix in TP 249 commit 317228@main (bug 174908839) is a related but distinct contract.

### Summary — v1.213.0 New Test Cases

| ID | Cat | P | Machine-verifiable focus |
|----|-----|---|--------------------------|
| B-244 | B | P1 | `revert-rule` cascade keyword rolls back to "as if the current rule had not been present"; distinct from `revert` (UA default) and `revert-layer` (previous cascade layer); `@supports` parity |
| B-245 | B | P1 | Transform-aware anchor positioning: anchored element tracks anchor's post-transform position; animated transforms cause the popover to track in real time; pre-Safari-27-beta control keeps pre-transform lag |
| B-246 | B | P2 | Singular `anchor-valid` / `anchor-visible` (renamed from plural); looks only at default anchor box; deprecated plural `anchors-valid` / `anchors-visible` is parsed but produces a non-`anchor-valid` value |
| B-247 | B | P2 | Browser-default `overflow-anchor: auto` scroll anchoring compensates for inserted content above the viewport; `overflow-anchor: none` opts out and produces a visible jump |
| B-248 | B | P2 | Predefined linear-light color spaces `srgb-linear` and `display-p3-linear` available in CSS Color Level 4; `@supports` true in Safari 27 beta and Chrome 150, false in Safari 26.6 |
| W-426 | W | P1 | `<model>` HTML element recognized on iOS / iPadOS / macOS as `HTMLModelElement`; USDZ 50 MB asset loads with bounded heap; non-Safari-27-beta returns `HTMLUnknownElement` |
| W-427 | W | P2 | `dynamic-range-limit` content-attribute reflects as `dynamicRangeLimit` IDL; `high` / `standard` values; unknown values fall back to `standard`; non-Safari-27-beta reads `null` |
| W-428 | W | P2 | WGSL `subgroup_size` / `subgroup_invocation_id` built-ins parse and execute; `subgroupAdd` returns expected reduction result; `subgroupMinSize` / `subgroupMaxSize` exposed in `adapter.limits`; pre-TP-249 control rejects the WGSL |
| C-230 | C | P1 | WebDriver `setDigitalCredentialWallet` / `setDigitalCredentialRejection` / `waitForDigitalCredentialIndefinite` / `dispatchDigitalCredentialUserRejection` commands resolve / reject the credential request; non-Safari-27-beta returns unknown-command error |

*(9 new test cases this cycle. Every acceptance criterion is executable through controlled CSSOM computed-style reads, `@supports` parity queries, WGSL parser round-trip with a controlled compute shader, USDZ asset load with bounded heap, `navigator.credentials.get` controlled wallet-payload resolution, anchor-positioning bounding-rect math on transformed elements, controlled `overflow-anchor` scroll-position measurement, `new Color("color(srgb-linear …)")` linear-light encoding, and WebDriver command round-trip; no case depends on subjective visual scoring.)*
