- [W-449] **[P1] Chrome 154 Stable Media Element Pseudo-Classes `:playing` / `:paused` / `:seeking` / `:buffering` / `:stalled` / `:muted` / `:volume-locked` Graduate From Chrome 152 Beta / Edge 152 Stable To Chrome 154 Stable So Audio / Video Game Authoring Has Cross-Engine Selector Parity (Interop 2026 Focus Area)** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`, Interop 2026 focus area at `https://wpt.fyi/interop-2026`) ships the media-element pseudo-classes `:playing` / `:paused` / `:seeking` / `:buffering` / `:stalled` / `:muted` / `:volume-locked` as enabled-by-default after graduating from the Chrome 152 beta introduction (B-257 v1.215.0) and Edge 152 stable adoption. The pseudo-classes match `<audio>` and `<video>` elements based on their state — `:playing` matches when the element is actively playing (not paused, not ended, not seeking), `:paused` matches when the element is paused via `pause()` or user action, `:seeking` matches when the element is currently seeking to a new playback position (i.e. the `seeking` event has fired and the `seeked` event has not yet fired), `:buffering` matches when the element is buffering because the browser does not have enough data to play (i.e. the `waiting` event has fired), `:stalled` matches when the element is stalled because data fetching has failed (i.e. the `stalled` event has fired), `:muted` matches when the element's `muted` IDL attribute is true, and `:volume-locked` matches when the user cannot change the volume because the platform's volume controls are disabled (e.g. on iOS Safari when the hardware mute switch is on). This is an Interop 2026 focus area because the pseudo-classes are the primary way to style media-element state from CSS — without them, authors need JS-driven class toggling which is brittle and lags the actual state. For a controlled fixture serving `<audio id="a" src="/test-tone.wav"></audio>` over HTTPS where `/test-tone.wav` is a 5-second 440 Hz sine wave served with `Content-Type: audio/wav`, assert that `:playing` matches the audio element while it is actively playing (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('audio:playing') === a` during the playback window after `a.play()` resolves — the controlled fixture does NOT use `:playing` during initial load because the audio has not started playing yet), `:paused` matches after `a.pause()` is called (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('audio:paused') === a` immediately after `a.pause()`), `:seeking` matches during a seek operation (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('audio:seeking') === a` between `a.currentTime = 3.0` and the `seeked` event firing), `:buffering` matches when the element enters the waiting state (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('audio:buffering') === a` after the `waiting` event fires — this requires a server endpoint that pauses data delivery mid-stream to force the waiting state), `:muted` matches when `a.muted = true` is set (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('audio:muted') === a` immediately after the property set), the pseudo-classes work on `<video>` elements too (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('video:playing') === v` after `v.play()` resolves for a 5-second test pattern served as `Content-Type: video/mp4`), the pseudo-classes do NOT match non-media elements (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('div:playing') === null` — a `<div>` element is never `:playing`), `@supports selector(:playing)` returns `true` on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate — pre-Chrome-154 controls return `false`), `Chrome 153` controls (no stable media-element pseudo-classes; the feature is Chrome 154 stable) report `document.querySelector('audio:playing') === null` even during playback (the selector does not match), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has partial media-element pseudo-classes support — `:muted` shipped long ago but `:playing` / `:paused` / `:seeking` / `:buffering` / `:stalled` / `:volume-locked` may or may not be present depending on the Safari version) report mixed coverage (controlled fixture verifying which pseudo-classes match on Safari 26.6 vs Chrome 154 — Safari may match `:muted` but report `null` for `:playing` / `:seeking` / `:buffering`), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox has partial media-element pseudo-classes support; the `:muted` pseudo-class was the first to ship, and `:playing` / `:paused` may or may not be present depending on the Firefox version) report mixed coverage (controlled fixture verifying which pseudo-classes match on Firefox 155 vs Chrome 154). Spec reference: `https://wpt.fyi/interop-2026` documents the Interop 2026 focus area and the WPT fixtures for cross-engine parity; the underlying CSS Selectors Level 4 spec at `https://www.w3.org/TR/selectors-4/#media-pseudo` defines the media-element pseudo-classes grammar. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the live `:playing` / `:paused` / `:seeking` / `:buffering` / `:muted` selectors match the expected state at each transition point, plus the `@supports` probe + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

- [W-450] **[P2] Chrome 154 Stable Window Shape API `window.setShape(rects)` Lets ChromeOS IWAs Specify Non-Rectangular / Non-Contiguous Window Layouts So Widgets And Floating Panels Match Native Look-And-Feel** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`) ships the Window Shape API as enabled-by-default after graduating from the Edge 152 origin trial and the Chrome 152 beta origin trial. The API lets an allowlisted ChromeOS Isolated Web App (IWA) specify a non-rectangular window shape via `window.setShape(rects)` where `rects` is an array of `DOMRect`-like objects (`{x, y, width, height}`). The motivation is to enable widgets / floating panels / overlays with native look-and-feel — e.g. a calculator IWA can specify a single rect, a calendar widget can specify a non-contiguous shape that wraps around the system clock, and a music player overlay can specify a custom shape that avoids the system tray. The API requires (a) unframed display mode (set via `window.matchMedia('(display-mode: unframed)')`), and (b) the `window-management` Permission-Policy. The existing enterprise policies (`DefaultWindowManagementSetting` / `WindowManagementAllowedForUrls` / `WindowManagementBlockedForUrls` per `https://chromeenterprise.google/policies/`) continue to apply — admins can block the API per-origin. For a controlled fixture running on ChromeOS in an IWA with `<meta http-equiv="Permissions-Policy" content="window-management=(self)">` and `display-mode: unframed`, assert that the `window.setShape` method exists (controlled fixture verifying `typeof window.setShape === "function"`), `window.setShape([{x:0, y:0, width:400, height:300}])` accepts a single-rect shape (controlled fixture verifying the call does NOT throw and the platform's window-system reflects the new shape — the visible window bounds equal `{x:0, y:0, width:400, height:300}` within ±1 px on a DPI-aware display), `window.setShape([{x:0, y:0, width:200, height:300}, {x:300, y:0, width:200, height:300}])` accepts a non-contiguous shape with a 100-px gap (controlled fixture verifying the call does NOT throw and the platform's window-system reflects the non-contiguous shape — the visible window consists of two separate rectangles), `window.setShape([])` resets to a single default rectangle (controlled fixture verifying the call does NOT throw and the visible window reverts to a single rectangle covering the full IWA bounds), `window.setShape([{x:0, y:0, width:400, height:300}])` rejects with `NotAllowedError` when the `window-management` permission is NOT granted (controlled fixture verifying the permission gate — `try { window.setShape([...]); } catch(e) { e.name === "NotAllowedError" }`), `window.setShape` rejects with `NotAllowedError` when the window is NOT in unframed display mode (controlled fixture verifying the display-mode gate — a regular HTTPS-served page in framed mode throws `NotAllowedError` from `window.setShape()`), the existing enterprise policies `WindowManagementAllowedForUrls` and `WindowManagementBlockedForUrls` apply (controlled fixture verifying a URL listed in `WindowManagementBlockedForUrls` throws `NotAllowedError` from `window.setShape()` even when the permission-policy allows it), `Chrome 153` controls (no stable Window Shape API; the feature was Edge 152 OT + Chrome 152 beta OT) report `typeof window.setShape === "undefined"` (the method does not exist on the Chrome 153 baseline), `Safari 26.6` controls (no Window Shape API; Safari has no IWA concept) report `typeof window.setShape === "undefined"`, `Firefox 155` controls (no Window Shape API; Firefox has no IWA concept) report `typeof window.setShape === "undefined"`. Spec reference: the Window Management API spec at `https://wicg.github.io/window-management/` documents the `setShape` extension; the Chrome IWA documentation at `https://chromeos.dev/en/web/isolated-web-apps` documents the IWA + unframed display mode + window-management permission requirements; the sample demo at `https://github.com/paulinagacek/Set-Shape-demo` is a working IWA that demonstrates the API. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the live `window.setShape()` round-trip + the `setShape([])` reset + the permission + display-mode gates + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable on ChromeOS for the live-fire part.

### Browser Compatibility / CSS (B)

- [B-270] **[P1] Chrome 154 Stable CSS Image Animation Property `image-animation: normal | running | paused` + `:animated-image` Pseudo-Class Let Authors Control And Detect Animation Of GIF / APNG / Animated WebP Image Assets Without JavaScript** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`) ships the CSS Image Animation property and pseudo-class: (a) `image-animation: normal | running | paused` CSS property that controls the animation playback of `<img>` elements and CSS `background-image` properties whose value is an animated image format (GIF / APNG / animated WebP), with `normal` being the default (play once / loop per the asset's metadata), `running` explicitly playing, and `paused` explicitly pausing; (b) `:animated-image` pseudo-class that matches when an `<img>` element or any element with an animated-image `background-image` is currently animating (i.e. is in the `running` playback state, not paused). The motivation is to give authors declarative control over animated-image playback — e.g. pause a 50-frame GIF until the user scrolls it into view (using IntersectionObserver + `image-animation: paused`), or detect whether a `<img>` is actually playing animation (using `:animated-image`) to provide a different visual treatment for static vs animated content. For a controlled fixture serving a 5-frame looping APNG image (e.g. a 100x100 px animated checkmark icon) via `Content-Type: image/apng` over HTTPS and embedding it via `<img id="img" src="/test.apng" alt="test">`, assert that `:animated-image` matches the image element while it is animating (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('img:animated-image') === img` immediately after page load when the image has started animating), setting `image-animation: paused` on the image element pauses the animation (controlled fixture verifying the CSSOM property is set — `getComputedStyle(img).imageAnimation === "paused"`, and `:animated-image` no longer matches — `document.querySelector('img:animated-image') === null`), setting `image-animation: running` resumes the animation (controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(img).imageAnimation === "running"` and `:animated-image` matches again), setting `image-animation: normal` returns the asset to its default playback behavior (controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(img).imageAnimation === "normal"` and the image resumes playing per its metadata), `:animated-image` does NOT match a static PNG image (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('img:animated-image') === null` for an `<img src="/static.png">` element — the pseudo-class only matches animated-image assets), `:animated-image` matches when an element has an animated-image `background-image` (controlled fixture verifying `document.querySelector('div:animated-image') === div` for a `<div style="background-image: url('/test.apng')">` element while the animation is running), `@supports (image-animation: paused)` returns `true` on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate), `Chrome 153` controls (no CSS Image Animation property) report `getComputedStyle(img).imageAnimation === ""` (the property is not present on the Chrome 153 baseline) and `:animated-image` does NOT match animated images (the pseudo-class does not exist), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has partial CSS Image Animation support — the `image-animation` property may or may not be exposed depending on the Safari version) report mixed coverage (controlled fixture verifying whether the property is exposed on Safari 26.6 vs Chrome 154), `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox has partial CSS Image Animation support; Firefox shipped the `:animated-image` pseudo-class earlier than `image-animation` property) report mixed coverage. Spec reference: the CSS Images Level 4 spec at `https://www.w3.org/TR/css-images-4/` documents the `image-animation` property and `:animated-image` pseudo-class grammar; the WPT fixtures for CSS Image Animation are at `https://wpt.fyi/css/css-images/`. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the live `image-animation` property round-trip + the `:animated-image` pseudo-class selector match + the `@supports` probe + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

- [B-271] **[P2] Chrome 154 Stable CSS4 `text-decoration-skip-spaces` Property Controls Whether Text-Decoration Lines (Underlines / Overlines / Line-Throughs) Skip Over Whitespace Characters With `auto` / `none` / `start` / `end` / `left` / `right` / `space-first` / `space-last` Values** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`) ships the `text-decoration-skip-spaces` CSS property from CSS Text Decoration Level 4: the property controls whether text-decoration lines (underlines / overlines / line-throughs) skip over whitespace characters in the decorated text. The motivation is to allow authors to prevent decorations from being drawn under spaces — visually more appealing than the legacy "underline-everything" behavior where a `text-decoration: underline` decoration extends under every space and creates long visual gaps in justified text. The values are: `auto` (skip decoration on word separators, per language-specific word-separator rules — the default), `none` (do NOT skip, draw decoration through all characters including spaces), `start` / `end` (skip the first / last space adjacent to a word boundary), `left` / `right` (skip the first / last space adjacent to the left / right edge, depending on writing direction), and `space-first` / `space-last` (skip only the first / last space in a sequence of spaces). The four WPT fixtures at `http://wpt.live/css/css-text-decor/text-decoration-skip-spaces-001.html` through `004.html` give controlled-fixture acceptance criteria. For a controlled fixture serving `<p id="t" style="text-decoration: underline; text-decoration-skip-spaces: none;">Hello World</p>` over HTTPS where the underline decoration is rendered, assert that the default `auto` value skips decoration on word-separator whitespace (controlled fixture verifying that the underline decoration between "Hello" and "World" is shorter than the full inter-word distance — i.e. the decoration does NOT extend under the space), `text-decoration-skip-spaces: none` does NOT skip decoration on whitespace (controlled fixture verifying the underline decoration between "Hello" and "World" is the full inter-word distance — i.e. the decoration DOES extend under the space, matching the legacy behavior), `text-decoration-skip-spaces: start` skips the first space adjacent to a word boundary (controlled fixture verifying the decoration skips the first space but draws through any subsequent spaces), `text-decoration-skip-spaces: end` skips the last space adjacent to a word boundary (controlled fixture verifying the decoration draws through leading spaces but skips the last space), `text-decoration-skip-spaces: space-first` skips only the first space in a sequence (controlled fixture verifying the decoration skips the first space but draws through the second / third space in a sequence like "A  B"), `text-decoration-skip-spaces: space-last` skips only the last space in a sequence (controlled fixture verifying the decoration draws through leading spaces but skips the last space in "A  B"), the property works in CSS shorthand form `text-decoration: underline skip-spaces-none` (controlled fixture verifying the shorthand parses correctly and the resulting `text-decoration-skip-spaces` computed value is `none`), `@supports (text-decoration-skip-spaces: none)` returns `true` on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the `@supports` probe is the capability gate), `Chrome 153` controls (no `text-decoration-skip-spaces` property; the legacy default is `auto` per the original CSS Text Decoration Level 3 contract) report `getComputedStyle(t).textDecorationSkipSpaces === ""` (the property is not present on the Chrome 153 baseline), `Safari 26.6` controls (Safari has partial CSS Text Decoration Level 4 support — the `text-decoration-skip-spaces` property may or may not be exposed depending on the Safari version) report mixed coverage, `Firefox 155` controls (Firefox has partial CSS Text Decoration Level 4 support; Firefox shipped `text-decoration-skip-ink` earlier than `text-decoration-skip-spaces`) report mixed coverage. Spec reference: CSS Text Decoration Level 4 at `https://www.w3.org/TR/css-text-decor-4/#text-decoration-skip-spaces-property` documents the property grammar; the WPT fixtures at `http://wpt.live/css/css-text-decor/text-decoration-skip-spaces-001.html` through `004.html` give controlled-fixture acceptance criteria. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the computed-style assertion for each of the 7 property values + the shorthand form + the `@supports` probe + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

- [B-272] **[P2] Chrome 154 Stable `<install>` HTML Element Lets Authors Declaratively Prompt Users To Install A Web App With Optional `src` And `referrerpolicy` Attributes For Cross-Origin Installation Prompts (Graduating From Edge 152 Origin Trial)** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`, chromestatus feature `5152834368700416`, samples at `https://liahiscock.github.io/PWA/WebInstall/element-store.html` + `https://kbhlee2121.github.io/pwa/web-install/only-elements.html`) ships the `<install>` HTML element as enabled-by-default after graduating from the Edge 152 origin trial. The element is a browser-controlled button-like element that, when activated by the user, installs the web app associated with the page's manifest. The element optionally accepts two attributes that allow installation of content from a different origin: (a) `src` attribute — when present, the element installs the manifest at the URL specified by `src` (a different origin from the current page), enabling cross-origin app installation prompts; (b) `referrerpolicy` attribute — when present, controls the referrer policy sent when fetching the manifest at `src`, defaulting to `no-referrer`. For a controlled fixture serving `<install id="inst">Install App</install>` over HTTPS where the page is installable (has a manifest at `/manifest.webmanifest` with `name`, `short_name`, `icons`, `start_url`, and `display` fields), assert that the `<install>` element renders as a browser-controlled button (controlled fixture verifying `getComputedStyle(inst).display !== "none"` and the element is focusable / clickable — `inst.matches(':hover')` and `inst.matches(':focus')` work as expected), clicking the element triggers the install flow (controlled fixture verifying the browser shows an install dialog — `window.beforeinstallprompt` is NOT fired because the declarative `<install>` element does NOT trigger the JS event; the install flow is handled entirely declaratively by the browser), the `<install>` element with no `src` attribute installs the current page's manifest (controlled fixture verifying the install flow resolves to the current origin's manifest at `/manifest.webmanifest`), the `<install>` element with `src="https://other-origin.example/manifest.webmanifest"` attribute installs a different origin's manifest (controlled fixture verifying the install flow resolves to the cross-origin manifest at the specified `src` URL — this is the primary use case for app stores that want to install third-party PWAs), the `referrerpolicy` attribute on `<install>` controls the referrer sent with the `src` manifest fetch (controlled fixture verifying `referrerpolicy="no-referrer"` causes the manifest fetch to omit the Referer header, and `referrerpolicy="origin"` causes the manifest fetch to send only the origin), `<install>` is rendered as an `HTMLElement` (controlled fixture verifying `inst instanceof HTMLElement === true` — the element does NOT have a custom interface), `Chrome 153` controls (no stable `<install>` element; the feature was Edge 152 OT) parse `<install>` as `HTMLUnknownElement` (controlled fixture verifying `document.createElement('install') instanceof HTMLUnknownElement === true` — the element is not yet recognized as a custom element on Chrome 153), `Safari 26.6` controls (no `<install>` element) parse `<install>` as `HTMLUnknownElement`, `Firefox 155` controls (no `<install>` element) parse `<install>` as `HTMLUnknownElement`. Spec reference: the explainer doc at `https://docs.google.com/document/d/1rGvLhD4SR8Y9M1wVmqgyesPNkbZGU7HOqlttjEFJ5Vo/edit?tab=t.tmx19oox759l#heading=h.j3tt49hqiuck` documents the element contract + the cross-origin installation semantics; the sample at `https://kbhlee2121.github.io/pwa/web-install/only-elements.html` gives a working demo of the declarative install flow. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the declarative install flow + the `src` cross-origin manifest fetch + the `referrerpolicy` attribute round-trip + the `HTMLUnknownElement` parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

### Security / Privacy Sandbox (S)

- [S-475] **[P0] Chrome 154 Stable Removes The `<fencedframe>` HTML Element (Step 1: Stub — No JS Calls Throw) And The `window.fence` APIs (Step 2: Complete Removal) Following Protected Audience + Shared Storage Deprecation** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`, Chromium IDL source at `https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/blink/renderer/core/html/fenced_frame/fenced_frame_config.idl`, blink-dev Intent-to-Deprecate thread for Protected Audience at `https://groups.google.com/a/chromium.org/g/blink-dev/c/k_nubsMb97g/m/awPD4IGLBAAJ`, blink-dev Intent-to-Deprecate thread for Shared Storage at `https://groups.google.com/a/chromium.org/g/blink-dev/c/uh5Ke6qyegc/m/WFTFnhyJBAAJ`) ships the M154 step-1 stub of the fenced-frame element AND the M154 step-2 complete removal of the `window.fence` APIs as part of the broader Privacy Sandbox deprecation (the Protected Audience API and Shared Storage API are being deprecated and removed, and since fenced frames can only be navigated using the `urn:uuid` in a `FencedFrameConfig` (which can only be created via `runAdAuction` and `selectURL`), removing PA + selectURL means fenced frames can no longer be navigated, so removing the fenced-frame element + `window.fence` APIs is the safe cleanup). The plan as published is: (a) **M154**: Keep the fenced-frame element and its associated IDL dependencies as **stubs** — i.e. `fenced-frame-element.config.setSharedStorageContext()` does NOT throw (it's a no-op stub), but the element no longer actually creates a fenced frame; (b) **M154**: In the same milestone, **completely remove `window.fence`** APIs — `window.fence.setReportEventDataForAutomaticBeacons()` / `window.fence.reportEvent()` / etc. all return `undefined`; (c) **M155 Canary/Beta**: Begin a controlled rollout of the stub FF HTML element removal via a field trial — the element starts resolving to `HTMLUnknownElement` for a small percentage of users; (d) **M155 Stable**: Assuming no regressions after 1% stable, request additional approval for full removal. The deprecation is gated by the broader Privacy Sandbox removal; the chromium bug tracks the deprecation at `https://crbug.com/435623334` (same bug tracker as S-473 XSLT deprecation timeline, but the deprecation target is different). For a controlled fixture serving `<fencedframe id="ff" mode="opaque-ads" src="https://attacker.example/"></fencedframe>` over HTTPS, assert that the `<fencedframe>` element exists as a stub on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying `document.createElement('fencedframe')` returns an element whose `.constructor.name === "HTMLElement"` — the element is parsed but is now a stub), `fencedframe.config.setSharedStorageContext("test")` does NOT throw on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the stub no-throw contract — the call returns `undefined` silently per the M154 step-1 plan), `window.fence` is `undefined` on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the M154 step-2 complete removal — `typeof window.fence === "undefined"` and `typeof window.fence.setReportEventDataForAutomaticBeacons === "undefined"`), `window.fence` was previously available on Chrome 153 stable (controlled fixture noting the Chrome 153 baseline has `typeof window.fence === "object"` with methods like `setReportEventDataForAutomaticBeacons` / `reportEvent` / `getNestedConfigs` — the M154 step-2 removal closes this surface), the fenced-frame element does NOT actually navigate to the `src` URL on Chrome 154 stable (controlled fixture verifying the element renders as an empty / stub DOM — the navigation path is broken because `FencedFrameConfig` can no longer be created via `runAdAuction` or `selectURL`), the deprecation does NOT remove the `<iframe>` element (controlled fixture verifying `<iframe>` continues to function normally — `<iframe src="https://example.com">` navigates as expected, the removal is fenced-frame-specific), `Chrome 154` controls (no stable Chrome 154 yet for cross-version check, but Chrome 153 baseline has `typeof window.fence === "object"` and the fenced-frame element navigates correctly) demonstrate the pre-removal state, `Safari 26.6` controls (no `<fencedframe>` element ever shipped in Safari; no `window.fence` ever shipped in Safari) report `document.createElement('fencedframe') instanceof HTMLUnknownElement === true` and `typeof window.fence === "undefined"` (Safari was never a target for fenced frames), `Firefox 155` controls (no `<fencedframe>` element; no `window.fence`) report the same parity as Safari. Spec reference: the Chromium IDL source at `https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/blink/renderer/core/html/fenced_frame/fenced_frame_config.idl` documents the stub no-throw contract; the blink-dev Intent threads document the broader Privacy Sandbox deprecation context. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the M154 step-1 stub no-throw contract + the M154 step-2 complete removal of `window.fence` + the `<fencedframe>` element no-navigation behavior + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

### Compatibility / Cross-Browser (C)

- [C-236] **[P2] Chrome 154 Stable Web Install API `navigator.install()` (3 Signatures: 0 Args / 1 Arg / 2 Args) Lets A Website Install Either Itself Or Another Origin's Web App After Graduating From Edge 152 Origin Trial** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`, chromestatus feature `5183481574850560`, sample at `https://kbhlee2121.github.io/pwa/web-install/index.html` + `https://microsoftedge.github.io/Demos/pwa-pwastore`) ships the Web Install API as enabled-by-default after graduating from the Edge 152 origin trial. The API provides 3 signatures that are all experimented with in parallel: (a) `navigator.install()` with 0 args — installs the current document's web app (the page that called `navigator.install()`); (b) `navigator.install(url)` with 1 arg — installs the web app at the specified URL, which can be either a same-origin URL (different page on the same site) or a different-origin URL (cross-origin install); (c) `navigator.install(url, options)` with 2 args — installs the web app at the specified URL with options like `referrerPolicy` or `installDescription`. The 0-arg signature is a "current document install"; the 1-arg and 2-arg signatures are "background document install". The motivation is to give authors a JS-driven alternative to the `beforeinstallprompt` event-based installation flow — particularly for app stores that want to install third-party PWAs without navigating away from the current page. For a controlled fixture serving a PWA-installable page (has a manifest at `/manifest.webmanifest` with `name`, `short_name`, `icons`, `start_url`, `display`) over HTTPS with a user-gesture activation handler, assert that the `navigator.install` method exists (controlled fixture verifying `typeof navigator.install === "function"`), `navigator.install()` with 0 args returns a `Promise<boolean>` that resolves to `true` when the user accepts the install prompt (controlled fixture verifying the install flow — the browser shows a confirmation dialog, and after the user accepts, `navigator.install()` resolves to `true`), `navigator.install()` rejects with `NotAllowedError` when called WITHOUT a user-gesture activation (controlled fixture verifying the activation gate — `try { navigator.install(); } catch(e) { e.name === "NotAllowedError" }` when called from a non-activation handler), `navigator.install("https://other-origin.example/")` with 1 arg returns a `Promise<boolean>` that resolves to `true` when the user accepts the cross-origin install prompt (controlled fixture verifying the cross-origin install flow — the browser fetches the manifest at the specified URL and prompts for installation), `navigator.install("https://other-origin.example/", {referrerPolicy: "no-referrer"})` with 2 args controls the referrer policy sent when fetching the cross-origin manifest (controlled fixture verifying the manifest fetch omits the Referer header when `referrerPolicy: "no-referrer"` is specified), `navigator.install` does NOT throw when the page is NOT installable (controlled fixture verifying the API returns a `Promise` that rejects with a meaningful error — e.g. `NotSupportedError` or `NotInstallableError` — rather than throwing synchronously), `Chrome 153` controls (no stable Web Install API; the feature was Edge 152 OT) report `typeof navigator.install === "undefined"` (the method does not exist on the Chrome 153 baseline), `Safari 26.6` controls (no Web Install API; Safari has no equivalent declarative install API beyond the `beforeinstallprompt` event) report `typeof navigator.install === "undefined"`, `Firefox 155` controls (no Web Install API; Firefox has no equivalent declarative install API beyond `beforeinstallprompt`) report `typeof navigator.install === "undefined"`. Spec reference: the explainer doc at `https://docs.google.com/document/d/12nSXJLm8mW0gWZ_yjlXfrV8r9gwJliVt4WVa-209-KA/edit?tab=t.0` documents the 3 signatures + the current-document vs background-document install semantics; the sample at `https://kbhlee2121.github.io/pwa/web-install/index.html` gives a working demo of all 3 signatures. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the 3-signature round-trip + the user-gesture activation gate + the cross-origin manifest fetch + the not-installable page rejection + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.

### Performance (P)

- [P-190] **[P1] Chrome 154 Stable IndexedDB: SQLite Backend Step 2 Of Multi-Phase Rollout Rewrites IDB On Top Of SQLite To Replace LevelDB + Flat-Files Hybrid (No Web API Change — Only Affects New Data Stores)** — Chrome 154 stable (Aug 2026, via `chromestatuslite.com/?version=154`, chromestatus feature `5126896685809664`, docs at `https://docs.google.com/document/d/1S9Rx67BmCZIP7tpasKaQ2FlV4TL1Z15DitR2kRZs9nw/edit?usp=sharing` + `https://docs.google.com/document/d/1-N4NqdYjqaWEbfVPYnCg2Ho1u4YK48pUoQ_tSy9SUg8/edit?usp=sharing`, sample at `https://evanstade.github.io/web-storage-demos/idb-txn-scopes`) ships step 2 of the multi-phase IndexedDB backend migration to SQLite. The migration rewrites Chromium's IndexedDB implementation on top of SQLite to replace the prior implementation that uses a hybrid of LevelDB (for key-value data) and flat files (for indexing). There is **no change to the Web API** — the `indexedDB` interface, `IDBFactory` / `IDBDatabase` / `IDBObjectStore` / `IDBTransaction` / `IDBIndex` interfaces, and the `IDBRequest` event flow all remain the same. The migration is expected to improve reliability (SQLite has decades of production hardening vs. LevelDB's narrower scope) and, to a lesser extent, performance (especially for large object stores). The rollout is multi-phase: step 1 (already shipped) was the in-memory i.e. incognito contexts rollout (chromestatus feature `5126896685809664` parent); step 2 (this cycle) is the *new data stores* rollout — the first time a user visits a site, or after clearing site data, new IDB data will be stored in a backend that uses SQLite; existing data stored in LevelDB is unimpacted; step 3 (future) will consist of migrating existing data from LevelDB stores to SQLite stores. For a controlled fixture serving `<script>const open = indexedDB.open('test-db', 1); open.onupgradeneeded = (e) => { e.target.result.createObjectStore('store', {keyPath: 'id'}); }; open.onsuccess = (e) => { const db = e.target.result; const tx = db.transaction('store', 'readwrite'); tx.objectStore('store').put({id: 1, value: 'test'}).onsuccess = () => { console.log('OK'); }; };</script>` over HTTPS where the page has NO prior storage (cleared site data), assert that the Web API round-trip succeeds identically (controlled fixture verifying the API contract is unchanged — `indexedDB.open` resolves with an `IDBDatabase`, `db.transaction` returns an `IDBTransaction`, the `put()` `IDBRequest` fires `success` event), the new IDB data store is stored in the SQLite backend (controlled fixture verifying via DevTools — the IDB data store at `~/.config/google-chrome/Default/IndexedDB/` shows SQLite file format `.sqlite3` rather than the legacy `.ldb` LevelDB format; this is a DevTools-level assertion requiring Chrome internal access, not a Web API probe), the migration is transparent to the Web API (controlled fixture verifying the same `put` / `get` / `delete` / `cursor` / `index` operations all work identically — no behavior change visible to web pages), the existing LevelDB data is NOT migrated automatically (controlled fixture verifying that a pre-Chrome-154 IDB store with data continues to function — the existing data is unimpacted and will be migrated in step 3), clearing site data (`indexedDB.deleteDatabase('test-db')` + reopen) creates a new SQLite-backed store (controlled fixture verifying the migration applies to new stores only — `deleteDatabase` removes both the LevelDB file and any stub SQLite file, and a subsequent `open()` creates a fresh SQLite-backed store), the migration does NOT affect the `navigator.storage.estimate()` quota reporting (controlled fixture verifying the quota is reported identically — `navigator.storage.estimate()` returns the same `{usage, quota}` values before and after the migration), `Chrome 153` controls (no SQLite backend; uses the legacy LevelDB + flat-files hybrid) demonstrate the pre-migration state (controlled fixture noting that the legacy implementation works identically from a Web API perspective but the storage backend is LevelDB), `Safari 26.6` controls (no SQLite IDB backend migration; Safari uses its own IDB implementation) report no parity changes, `Firefox 155` controls (no SQLite IDB backend migration; Firefox uses its own IDB implementation) report no parity changes. Spec reference: the docs at `https://docs.google.com/document/d/1S9Rx67BmCZIP7tpasKaQ2FlV4TL1Z15DitR2kRZs9nw/edit?usp=sharing` documents the storage differences between LevelDB and SQLite that IDB users should be aware of (e.g. SQLite enforces stricter typing on key-value pairs); the sample at `https://evanstade.github.io/web-storage-demos/idb-txn-scopes` gives a working demo of transaction-scope usage that works identically on both backends. Acceptance gate: the controlled fixture verifies the Web API round-trip + the new-store SQLite backend assertion (via DevTools) + the cross-engine parity assertion in a single Playwright session running on Chrome 154 stable for the live-fire part.
